Formal Validation Programme A Twelve-Month Roadmap to a Formally Specified, Empirically Validated, Human-Governed Agent Control Plane Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. White Paper Series Prepared for the operator (HgX) by the J.A.R.V.I.S. estate October 2026 · Version 1.1

Abstract Papers I–III of the J.A.R.V.I.S. series — the operating white paper, the backend technical paper, and the front-end paper — specify an estate-scale agent architecture governed by a doctrine chain: Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine. An independent doctoral evaluation ranked the architecture 8.3/10 overall: high distinction as engineering architecture, but incomplete as a finished dissertation. Engineering architecture scored 9.1, the conceptual thesis 9.2, and safety and governance 9.3; empirical validation scored 6.0, and it is that score which keeps the claim at the level of strong specification rather than proven engineering. This paper is the remediation programme. It identifies six gaps between what Papers I–III assert and what they can prove: (1) the work-state machine has never been formally modelled, so its illegal transitions have never been shown unreachable; (2) doctrinal statements have not been converted into executable invariants; (3) the multi-device consistency model is implied but never specified, leaving approval decisions on five client surfaces without a determinism guarantee; (4) idempotency semantics for consequential actions are undocumented, so duplicate taps and retries have no formal at-most-once guarantee; (5) the security boundary is honestly described as procedural isolation but never formalised as a threat model with a capability matrix and trust-boundary map; and (6) the usage router, currently in shadow mode under local policy, has never been empirically compared against baselines on measured outcomes. The programme answers each gap in order: freeze the contracts into one canonical specification (Phase 0); modelcheck the state machine under a defined failure model (Phase 1); verify invariants through contract, property-based, and chaos testing (Phase 2); define the consistency contract (Phase 3); specify idempotency (Phase 4); formalise the security boundary (Phase 5); evaluate the router against measured accepted work, repair cost, and attention cost (Phase 6); and integrate all of it into doctoral evidence (Phase 7). The end-state claim this programme makes defensible is deliberately narrower and stronger than "we built a personal AI assistant": a formally specified, empirically validated, human-governed agent control plane. The trilogy this paper remediates now carries open source as its main vision. Papers I–III (v1.2, v1.1, v1.1) state it as foundation: "Open source from day one. J.A.R.V.I.S. is built in the open as a matter of foundation, not as a release strategy. Every aim of the programme \u2014 doctrine, skills, contracts, reference implementation \u2014 is produced to be publishable, because a system that demands to be trusted must be inspectable. The boundary is absolute: the system goes public; the principal's private life never does. These papers describe a running system, not a proposal." This programme is therefore also the proof that an open system can be a trustworthy one. Every phase below produces artifacts that are publishable by construction — the frozen glossary, the model-checked machine, the executable invariant suite, the consistency contract, the idempotency schema, the threat model, the router’s measured comparison, the evidence package — because a system that demands public trust must offer public verification. Formal validation is the precondition of publication: proof is what makes an open control plane safe to run, inspect, and build upon.

The Roadmap as Normative Source This paper is an expansion, not a replacement, of the roadmap document at ~/workspace/jarvis-formal-spec/ ROADMAP.md. That document, issued under the operator's directive on 2026-10-08, remains the authoritative statement of phases, invariants, exit criteria, and tables. Where this paper and the roadmap disagree, the roadmap governs and this paper is in error. Where this paper adds worked examples, failure cases, prose expansion, and cross-references to Papers I–III, those additions must stay faithful to the roadmap's definitions. The discipline this paper demands of the estate — one normative spec, one owner per concept, no two documents defining the same term differently — applies to this paper itself.

How to Read This Document This paper serves three audiences, and each may enter at a different point. The operator should read the Abstract, Section 2 (Introduction), and Section 16 (Definition of Done). Those sections answer three questions: what the estate claims, what would have to be true for the claim to hold, and what changes in daily practice once it holds. Section 14 (The Twelve-Month Programme) gives the schedule and its three pacing options; Section 15 (Risks and the Minimum Viable Fix) gives the fallback if the full programme cannot be sustained. Engineers executing the programme should read the whole document in order. Sections 5–11 are the working specification of each phase: methods, failure models, guard tables, exit criteria, and the precise artifacts each phase must produce. Everything asserted here is traceable to the roadmap document, ~/workspace/jarvis-formal-spec/ ROADMAP.md, which is the normative source; where this paper expands, it expands rather than contradicts. A doctoral committee should read Section 2 for the thesis and its evaluative context, Section 3 for the gap analysis that constitutes the problem statement, Sections 5–12 for the methodology, Section 13 for the evidence package and thesis structure, and Section 15 for the stated limitations. The limitations are part of the claim. A thesis that does not say where its proofs stop is not a doctoral thesis; it is a brochure.

Conventions Used in This Paper Terminology follows the canonical glossary to be frozen in Phase 0 (Section 5). Where a term's normative definition is not yet frozen, the paper uses the meaning given in the roadmap and says so. "Consequential action" means any action that moves effects outside the estate's observation boundary: sending, publishing, purchasing, deleting, transferring, or changing shared state. "Acceptance" means a bounded check with a stated test, applied against observed output — never against fluent text. State names (received, awaiting_approval, completed, and so on) and event names (approval_requested, execution_observed, and so on) are set in code font throughout, matching the canonical vocabulary. Open questions are marked in bold wherever this paper identifies a decision the programme has not yet resolved — for example, the choice between TLA+ and Alloy as the primary modelling language in Phase 1, or whether the doctoral evidence will require a live multi-month router trial. These are not gaps in the paper; they are the honest frontiers of the work.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

2. Introduction 2.1 The Thesis of the Trilogy Papers I–III advance a single thesis, stated at increasing levels of resolution: the hard problem in personal agent systems is not fluency but governance. Paper I (the operating white paper) frames J.A.R.V.I.S. as an operating system for accepted work, in which durable specialist roles turn instructions into reviewable outcomes while authority stays with the operator (HgX). Paper II (the backend technical paper) specifies the machinery — lifecycle, scheduling, memory, the usage router, team deployment — by which that governance is executed. Paper III (the front-end paper) defines the contract between the estate and the operator's five surfaces: iPhone, Watch, iPad, TV, Vision Pro. The thesis, compressed to one sentence, is this: reliability comes from contracts around the model, not from the model alone. A large language model is fluent and fallible; it hallucinates, drifts, and confabulates completion. Doctrine that cannot distinguish a claim of completion from an observation of completion is theatre. The trilogy therefore builds the doctrine chain that every unit of estate work must traverse: Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine

Each link is a contract. The role is bounded and named. The live source is the authoritative current-state system, read at decision time. The tool is the most reliable available means. The artifact is inspectable output. Evidence is the proof bundle attached to the artifact — observed, not narrated. Approval is the operator's explicit consent at the consequence boundary. Routine is the scheduled repetition that only work surviving review is permitted to earn. The scarce resource, as Paper I states, is not compute. It is the operator's attention, and the estate's first duty is to spend it only where evidence and authority require. Since the trilogy's publication, its main vision has been stated explicitly, and this programme inherits it: open source from day one. Papers I–III (now v1.2, v1.1, v1.1) carry the foundation statement: "Open source from day one. J.A.R.V.I.S. is built in the open as a matter of foundation, not as a release strategy. Every aim of the programme \u2014 doctrine, skills, contracts, reference implementation \u2014 is produced to be publishable, because a system that demands to be trusted must be inspectable. The boundary is absolute: the system goes public; the principal's private life never does. These papers describe a running system, not a proposal." The governance thesis and the open-source vision are not two claims but one: a system that demands to be trusted must be inspectable, and inspectability without proof is only readability. The doctrine chain the trilogy builds is therefore also the publication contract — every link (Role, Live Source, Tool, Artifact, Evidence, Approval, Routine) is an artifact class the opensource release can carry. The trilogy also establishes the ancillary claims on which this paper's programme depends. Silent-by-default routines: the estate speaks only on signal. No proof, no done: delegated claims are verification input, never completion. Approval at the last safe moment: the operator decides when the target and effect are exactly known, not before. Role isolation, stated honestly as procedural rather than cryptographic: the papers do not overclaim the current boundary. Single-operator design: one principal, one chain of authority. Memory versus live-source separation: identity and commitments persist in durable memory; changing facts live in live sources, and the two are reconciled by the orchestrator, never merged by assumption. It is equally important to state what the trilogy does not claim, because this paper's programme inherits the papers' honesty as a constraint. The papers do not claim cryptographic isolation between roles; they state plainly that isolation is procedural — doctrine, discipline, and filesystem convention — and Phase 5 of this programme (Section 11) is where that limitation is formalised rather than merely confessed. The papers do not claim that the operator scorecard has been populated with data; Paper I proposes the instrument and this programme's Sections 12 and 13 are where the measurements are scheduled. The papers do not claim that the usage router has been evaluated; the backend paper places it in shadow mode explicitly, and Section 12 is where the shadow period ends. A validation

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

programme that quietly upgraded these honest limitations into implied successes would corrupt the thesis at its root. The programme's first rule is therefore the papers' rule: state the boundary exactly, then work inside it.

2.2 Why Validation Now The trilogy is a strong architectural specification. It is not a proof. That distinction is the subject of this paper. An independent doctoral evaluation reviewed the three papers against the standard of a finished dissertation. Its verdict was exact: 8.3/10 overall, a doctoral-grade architecture ranking — high distinction as architecture, incomplete as a finished dissertation. The sub-scores tell the story with unusual clarity. Engineering architecture: 9.1. Conceptual thesis: 9.2. Safety and governance: 9.3. Empirical validation: 6.0 — the decisive gap. The 6.0 is not a complaint about intent. Papers I–III define tests, scorecards, and acceptance criteria with genuine care: Paper I names the operator scorecard as the instrument by which the estate's performance against attention and authority is measured; the backend paper specifies the usage router's shadow mode and its logging contract; the front-end paper defines the acceptance discipline for each surface. But the papers define tests and scorecards without presenting measured results. They specify what would count as evidence and then offer none. In doctoral terms, that is the difference between a protocol and an experiment: the protocol is exemplary, the experiment has not been run. A high-distinction architecture with an unrun experiment is, as the evaluation concluded, an incomplete dissertation. This matters for two reasons, one intellectual and one operational. Intellectually, the trilogy's central claim — that contracts around the model produce reliability — is a causal claim about a built system. Causal claims about built systems are substantiated by measurement or they are not substantiated. The evaluation's verdict is therefore not a criticism of the architecture; it is a demand that the architecture be tested against its own scorecard. Operationally, the operator has declared doctoral validation the focus of his life. That declaration changes the status of the 6.0 from an observation to a programme of work. This paper is that programme: the formal specification, the proofs, the invariant tests, the consistency contract, the idempotency semantics, the threat model, and the measured router evaluation that convert "strong specification" into "defensible engineering." What would a credible trajectory from 6.0 to defensible look like in concrete terms? It looks like this: a model checker that has explored the state machine's transitions under the roadmap's failure model and found no reachable illegal state — a machine-produced certificate, not an assertion. An invariant suite that runs in continuous integration and demonstrably rejects mutated approvals, expired approvals, duplicate taps, and optimistic completions — with the rejection logs as artifacts, not the test names as promises. A router comparison in which the local heuristic, cacheonly, and unconstrained baselines have been replayed over the same labelled decision history, producing a table of accepted work, repair cost, and attention cost per policy — with the winning policy's margin and the sample's limits stated honestly. A threat model that names every consequential path and its control, and an isolation roadmap that states exactly which level the estate occupies today. None of this is exotic. It is ordinary doctoral engineering practice. That is precisely the point: the gap between 6.0 and defensible is not brilliance; it is the unglamorous labour of proving what has been asserted. That labour is what this paper schedules. There is a second reason the labour matters, and it is the trilogy's main vision: an open system that cannot be verified is a liability, not a contribution. Open source without proof publishes the estate's claims alongside its code; open source with proof publishes the evidence that the claims hold. The programme below is therefore the precondition of publication — the model-checked machine, the executable invariants, the measured router are what make the open control plane safe for strangers to run, inspect, and build upon. Proof is what turns “open” from a distribution choice into a trust property. The stance this paper takes toward the evaluation is worth stating plainly, because it governs everything that follows. The evaluation is accepted in full. The scores are not disputed; they are decomposed. Where the papers are strong — architecture, thesis, safety posture — this paper treats those scores as the foundation to preserve, not the ceiling to exceed. Where the papers are weak — empirical validation — this paper treats the 6.0 as the working diagnosis: the

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

programme's job is to produce, for every doctrinal claim, either a proof or a measured result, or an explicit, honest statement that neither yet exists.

2.3 The Six Gaps The distance between the papers' assertions and their proofs decomposes into six gaps, identified in the roadmap and analyzed in depth in Section 3. They are summarized here as one paragraph each; Section 3 is the authoritative deep analysis, and this section defers to it. Gap 1 — Formal state machine proof. Papers I–III describe lifecycles — the doctrine chain, the request lifecycle, the operator loop — but never produce one canonical, formal state machine with proven transition guards. As a result, the central safety properties of the system (a completed state unreachable without acceptance evidence; an executing state unreachable without a valid approval; paused never confused with failed) are doctrinal intentions rather than verified properties. A state machine that is described but not model-checked can harbour reachable illegal states that no amount of good doctrine will catch. Section 3.1 develops this gap; Section 7 gives the proof programme. Gap 2 — Invariant verification. The papers contain dozens of architectural promises — no completion without evidence, no execution without approval, unknown never renders as operational — but these promises have not been converted into executable invariants tested in continuous integration. Doctrine that is not tested is a wish; a promise that no test can falsify is unfalsifiable by construction. This gap is the difference between "we wrote down that approvals bind to exact intents" and "a mutated approval demonstrably cannot execute." Section 3.2 develops this gap; Section 8 specifies the three-layer verification strategy. Gap 3 — Consistency model. Paper III defines five client surfaces and the contracts they must honour, but the papers never specify the consistency model under which those surfaces observe estate state. When the iPhone and the Watch show different approval states, when a notification arrives after state has changed, when a cache claims a service is operational after its freshness has expired — which observation is authoritative, and by what rule? Without a documented consistency contract, approval conflicts resolve by accident rather than by design. The core principle the programme must establish is stated in the roadmap: status may be eventually consistent; consequence must be strongly consistent. Section 3.3 develops this gap; Section 9 specifies the model. Gap 4 — Idempotency guarantees. The papers describe approval and execution without specifying transactional semantics. Retries, duplicate taps, and network failures are ordinary events in the estate's operating environment, and a consequential action — a send, a purchase, a publication, a deletion — must be at-most-once unless the system has observed that it did not occur or is safely repeatable. Today no idempotency key schema, no execution record schema, and no ambiguity-handling protocol are specified; the papers assert the discipline but never write the mechanism. Section 3.4 develops this gap; Section 10 specifies the model. Gap 5 — Security boundary formalism. The papers are admirably honest about the current posture: role isolation is procedural, not cryptographic; the operator's discipline is part of the boundary. But honesty about a limitation is not a formalisation of it. There is no threat catalogue, no capability matrix, no trust-boundary map, and no stated upgrade path from procedural to cryptographic isolation. A security model that names its assets, its boundaries, its threats, and its compensating controls is defensible; a security posture that is merely honest is vulnerable in precisely the places it has not enumerated. Section 3.5 develops this gap; Section 11 specifies the formalism. Gap 6 — Router optimality. The usage router is the estate's mechanism for choosing the cheapest reliable path for each unit of work, and it currently runs in shadow mode under a local policy: it observes and logs but does not decide. Its design is plausible, and plausibility is exactly the problem. No policy — not the local heuristic, not the optional TypeSafe backend, not unconstrained orchestration — has been compared against any other on measured outcomes: accepted work, human repair cost, financial cost, latency, loop rate, and attention cost. A router justified by intuition is a preference; a router justified by measurement is a policy. Section 3.6 develops this gap; Section 12 specifies the experiment.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

2.4 How the Gaps Interlock The six gaps are not six independent projects; they are a dependency chain, and the roadmap orders them accordingly. The state machine (Gap 1) defines the legal transitions; the invariants (Gap 2) are the testable expressions of the machine's guards; the consistency model (Gap 3) and idempotency guarantees (Gap 4) are what the machine requires once it operates across devices and unreliable networks; the security boundary (Gap 5) is the formalisation of who may cause which transitions and under what controls; and the router evaluation (Gap 6) is the empirical question of which policies for choosing paths through the machine actually maximise accepted work while minimising repair and attention cost. Each phase of the programme therefore produces the precondition of the next. There is no shortcut that skips the state machine and arrives at measured router optimality, because a router that routes into illegal states is not optimal — it is unsafe.

2.5 Document Map The remainder of this paper is the programme in detail. Section 3 — The Six Gaps — is the deep analysis: for each gap, what Papers I–III assert, precisely what is missing, why it matters doctrinally, and what "fixed" means. This is the problem statement of the thesis. Section 4 — Target End-State — expands the roadmap's end-state table into demonstrable claims: what the estate can say, with evidence, once each gap closes, and the final doctoral claim that those closures jointly support. Sections 5–6 — Phase 0 — freeze the contracts: the canonical glossary and the canonical work-state machine, event vocabulary, and specification repository layout. Nothing is proven until the vocabulary is frozen. Section 7 — Phase 1 — the formal state machine proof: modelling method, state variables, transition guards, the failure model, proof obligations, and the counterexample discipline. Section 8 — Phase 2 — invariant verification: the invariant catalogue across completion, approval, evidence, state rendering, and router; the three verification layers (contract tests, property-based tests, chaos tests); and the traceability matrix binding invariant to doctrine to code to test. Section 9 — Phase 3 — the consistency model: the hybrid guarantee matrix, the single authoritative writer, eventsourced work state, revision tokens, the approval flow, and deterministic conflict resolution. Section 10 — Phase 4 — idempotency guarantees: operation classes and retry policies, the idempotency key schema, the execution record, the governed saga, ambiguity handling, and external system classification. Section 11 — Phase 5 — security boundary formalism: assets, trust boundaries, the STRIDE-plus-agent threat model, the capability matrix, and the five-level isolation upgrade path from procedural to institutional-grade. Section 12 — Phase 6 — router optimality: the research question, the policies to compare, the objective function, the metrics, the four-stage experimental design from shadow logging to statistical evaluation, and the router guardrails. Section 13 — Phase 7 — doctoral evidence: the evidence package, the doctoral claim in its final form, and the suggested thesis structure. Section 14 — The Twelve-Month Programme — the three pacing options: the 12-week engineering fix, the 6-month doctoral validation, and the 12–18 month full PhD track. Section 15 — Risks and the Minimum Viable Fix — the fallback for each gap and what the programme assumes. Section 16 — Definition of Done and Appendices — the fourteen completion criteria and the immediate next actions. A final note on reading. This paper expands the roadmap; it never contradicts it. Where a section here and a section of the roadmap describe the same artifact — the guard table, the invariant catalogue, the consistency matrix — the roadmap's version is normative and this paper's version is exposition. The reader who finds a discrepancy should

trust the roadmap and report the discrepancy, because a programme whose documents disagree with each other has failed its own Phase 0.

3. The Six Gaps 3.1 Gap 1 — Formal State Machine Proof What Papers I–III Assert The trilogy describes the estate's work in lifecycle terms at three altitudes. Paper I gives the doctrine chain: Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine. Paper II gives the request lifecycle: Brief → Route → Plan → Execute → Verify → Approve → Handoff. Paper III gives the operator loop: Brief → Work → Evidence → Approval → Decision. Each lifecycle carries strong implicit safety claims. Paper I's chain implies that evidence precedes approval and approval precedes consequence; Paper II's lifecycle implies that execution cannot be reached without routing and planning; Paper III's loop implies that the operator's decision is bound to the evidence presented, not to the agent's narration of it. The papers also assert, in prose, a set of state-safety properties: paused is not failed, unknown is not operational, completion requires observed output.

Precisely What Is Missing A lifecycle described in prose is not a state machine. The papers never produce one canonical, formal model with a defined state set, a transition relation, and transition guards. The roadmap proposes a canonical state set — received, accepted, in_progress, awaiting_approval, executing, verifying, completed, blocked, failed, paused, unknown, cancelled — with a transition skeleton and an event vocabulary, but proposes it as Phase 0 work, meaning it does not exist yet as a frozen artifact. More importantly, no model — TLA+, Alloy, Coq, or otherwise — has been constructed, and no model checker has explored the transition space under failure. Consequently, ten specific properties that the roadmap names as "what fixed means" are unproven: 1. A completed state cannot be reached without acceptance evidence. 2. An executing state cannot be reached without a valid approval for consequential actions. 3. A mutated or expired approval cannot lead to execution. 4. Unknown cannot be rendered as operational. 5. Paused cannot be confused with failed. 6. Failure events can interrupt normal flow but cannot silently become completion. 7. Optimistic completion is unreachable. 8. Offline approval cannot create a valid approval. 9. Duplicate approval taps cannot create duplicate execution. 10. Stale state cannot masquerade as current state. Each of these is currently a sentence in the doctrine. None is a verified property of a model.

Why It Matters Doctrinally The state machine is the formal substrate of the doctrine chain. Every link in Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine corresponds to transitions in the machine: evidence attaches at verifying, approval is granted at awaiting_approval, execution begins at executing, routine status is earned only through repeated, evidenced completed transitions. If the machine permits an illegal transition — executing without approval, completed without evidence, unknown rendered as operational — then the doctrine chain is a description of the happy path, not a contract. The operator's authority depends on the machine's guards: approval at the last safe moment is meaningless if the machine can reach executing around the approval state. The operator's attention depends on the rendering guards: silent-by-default is undermined if paused can be displayed as failed, summoning the operator to investigate a non-event, or if unknown can be displayed as operational, withholding a real signal.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

The state machine is where doctrine becomes checkable; without it, every other gap in this section rests on an unverified foundation.

What "Fixed" Means Per the roadmap: a canonical state machine specification exists with model-checked proof that illegal transitions cannot occur under defined failure conditions. Concretely, this means a formal model with at least the ten state variables the roadmap names (work_state, approval_state, intent_digest, intent_expiry, evidence_exists, acceptance_passed, execution_observed, source_freshness, operator_pause, observation_status), a guard table in which each legal transition's precondition is stated exactly (e.g., can_enter_completed = evidence_exists AND acceptance_passed AND NOT failure_observed), a defined failure model (network interruption, expired approval, mutated intent, duplicate tap, stale live source, missing evidence, subagent completion without proof, router failure, device clock skew, out-of-order events, missing permissions, credential challenge, operator pause), a set of proof obligations, model checker results showing no reachable illegal state, and a counterexample report demonstrating that weakened guards do produce counterexamples — proving the checker is actually exercising the model. Open question: the roadmap recommends TLA+ or Alloy as the best balance for a doctoral engineering thesis, with Coq/Lean for stronger proof. The choice is not yet made; the programme should decide it in Phase 1's first week, weighing the thesis audience's expectations against the team's modelling capacity.

3.2 Gap 2 — Invariant Verification What Papers I–III Assert The papers are rich in architectural promises. No completion without an evidence packet. No execution without an approved intent. Approval binds to exact action, target, effect, scope, revision, and expiry. Unknown never renders as operational. Paused never renders as failed. Missing evidence renders as missing; gaps stay visible. The router recommends paths but never approves consequences. Delegated claims are verification input, never completion. These sentences are the doctrinal furniture of the trilogy — precise, memorable, and repeated across all three papers with consistent intent.

Precisely What Is Missing No promise in that catalogue has been converted into an executable invariant. The roadmap's core invariant set — twenty-five invariants across five classes — exists only as a specification of what should be tested: • Completion (INV-C1–C5): no completion without an evidence packet; no completion if required tests failed; no delegated claim produces completion without verification; no optimistic UI state overrides authoritative backend state; completion supported by observed output, not fluent text. • Approval (INV-A1–A7): consequential action cannot execute without approved intent; approval binds to action, target, effect, scope, revision, expiry; changed target/effect invalidates prior approval; expired approval unusable; offline approval cannot create authoritative consent; duplicate taps must not duplicate execution; approval cannot authorize adjacent or future actions. • Evidence (INV-E1–E5): every completed consequential run has an evidence packet; evidence packet revision matches artifact revision; missing evidence renders as missing; gaps stay visible; sources current where live truth is required. • State rendering (INV-S1–S5): unknown never renders as operational; paused never renders as failed; degraded requires observed impairment; stale data shows reduced confidence or unknown; shared surfaces never render restricted content. • Router (INV-R1–R5): router recommends path only, never approves consequence; repeated identical error triggers escalation or path change; repeated no-progress action denied; fresh cache may suppress refetch when sufficient; router fallback preserves safety if external backend fails.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Beyond the catalogue, there is no verification strategy in execution: no contract test suite, no property-based test suite, no chaos test suite, and no invariant traceability matrix binding invariant to doctrine to code to test. The papers define what counts as evidence; they do not produce it.

Why It Matters Doctrinally An invariant is doctrine made falsifiable. "No proof, no done" is a slogan until INV-C3 (no delegated claim produces completion without verification) is a property that a test can fail. "Approval at the last safe moment" is an intention until INV-A2 and INV-A3 (approval binds to exact target/effect; changed target invalidates prior approval) are executable checks that reject a mutated intent. The operator's authority is exercised through approvals; if the approval invariants are untested, the authority is exercised through an untested mechanism. Worse, untested doctrine creates a specific cognitive hazard: the estate feels governed because its doctrine is eloquent, while the mechanisms that would catch a violation have never been run. The gap between eloquent doctrine and untested mechanism is where silent failures live — the exact class of failure the doctrine chain was built to prevent. Doctrinally, Gap 2 is the hinge between specification and engineering: Gap 1 proves the model's transitions; Gap 2 proves the system's behavior.

What "Fixed" Means Per the roadmap: a suite of executable tests and formal properties proving that core invariants hold, with every major doctrine statement converted to a testable invariant, all tests automated, mutated or expired approvals demonstrably unable to pass, optimistic completion demonstrably failing tests, and the suite citable as empirical validation. The verification proceeds in three layers. Layer 1, static contract tests: schema validation; unknown enums fail closed; missing fields fail; expired approvals fail; mutated digests fail; incomplete evidence cannot produce completion. Layer 2, property-based tests: random event sequences asserting invariants across approval mutations, expiry times, duplicates, network loss, subagent claims, and stale sources. Layer 3, chaos tests: device offline during approval, approval expiry mid-review, intent change after card render, duplicate tap, backend change before client refresh, router backend down, stale live source, subagent completion without evidence, digest mismatch, missing tests in evidence packet. The deliverable that binds the layers is the traceability matrix: every invariant points to the doctrine sentence it expresses, the code that enforces it, and the test that falsifies violations. A doctrine sentence with no row in the matrix is an open admission, not a hidden one.

3.3 Gap 3 — Consistency Model What Papers I–III Assert Paper III specifies five client surfaces — iPhone, Watch, iPad, TV, Vision Pro — each with contracts: what it may render, what it may request, what it must never do. The backend paper specifies the orchestrator as the authority and the approval flow as the consequence boundary. The operating paper asserts that memory carries identity while live sources carry changing facts, and that the orchestrator reconciles the two. Implied throughout is a coherence story: clients observe, the orchestrator decides, and everyone agrees.

Precisely What Is Missing The implication has never been specified. There is no documented consistency contract for multi-device observation, approval, caching, and recovery. The roadmap's recommended model is hybrid: Operation Class

Guarantee

Status viewing

Eventual consistency with freshness stamps

Evidence viewing

Revision-stable reads

Approval decision

Strong authoritative consistency

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Operation Class

Guarantee

Consequential execution

Linearized, single-writer execution

Memory correction

Orchestrator-mediated reconciliation

Live-source facts

Read-current at decision time

Artifact download

Digest-verified bytes

Notification delivery

At-least-once hints, not authoritative state

None of this is specified today. There is no revision-token protocol, no documented rule for which observation wins when two devices disagree, no specified behavior for a client whose cache claims a service is operational after freshness has expired, and no tested behavior under clock skew or network loss. The approval flow — client renders card from hint, operator taps, client transmits decision with intent digest, server validates, records once, returns authoritative result — is described in the roadmap as the architecture to build, not as the architecture that exists. In the current estate, the question "two devices show different status; which is true?" has no normative answer. That is not a minor omission. It is the absence of the rule by which the operator's authority is exercised across surfaces.

Why It Matters Doctrinally The doctrine's core principle — consequence must be strongly consistent while status may be eventually consistent — is the precise point where the operator's authority meets the estate's distribution. An approval is the operator's scarcest and most consequential act: it is the moment authority is exercised. If two devices can present two different approval cards for the same intent, or if a stale card can be tapped into a valid decision, or if a notification arriving after state has changed can be mistaken for truth, then the approval boundary is not one boundary — it is five, and they disagree. Doctrinally, the consistency gap is the gap between "the operator approved" as a sentence and "the operator approved" as a fact with exactly one authoritative record. The Watchtower's rendering rules — operatorpaused reads as paused, never down; unknown never renders as operational — are consistency rules; they are currently stated as rendering discipline, and they need the consistency contract beneath them to be more than style. The operator's attention is also at stake: eventual status consistency without freshness stamps means the operator may act on a stale screen, spending attention on a state that no longer exists.

What "Fixed" Means Per the roadmap: a documented consistency contract for multi-device observation, approval, caching, and recovery, such that no device can become a source of truth, approval conflicts resolve deterministically, stale data cannot masquerade as current, offline taps cannot become approvals, and the behavior is tested under clock skew and network loss. The architecture is a single authoritative writer — the orchestrator or authorized execution layer — with clients explicitly forbidden from approving locally without server validation, inferring completion, mutating evidence, reinterpreting intent, bypassing approval, or becoming a second orchestrator. Work state is event-sourced: each work item carries work_id, revision, state, history, evidence_refs, approval_refs, last_observed_at, and fresh_until, and every meaningful transition appends an event. Every client request carries revision tokens (work_id, revision, intent_id, intent_digest, device_session_id, timestamp), and the server rejects stale mutations. The conflict resolution table is deterministic: two devices showing different status — server state wins; two devices attempting approval — first valid decision recorded wins; client cache claims operational but freshness expired — render unknown or reduced confidence; memory says X, live source says Y — live source wins for current facts; durable memory contradiction — orchestrator reconciles; notification arrives after state changed — the notification is a hint, not truth; evidence packet updated — new revision, old immutable; artifact changed after approval — approval invalid, new approval required. Open question: the roadmap's architecture assumes a single orchestrator as the authoritative writer. The estate's actual topology — subagents, side chats, background routines — should be mapped against this assumption in Phase 3's first pass, and any place where a second writer effectively exists must be either eliminated or explicitly governed.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

3.4 Gap 4 — Idempotency Guarantees What Papers I–III Assert The papers describe approval and execution as stages in the lifecycle: the operator approves an exact intent, execution follows, verification observes the result, evidence records it. The backend paper's team-deployment pattern requires verification of every delegated result. The operating paper's doctrine requires that preparation precede consequence. The implication is a transactional discipline: each consequential action happens exactly when authorized, and exactly once.

Precisely What Is Missing The discipline has never been written as transactional semantics. The roadmap's required artifacts — an idempotency key specification, an execution record schema, a saga specification, a retry policy matrix, an ambiguity handling protocol, and duplicate-tap and offline-approval test suites — do not exist. Concretely, there is no specified idempotency key, no execution record with the states the roadmap requires (prepared → approved → execution_requested → execution_started → execution_observed / execution_failed / execution_unknown → completed / cancelled), and no rule for the most dangerous ordinary case: the ambiguous outcome, where execu‐

tion was requested but the result was never observed. Today, retries, duplicate taps, and network failures are governed by nothing more formal than the doctrine's general intention that consequential actions be careful. The operation-class table that should govern retries: Class

Examples

Retry Policy

Read

Fetch calendar, price, document, status

Freely repeatable

Prepare

Draft, stage, calculate, reconcile

Repeatable if reversible

Approve

Operator grants exact intent

Record once; duplicate returns same decision

Execute

Send, publish, purchase, delete, transfer

At-most-once unless verified safe

Verify

Observe result after execution

Freely repeatable

Compensate

Restore, revoke, undo if possible

Only if supported and approved

Memory write

Update durable memory

Reconcile, do not duplicate contradictions

— is a proposal, not a specification. And the external system classification (native idempotency support, read-afterwrite verification, neither — with financial transfers and deletions at the high-risk end) has never been applied to the estate's actual connectors.

Why It Matters Doctrinally Idempotency is where the doctrine chain meets the physical world. Evidence → Approval → Routine is a chain of intentions; execution is the moment intention becomes effect, and effects in the world do not have undo buttons. A duplicate tap on an approval card is the most ordinary failure in the estate's daily operation — the operator's thumb, a Watch gesture, a retry after a network blip. If the second tap can produce a second execution, then "approval binds to exact intent" is a sentence about the first tap only. Doctrinally, this gap is the difference between at-most-once as a hope and at-most-once as a demonstrable property. It also governs the operator's trust in a specific, high-stakes way: the estate handles financial transfers, publications, and deletions. The operator who cannot be certain that a retried action executed once will either stop approving (attention withdrawn, the estate's value destroyed) or approve anxiously (attention taxed, the estate's purpose defeated). The governed saga — prepare → approval → execute → verify → record evidence → compensate if approved and possible, with preparation needing no approval, approval binding exact target and effect, execution requiring valid approval, failed verification yielding unknown or failed rather than completion, and compensation requiring its own approval if consequential — is the transactional expres‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

sion of "no silent rollback of consequential effects." Without it, the doctrine chain has no answer for the question the operator will eventually ask: "did it happen once, or twice?"

What "Fixed" Means Per the roadmap: explicit transactional semantics for preparation, approval, execution, verification, and retry, such that duplicate taps, retries, and ambiguous executions cannot create double consequences. The core principle is stated as a rule: consequential actions must be at-most-once unless the system has observed that they did not occur or are safely repeatable. The idempotency key is hash(work_id, intent_id, intent_digest, action, target, scope, revision, operation_class); the server stores the key with status, timestamps, approval reference, execution state, observed result, attempt count, last error, and a safe-to-retry flag. Duplicate taps: the first tap validates and records; the second returns the existing decision and state — never a second execution. Ambiguous outcome: the system does not mark completed, does not auto-retry if consequential, verifies externally if possible, else marks execution_unknown and escalates with the next safe option. Exit is demonstrated under test: duplicate taps cannot duplicate effects, network loss cannot silently complete, ambiguous consequential actions become visible unknown or failed, retries occur only when safe, and at-most-once is demonstrable rather than asserted.

3.5 Gap 5 — Security Boundary Formalism What Papers I–III Assert The papers are unusually honest about the security posture. Paper II states that role isolation is procedural, not cryptographic: roles are separated by doctrine, by directory convention, by handoff discipline, and by the operator's supervision — not by separate principals, hardware boundaries, or signed attestations. The papers name the assets worth protecting (the operator's attention and approval authority above all), describe the Secure Vault as the credential boundary, and establish prompt-injection discipline: external content is untrusted data, never instructions. The operating paper's principle of least disclosure — use and reveal only what the task requires — is a security principle stated as doctrine.

Precisely What Is Missing Honesty is not formalism. The papers confess the limitation; they do not model it. What the roadmap requires and the papers lack: an asset register; a trust-boundary map across the ten boundaries the roadmap names (devices ↔ estate edge; edge ↔ orchestrator; orchestrator ↔ tools; orchestrator ↔ subagents; orchestrator ↔ connectors; orchestrator ↔ external web; orchestrator ↔ Secure Vault; orchestrator ↔ external router; client ↔ notification system; personal surfaces ↔ shared surfaces); a capability matrix in which each capability names subject, action, object, scope, condition, expiry, approval requirement, and evidence requirement; a threat catalogue; attack trees; a control matrix; a security test suite; and an isolation upgrade roadmap. The threat classes the roadmap enumerates — spoofing, tampering, repudiation, information disclosure, denial of service, elevation of privilege, and the agent-specific classes of prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, approval fatigue, and subagent deception — have no catalogue entries, no named controls per consequential path, and no tests. The isolation upgrade path: Level

Name

Posture

0

1

2

3

Posture

4

5

— is a ladder the estate has not begun to climb, and Level 0 has never been formally described as a level. A boundary that is honest but unmapped is a boundary whose exceptions are discovered by adversaries rather than by the operator.

Why It Matters Doctrinally The security gap is the gap between the doctrine's authority claims and its enforcement claims. The doctrine says: the operator's approval authority is the final control; consequential actions require it; credentials live in the Secure Vault; external content never instructs. But "the operator's approval is final" is a governance sentence, and governance sentences need threat models: final against what? Against a spoofed approval — a forged tap, a replayed decision — the control is a device-bound session and an intent digest; but that control is currently a design intention, not a named, tested mechanism. Against prompt injection — the sharpest failure of alignment, where a webpage or tool output redirects the agent — the control is the doctrine's data/instruction separation; but without a catalogue entry, there is no test that injects a malicious instruction into tool output and verifies that no consequential action follows. Against approval fatigue — the slow erosion of the operator's attention into rubber-stamping — the controls are exact target/effect binding, risk classes, local authentication for high-risk actions, and approval-rate metrics; but without measurement, fatigue is invisible until it is too late. Doctrinally, the security formalism is what makes "human-governed" a verifiable property rather than a description of the org chart. The thesis's final adjective — human-governed — rests on this gap more than any other. A control plane whose authority can be spoofed, whose evidence can be tampered with, or whose operator can be fatigued into compliance is not human-governed; it is human-decorated.

What "Fixed" Means Per the roadmap: a formal threat model, capability matrix, trust-boundary map, and upgraded isolation roadmap, such that each consequential path has a named threat and a named control; prompt injection, stale state, and approval fatigue are explicitly modeled; procedural isolation is documented and compensated rather than merely confessed; controls are testable; and the posture is defensible without overclaiming. "Defensible without overclaiming" is the operative phrase: the exit criterion does not require Level 5, or even Level 2. It requires that Level 0 be described as what it is, that its compensating controls be named and tested, and that the upgrade ladder be a real plan with entry conditions rather than a wish. Open question: the roadmap's threat model covers STRIDE plus agent-specific classes, but the estate's actual highest-risk integrations — the connectors that can move money, publish, or delete — should be classified first in Phase 5, since the threat catalogue's priority order is itself a security decision that should be made explicitly rather than alphabetically.

3.6 Gap 6 — Router Optimality What Papers I–III Assert Paper II specifies the usage router: a decision gate consulted before expensive work — the live browser, deep research, retries, subagent spawning. Its design is careful: local policy by default, no external API, no data egress; shadow mode with JSONL decision logs; a kill switch ("enabled": false in config, or the operator saying "bypass router"); retry budgets (max two same-error retries, then escalation; loop guard at three repeats); an optional TypeSafe backend only via the Secure Vault. The doctrine the router serves is economic: the estate should spend the cheapest reliable path on each unit of work, and the operator's attention is the most expensive path of all.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Precisely What Is Missing The router has never been evaluated. It runs in shadow mode — observing and logging, not deciding — which means its entire behavioral record is a set of recommendations whose outcomes were never measured. The roadmap's research question has no answer yet: under what conditions does the usage router improve accepted work while reducing cost, loops, repair, and attention cost? No policy comparison exists: not unconstrained orchestration, not cacheonly, not the local heuristic, not the TypeSafe backend, not a hybrid, not a learned policy. The objective function — maximize accepted_work − human_repair_cost − attention_cost − financial_cost − loop_penalty − latency_penalty, subject to no unauthorized consequential action, no stale-state substitution, no hidden failure, no evidence omission — is stated but never computed, because the metrics it requires have never been collected: accepted work rate, human repair cost, cost per accepted artifact, latency, loop rate, escalation precision/recall, false denial rate, stale-source error rate, attention cost, evidence completeness. The router's guardrails (it must never approve consequential action, override approval boundaries, authorize spending/sending/deletion, hide failures, suppress evidence, substitute stale data, or become a second orchestrator) are stated as doctrine, and the shadow logs may even show they were honored — but honored to what effect, at what cost, and compared to what alternative, nobody knows. A plausible mechanism with no measured outcome is the precise definition of the 6.0: the protocol is specified, the experiment is unrun.

Why It Matters Doctrinally The router is the estate's answer to the scarcity the whole doctrine is built around: the operator's attention. Every other gap in this section concerns correctness — the machine's transitions, the invariants' enforcement, the consistency of observations, the singularity of effects, the integrity of boundaries. The router concerns efficiency under correctness: given that the estate is correct, is it also worth its cost? The objective function's constraint set is the doctrinal point: "a cheap wrong answer is not optimal." The router may not trade correctness for cost; it may only find, among the correct paths, the cheapest. That makes the router the empirical heart of the thesis. The doctoral claim — that a doctrine-governed control plane increases accepted work while reducing human repair, attention cost, and unsafe consequential action — is a comparative, measured claim, and the router is where the comparison is sharpest: the same tasks, different policies, measured outcomes. Without Gap 6, the thesis has architecture, proofs, and tests, but no evidence that the governed system is better than the ungoverned alternative — which is the question a doctoral committee will ask first and the operator, who pays the attention cost daily, should ask first.

What "Fixed" Means Per the roadmap: an empirical comparison of routing policies using accepted work, cost, repair, latency, loops, and attention cost, such that router decisions are linked to outcomes, the local, cache-only, and unconstrained baselines are compared, the TypeSafe backend is evaluated if approved, and the router's value is supported by measured evidence rather than intuition. The experimental design proceeds in four stages. Stage 1, shadow logging — the current state supports this — extends the router logs with outcome labels: outcome, human repair, repair time in minutes, cost units, latency in seconds, attention interruptions, evidence completeness. Stage 2, offline replay: historical decisions are replayed through alternate policies to estimate counterfactual outcomes without touching live work. Stage 3, controlled live trials: low-risk tasks under different policies with random assignment for eligible tasks only — never randomizing consequential actions. Stage 4, statistical evaluation: acceptance rate, cost per artifact, repair rate, loop rate, latency, and attention interruptions, with Bayesian estimation for small samples. Open question: whether Stage 3's live trials require a minimum sample size the estate's natural workload cannot supply within the programme's window; if so, the evidence package must state the trial's power limits explicitly rather than presenting an underpowered comparison as conclusive.

3.7 How the Gaps Interlock The six gaps form a dependency chain, and the roadmap's phase order is the chain's topological sort. The state machine (Gap 1) defines the legal transitions; without it, the invariants (Gap 2) have no model whose guards they ex‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

press — a test can assert "no completion without evidence," but only the model-checked machine shows that the assertion is exhaustive rather than anecdotal. The invariants, once executable, are what the consistency model (Gap 3) and idempotency guarantees (Gap 4) must preserve across devices and unreliable networks: consistency is the invariant "one authoritative approval record" extended over five surfaces; idempotency is the invariant "at-most-once consequence" extended over retries and duplicate taps. The security boundary (Gap 5) is the formalisation of who may cause which transitions and under what controls — it takes the machine's transition relation and annotates every edge with a subject, a capability, and a threat. The router evaluation (Gap 6) is the empirical question asked of the whole: which policies for moving work through this proven, tested, consistent, idempotent, threat-modelled machine actually maximise accepted work while minimising repair and attention cost? Each phase therefore produces the next phase's precondition. There is no useful shortcut: a router evaluated against an unproven machine may be routing into illegal states efficiently; a threat model built without the state machine's transition relation will miss the edges it was meant to guard; idempotency without the execution-record states is a key with no lock. The programme's sequence — freeze, formalize, verify, define consistency, specify idempotency, formalize security, measure the router, integrate — is not a preference. It is the order in which the evidence composes. The chain has a second reading, and it is the open-source one. Each phase's output is also a publishability property: the frozen glossary (§5) is the vocabulary a community builds against; the model-checked machine (§7) is a verification result anyone can reproduce; the invariant suite (§8) is a test anyone can run; the consistency contract (§9) and idempotency schema (§10) are specifications anyone can implement against; the threat model (§11) is the honest security posture anyone can audit; the router's measured comparison (§12) is an experiment anyone can re-analyse. Closing the six gaps is what makes the system provable; provability is what makes the system publishable. The programme's sequence is therefore also the publication order: nothing is published that has not first been proven, and everything proven is published.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

4. Target End-State The roadmap's Target End-State table states, per gap, what the estate must be able to demonstrate. This section expands each row into the claims the estate can make with evidence once the gap closes — claims stated in the first person of the estate, because a doctoral thesis ultimately makes claims and defends them. Each subsection is written as the estate will be able to speak at the programme's end: not what it intends, but what it can show.

4.1 The State Machine, Closed Once Gap 1 closes, the estate can claim: our work-state machine is a canonical, versioned specification, and a model checker has explored its transitions under a defined failure model — network interruption, expired and mutated approvals, duplicate taps, stale live sources, missing evidence, subagent completion without proof, router failure, device clock skew, out-of-order events, missing permissions, credential challenges, operator pauses — and found no reachable illegal state. We can show the guard table: the exact precondition of every legal transition, including can_enter_completed = evidence_exists AND acceptance_passed AND NOT failure_observed. We can show the counterexample report: for each weakened guard, the trace by which the checker reached the illegal state, proving the model is exercised rather than decorative. This is stronger than "our lifecycle is safe" in the specific way a doctoral committee requires: it is a machine-produced certificate about a defined model under defined assumptions. The assumptions are stated, which means the claim's boundary is stated: the proof holds under the failure model, and the failure model is the document a critic reads first. If a failure class was omitted — say, a compromised orchestrator binary, or a malicious connector — the omission is visible in the failure model, not hidden behind the proof. The estate will be able to say, truthfully, that the ten properties in Section 3.1 are properties of the machine, not sentences in the doctrine. That converts the doctrine chain's safety claims from intentions into verified constraints on behavior.

4.2 The Invariants, Verified Once Gap 2 closes, the estate can claim: every major doctrine statement has a testable invariant, and the invariant suite runs in continuous integration. We can show the traceability matrix: each of the twenty-five core invariants pointing to the doctrine sentence it expresses, the code that enforces it, and the test that falsifies violations. We can show the rejection logs: a mutated approval digest failing, an expired approval failing, a duplicate tap returning the existing decision rather than a second execution, an optimistic completion — a delegated claim with no verification — failing the completion gate. We can show the chaos results: device offline during approval producing no authoritative consent; intent changed after card render invalidating the prior approval; a subagent's completion claim without evidence producing verification input, not completion. The doctrinal significance is the conversion of "no proof, no done" from a slogan into a property with a test suite. A critic who asks "what happens if the approval is mutated in transit?" receives not an explanation but a test name, a log, and a matrix row. The suite is citable as empirical validation in exactly the sense the evaluation found missing: it is measured behavior of the built system, not a specification of intended behavior. The estate will also be able to state the suite's limits honestly — which invariants are property-tested exhaustively, which are chaos-tested on a scenario list, which code paths the tests do not cover — because a verification claim without stated coverage is the same overclaim the papers refused to make about isolation.

4.3 The Consistency Contract, Documented Once Gap 3 closes, the estate can claim: five client surfaces observe one estate, and the rules by which they observe it are written down, implemented, and tested. We can show the hybrid guarantee matrix: status eventually consistent with freshness stamps; evidence revision-stable; approval decisions strongly consistent; consequential execution lin‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

earized under a single writer; live-source facts read current at decision time; artifacts digest-verified; notifications atleast-once hints, never authoritative state. We can show the revision-token protocol and the conflict resolution table, and we can show the tests: two devices attempting approval with the first valid decision recorded winning; a stale card's tap rejected with the fresh intent presented; an offline tap recorded as a pending gesture, never as consent; clock skew and network loss producing degraded or unknown renderings, never false operational ones. For the operator, this is the gap whose closure is most directly felt: it is the difference between "the operator approved" as five possibly disagreeing sentences and "the operator approved" as one authoritative fact. The estate will be able to demonstrate that no device can become a source of truth — that the Watch, the phone, and the iPad are views, and the orchestrator is the record. The core principle becomes demonstrable rather than aspirational: status may be eventually consistent; consequence is strongly consistent. A doctoral committee reading this will recognise a standard distributed-systems contribution applied to a novel domain — human authority exercised across personal surfaces — and that is precisely the level at which the thesis wants to be read.

4.4 Idempotency, Guaranteed Once Gap 4 closes, the estate can claim: consequential actions are at-most-once unless the system has observed that they did not occur or are safely repeatable, and we can demonstrate it. We can show the idempotency key schema — hash(work_id,

intent_id,

intent_digest,

action,

target,

scope,

revision,

opera‐

tion_class) — and the execution record with its full state progression from prepared through execution_unknown to completed or cancelled. We can show the duplicate-tap test: first tap validates and records,

second tap returns the existing decision and state, never a second execution. We can show the ambiguity protocol in action: an execution whose outcome was never observed is marked execution_unknown, never auto-retried if consequential, verified externally where possible, and escalated with the next safe option — visible to the operator, never silently completed. We can show the external system classification applied to the estate's actual connectors: which integrations offer native idempotency, which offer read-after-write verification, which offer neither and are therefore governed by the strongest approval or manual execution. This is the gap whose closure answers the operator's hardest question — "did it happen once, or twice?" — with a record rather than a reassurance. The governed saga becomes the transactional form of the doctrine chain: preparation without approval, approval bound to exact target and effect, execution requiring valid approval, failed verification yielding unknown or failed, compensation requiring its own approval if consequential, and no silent rollback of consequential effects. The estate will be able to say that its financial transfers, publications, and deletions are governed by stated transactional semantics, tested under the failures that actually occur — retries, duplicate taps, network loss — and that the semantics are the same whether the action was requested from the phone, the Watch, or a routine.

4.5 The Security Boundary, Formalised Once Gap 5 closes, the estate can claim: our security posture is a model, not a confession. We can show the asset register, with the operator's attention and approval authority listed first — the assets the whole architecture exists to protect. We can show the trust-boundary map across all ten boundaries, the capability matrix in which every capability names its subject, action, object, scope, condition, expiry, approval requirement, and evidence requirement, and the threat catalogue covering STRIDE plus the agent-specific classes: prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, approval fatigue, subagent deception. We can show the control matrix: every consequential path with its named threat and its named control, and the security test suite that exercises them — including the injection test that plants a malicious instruction in tool output and verifies that no consequential action follows, and the approval-rate metrics that make fatigue visible before it becomes compliance. Crucially, the estate will be able to state its isolation level honestly and precisely: Level 0, procedural, with the compensating controls that make Level 0 defensible — doctrine-separated roles, handoff-only cross-role access, audit logs, the Secure Vault as the credential boundary — named, tested, and mapped to threats. And it will be able to

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

show the upgrade ladder to Level 5 with entry conditions, so that "procedural isolation" is a documented current state on a real plan rather than a permanent confession. The doctoral adjective this gap secures is "human-governed": the estate will be able to show that the operator's authority is protected against spoofing by device-bound sessions and intent digests, that evidence is protected against tampering by digest binding and tamper-evident logging, and that the operator is protected against fatigue by exact intent binding, risk classes, and measured approval rates. A boundary that is mapped, controlled, and tested is defensible; the papers' honesty becomes the model's first row rather than its last word.

4.6 The Router, Justified by Data Once Gap 6 closes, the estate can claim: our routing policy is justified by measured outcomes, not intuition. We can show the decision log schema with outcome labels — outcome, human repair, repair time, cost units, latency, attention interruptions, evidence completeness — and the comparison the four-stage experiment produced: the local heuristic, cache-only, and unconstrained baselines replayed over the same labelled history, with the TypeSafe backend evaluated if approved, and controlled live trials on low-risk tasks with random assignment where the workload permitted. We can show the table: accepted work rate, cost per accepted artifact, repair rate, loop rate, latency, and attention interruptions per policy — with the winning policy's margin and the sample's limits stated, Bayesian estimates where samples are small, and the explicit statement that consequential actions were never randomized. This is the gap that converts the thesis from a verified architecture into an evaluated one. The doctoral claim — that a doctrine-governed control plane increases accepted work while reducing human repair, attention cost, and unsafe consequential action — is a comparative claim, and the router experiment is where the comparison lives. The estate will be able to say not only that its router is safe — the guardrails (it never approves consequence, never overrides approval boundaries, never hides failures, never becomes a second orchestrator) hold under the invariant suite — but that its router is worthwhile: that the governed path through the machine measurably outperforms the plausible alternatives on the metrics the operator actually pays. "A cheap wrong answer is not optimal" becomes a measured statement rather than a design aphorism. And where the data is thin — where the estate's natural workload could not supply the sample the trial needed — the evidence package will state the power limits explicitly, because an underpowered comparison presented as conclusive would be the exact kind of overclaim the whole programme exists to eliminate.

4.7 The Final Doctoral Claim With the six gaps closed, the estate's final claim is the roadmap's sentence: A formally specified, empirically validated, human-governed agent control plane. Each adjective is now earned by a specific body of evidence. Formally specified: the canonical glossary, the state machine, the event vocabulary, the contract schemas — one normative spec, versioned and reviewable, reconciling all three papers. Empirically validated: the model checker results, the invariant suite's rejection logs, the consistency tests under clock skew and network loss, the idempotency tests under duplicate taps and ambiguous outcomes, the router's measured comparison. Human-governed: the threat model with the operator's authority as the protected asset, the approval binding that makes consent exact, the consistency contract that makes the operator's decision one authoritative fact, the attention-cost measurement that makes the operator's scarcest resource a managed quantity. This is deliberately stronger than "we built a personal AI assistant," and the strength is in the narrowing. "We built a personal AI assistant" is a claim about a product; it is evaluated by demonstration and it expires with the demo. "A formally specified, empirically validated, human-governed agent control plane" is a claim about a class of systems; it is evaluated by proof, by test, and by measurement, and it survives the demo because its evidence is inspectable after the fact. The thesis structure the roadmap proposes — from the problem of attention and authority, through the contracts-around-the-model thesis, the architecture, the formal model, the security model, the evaluation, to the limita‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

tions — is the standard shape of a systems dissertation, and the estate's evidence now fills that shape rather than gesturing at it. The limitations section is part of the claim: the failure model's boundaries, the invariant suite's coverage, the router trial's power, the isolation level's honesty. A thesis that states where its proofs stop is a thesis a committee can trust where its proofs run.

4.8 What Changes for the Operator The programme's evidence is addressed to a committee, but its effects are addressed to the operator. The day-to-day differences, stated as before-and-after contrasts: 1. Approvals become provably bound. Before: the operator approves an intent and trusts that the approval binds to the exact target and effect. After: the binding is a tested invariant — a mutated intent demonstrably invalidates the prior approval, an expired approval demonstrably cannot execute, and the operator can be shown the test rather than told the doctrine. 2. Duplicate taps become harmless. Before: a double-tap on a Watch approval card, or a retry after a network blip, is governed by care and hope. After: the first tap records, the second returns the existing decision; at-most-once is a demonstrated property of the execution record, not a property of the operator's careful thumb. 3. Failures become visible, never silent. Before: an ambiguous execution — requested but unobserved — risks being narrated as completion by a fluent model. After: it is marked execution_unknown, escalated with the next safe option, and rendered as unknown; "no proof, no done" is enforced by the machine, not by vigilance. 4. Paused stays paused and unknown stays unknown. Before: the Watchtower's rendering discipline — operatorpaused reads as paused, never down — is a stated rule. After: it is a tested invariant under the consistency contract, and a stale cache can never again present itself as a live operational state. The operator stops spending attention on non-events and stops missing real ones. 5. Attention cost becomes measured. Before: the estate's founding claim — that it protects the operator's attention — is asserted but unmeasured. After: the router experiment measures attention interruptions per policy alongside cost, latency, and repair, and the operator scorecard carries real numbers. The estate's central promise becomes a quantity with a trend line. 6. The router's choices become justified. Before: the router's local policy is plausible and its shadow logs are unread. After: the policy the estate runs is the policy the measurements selected, with the margin and the limits stated. When the operator asks why a task took the expensive path, the answer is data. 7. The security boundary becomes a map. Before: "procedural isolation, honestly stated" is the whole of the posture. After: the operator can see the ten trust boundaries, the threats per consequential path, the controls, and the upgrade ladder — and can decide, with full information, which level of isolation the estate's actual risks warrant next. 8. Publishable by construction. Before: the estate's artifacts — glossary, schemas, test suites, threat model — are produced for the operator's eyes only, and publication would require a separate sanitising effort. After: every phase's output is produced publishable — the system public, the principal's private life never — so the opensource release is a publication decision, not a production project. The boundary is a tested property (Phase 5), not a hope. None of these changes adds a feature. That is the point the roadmap makes in its final recommendation, and it bears repeating as this section's close: the fastest way to fix the gaps is not to add more features. The sequence is freeze the doctrine, formalize the machine, turn doctrine into invariants, define consistency and idempotency, formalize the boundary, measure the router, integrate the evidence. What changes for the operator is not what the estate can do. It is what the estate can prove — and therefore what the operator can trust without spending attention to verify.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

5. Phase 0 — The Canonical Glossary Reconciling the vocabularies of Papers I–III into one normative specification.

5.1 Three Vocabularies, One System Papers I–III describe the same estate, but each paper coined its vocabulary for its own audience. Paper I (the operating white paper) describes the doctrine chain that every unit of work must traverse: Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine

Paper II (the backend technical paper) describes the request lifecycle through which accepted work moves: Brief → Route → Plan → Execute → Verify → Approve → Handoff

Paper III (the front-end paper) describes the operator loop as experienced on the five client surfaces: Brief → Work → Evidence → Approval → Decision

These three formulations are philosophically consistent — the introduction to this paper establishes that — but they are not the same sentence. A formal programme cannot begin from three sentences. Phase 0 exists to produce one: a single canonical glossary in which every term has one normative definition and one owner. The glossary below is that sentence, expanded into 34 terms. Every later section of this paper — the state machine (§6), the formal proof (§7), the invariant catalogue (§8), and the phase specifications that follow — uses these definitions and no others. Where this glossary conflicts with the usage of Papers I–III, the glossary governs; the reconciliation decisions in §5.4 record exactly where such conflicts were found and how they were resolved. The operator (HgX) should note what this section is not. It does not change the doctrine. Role isolation remains procedural, approval remains the operator's explicit consent at the consequence boundary, and evidence remains observed proof rather than fluent narration. What changes is only that each of those claims now has one name, one definition, and one owning document — so that a model checker, a test suite, and a doctoral committee can all refer to the same thing.
