Three observations follow from the table. First, Brief, Evidence, and Approval are already shared vocabulary across the papers that use them; no reconciliation is required, only canonisation. Second, several lifecycle stages name activities (Route, Plan, Verify) rather than governed objects; the canonical model governs objects (the routing decision, the plan, the evidence packet) because objects can carry digests, revisions, and acceptance tests while activities cannot. Third, Paper III's Decision and Paper II's Approve name the same operator moment from opposite sides of the glass: the operator experiences a decision; the estate records an approval stage. The decision packet (§5.6) is the artifact of that moment, and the two names are thereby reconciled without either paper being wrong.

5.3 Why the Vocabularies Are Compatible Compatibility here is a precise claim, not a diplomatic one. Two vocabularies are compatible for the purposes of this programme if every element of each can be mapped to the canonical model without changing what the estate does — that is, the mapping is a renaming plus a small number of explicit structural decisions, and no paper's operational semantics are altered. The doctrine chain (Paper I) is a governance ordering: it says which contracts a unit of work must satisfy and in what sequence authority is engaged. The request lifecycle (Paper II) is an execution ordering: it says what the orchestrator does, step by step, to move work through those contracts. The operator loop (Paper III) is an observation ordering: it says what the operator sees and where the operator's authority enters. Three orderings of one process, each complete for its audience, each naming the joints it cares about. The doctrine chain does not name "Plan" because planning is the orchestrator's internal business, not a governance joint. The lifecycle does not name "Role" because the orchestrator is the subject of the lifecycle, not one of its stages. The operator loop collapses "Route → Plan → Execute → Verify" into "Work" because the operator neither routes nor plans; the operator sees work being done and then sees evidence. None of these are contradictions. They are projections — the same object viewed from the governance axis, the execution axis, and the observation axis. The canonical model in §6 is the object itself: the work-state machine, whose states and events give each projection a shared referent. Once the machine exists, "the operator loop's Evidence" and "the lifecycle's Verify" are no longer two phrases that happen to mean similar things; they are two names for the transition in_progress → verifying and the object it produces. That is what freezing the contracts means, and it is why Phase 0 precedes proof: you cannot model-check a system whose states have three names.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

5.4 Reconciliation Decisions Where the projections genuinely differed in structure — not merely in naming — this section records the decision taken, its rationale, and the section that owns its consequences. These decisions are part of the normative spec, not commentary on it. RD-1 — "Execute" is split across two machine states. Paper II's Execute stage covers both non-consequential tool work (reading a calendar, drafting text, computing a figure) and consequential action (sending, publishing, purchasing). The canonical machine places non-consequential execution inside in_progress and reserves the executing state for approval-gated consequential execution. Rationale: the approval gate is the safety-critical boundary of the estate, and a boundary that is a stage label rather than a machine state cannot carry a transition guard. Consequences owned by §6 (transition table) and §7 (the can_enter_executing guard). RD-2 — Approval is a state–event pair, not a stage. Paper II's "Approve" and Paper III's "Approval" both become the awaiting_approval state together with the events approval_requested, approval_granted, approval_denied, and approval_expired. Rationale: an approval is not something the estate does; it is something the estate waits for, and waiting is a state with an expiry. This decision is what makes INV-A4 (expired approval unusable) expressible as a guard rather than a hope. Owned by §6. RD-3 — "Handoff" is an event and a packet, not a state. Paper II's final stage, "Handoff and capture," decomposes into the verifying → completed transition (acceptance passed, §6), the delivery of the handoff to the operator, and the promotion of stable method or durable facts to their stores. Rationale: completion is a property of the work item (a state); handoff is the communication of that property plus its evidence. Conflating them would let a delivered message masquerade as a verified result — precisely the optimistic-completion failure INV-C5 forbids. Owned by §6 and §8. RD-4 — "Decision" (Paper III) and "Approve" (Paper II) meet in the decision packet. The operator's tap is a decision event; the estate's stage is approval. The decision packet — carrying intent_id, intent_digest, the decision, the device session, and the server-recorded timestamp — is the single artifact both names describe. Rationale: this makes INV-A5 (offline approval cannot create authoritative consent) testable, because "authoritative" now has a definition: recorded by the server, digest-matched, timestamped. Owned by §6 (contract schema overview) and Phase 3. RD-5 — A routine is a brief generator, not a work-item state. Paper I's "Routine" and Paper II's "promote to routine" describe scheduling, not a phase of work. Canonically, a routine is a scheduled skill that has earned repetition; each firing emits a brief_received event, and the resulting work item traverses the machine independently. Rationale: routines must be pausable, degradable, and observable by the Watchtower as services — none of which is expressible if "routine" is a terminal stage of a work item. Owned by §6 (Watchtower contract) and the glossary definition below. RD-6 — Role is a governance boundary; subagent and worker are runtime constructs. Paper I's "Role" is not a kind of process. A role is a named, bounded remit with its own memory scope and approval posture; a subagent is the estate's implementation of delegated work; a worker is the generic term for any delegated execution unit. A subagent executes within a role; the role is not the subagent. Rationale: this preserves Paper I's honest statement that role isolation is procedural — the boundary is doctrine, discipline, and filesystem convention, not a process boundary — while giving Phase 5 a clean subject (the role) and a clean mechanism (the worker) to formalise. Owned by §11 (Phase 5).

5.5 Open Questions The following decisions are genuinely unresolved. Each is marked as required, with a recommendation and the reason the programme can proceed with the recommendation as a working assumption.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Open question OQ-1 — Should verifying distinguish human verification from machine verification? The machine's guard for verifying → completed is acceptance_passed; the guard does not care who ran the acceptance test. Recommendation: keep one verifying state and record the verification method (human, automated test, external observation) as provenance inside the evidence packet. Rationale: splitting the state doubles the transition table for no guard-level benefit, and the distinction that matters — whether the acceptance test was stated before the output was observed — is a property of the packet, not the state. If Phase 7's doctoral evidence requires human-verification rates as a metric, the packet provenance already supports it. Open question OQ-2 — Should blocked distinguish blockage-on-operator from blockage-on-source? A work item can stall because the operator has not answered a clarifying question or because a live source is unreachable; the recovery paths differ. Recommendation: keep one blocked state and record the blockage cause in the execution record. Rationale: as with OQ-1, the guard structure is identical (blockage cleared → in_progress), and cause is diagnostic metadata. The Watchtower's rendering may surface the cause without the machine needing a new state. Open question OQ-3 — Where does an expired approval return? The roadmap skeleton lists awaiting_approval → cancelled but does not say where approval_expired leads. Cancelling the work because the operator was slow to decide conflates intent expiry with work abandonment. Recommendation: approval_denied → cancelled (the operator refused the consequence; the work item ends), while approval_expired → in_progress (the intent lapsed; the work item survives and may issue a fresh approval_requested with a new intent and revision). Rationale: expiry is a property of the intent, not a verdict on the work — INV-A4 kills the approval, not the task. This recommendation is adopted in §6's transition table; if the operator prefers expiry to cancel work items, the change is one row.

5.6 The Canonical Glossary Each term carries exactly one normative definition and one owner. The owner is the phase and specification document responsible for the term's formal treatment; questions of meaning are settled by the owner, and no other document may redefine the term. Work item. One bounded unit of requested estate work, identified by a work_id, carrying a revision counter and a single current state from the canonical machine. Owner: Phase 0 spec (spec/state-machine.md). Brief. The structured submission that creates a work item: objective, audience, success criteria, tools, constraints, and deadline, from the operator or an authorized routine. A brief is well-formed only if its success criteria are stated as acceptance tests. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Artifact. Inspectable output produced by work — a file, a message draft, a figure, a decision record. An artifact is content-addressed by digest and bound to a work-item revision; fluent text that has not been observed against its acceptance test is not an artifact. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Evidence packet. The structured proof bundle attached to work: artifact digests, live-source observations with freshness stamps, test results, approval references, and explicitly listed gaps. The packet is the unit of verification; "no proof, no done" is the rule that no work item completes without one. Owner: Phase 0 spec (contracts/evidencepacket.schema.json). Approval intent. The exact consequential action awaiting the operator: action, target, effect, scope, revision, and expiry, bound together by the intent digest. An intent is not a request for general permission; it is a fully specified consequence the operator may grant or deny. Owner: Phase 0 spec (contracts/approval-intent.schema.json). Intent digest. The cryptographic digest over the canonical serialisation of an approval intent. The digest is the binding mechanism: any change to target, effect, scope, or revision changes the digest and invalidates prior approval (INV-A3). The exact canonicalisation and hash function are defined by the Phase 0 spec. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Approval decision. The operator's recorded verdict on an intent — granted or denied — captured with intent_id, intent_digest, device session, timestamp, and authentication method. A decision is authoritative only when recor‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

ded by the server; a tap on a client surface is a gesture until the server records it (INV-A5). Owner: Phase 0 spec (contracts/decision-packet.schema.json). Routine. A scheduled skill that has earned repetition through reviewed, accepted work. A routine is a brief generator, not a work-item state: each scheduled firing emits brief_received, and the resulting work item has its own lifecycle. Routines are silent by default and observable by the Watchtower. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Live source. An authoritative current-state system — a calendar, a mailbox, a price feed, a device sensor — that owns some changing fact about the world. Current facts are read from live sources at decision time; they are never inferred from memory. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Durable memory. Identity, preferences, principles, and commitments that persist across sessions: the curated MEMORY.md, relationship pages, and dated notes. Durable memory carries what the operator is and has decided; it never carries what the world currently is — that is the live source's job, and the orchestrator reconciles the two without merging them by assumption. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Orchestrator. The single authoritative control plane of the estate: it receives briefs, routes, plans, delegates, verifies, requests approval, and owns the final handoff. There is exactly one orchestrator; clients render, subagents execute bounded work, the router recommends — none of them orchestrate. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Subagent. The estate's implementation of delegated work: an independent bounded job briefed by the orchestrator, whose output is verification input, never a completion signal. The orchestrator remains accountable for acceptance of delegated work (INV-C3). Owner: Phase 0 spec. Worker. The generic term for any delegated execution unit, including subagents and any future execution mechanism. In the current estate every worker is a subagent; the term exists so the specification survives the mechanism. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Handoff. The delivery to the operator of a completed work item's result, evidence packet, gaps, and links, together with the promotion of stable method or durable facts to their stores. Handoff is the communication of completion, not completion itself (RD-3). Owner: Phase 0 spec. Revision. The monotonically increasing counter on a work item, artifact, evidence packet, or intent. Every mutation creates a new revision; old revisions are immutable. Revision binding is what prevents an approval granted against yesterday's artifact from executing against today's (INV-A2, INV-E2). Owner: Phase 0 spec. Idempotency key. The deterministic key hash(work_id, intent_id, intent_digest, action, target, scope, revision, operation_class) that identifies one logical consequential operation across retries, duplicate taps, and network failures. The server records the key's execution state; a repeated key returns the recorded outcome instead of executing again. Owner: Phase 4 idempotency spec (spec/idempotency.md). Execution record. The server-side record of one consequential operation's lifecycle: prepared → approved → execution_requested → execution_started → execution_observed / execution_failed / execution_unknown → completed / cancelled, with the idempotency key, approval reference, attempt count, ob‐

served result, and safe-to-retry flag. Owner: Phase 4 idempotency spec. Saga. The governed transaction pattern for consequential work, used because the estate assumes no distributed transactions: prepare → approval → execute → verify → record evidence → compensate (only if approved and possible). Each step has its own approval and evidence requirements; there is no silent rollback of consequential effects. Owner: Phase 4 idempotency spec. Capability. A formal grant with seven fields — subject, action, object, scope, condition, expiry, approval requirement, evidence requirement — specifying exactly what a worker, routine, or connector may do. Capabilities are the unit of least privilege in Phase 5. Owner: Phase 5 security spec (spec/security-threat-model.md). Trust boundary. A named interface across which trust is not transitive: the ten boundaries of the roadmap (devices ↔ estate edge, edge ↔ orchestrator, orchestrator ↔ tools, ↔ subagents, ↔ connectors, ↔ external web, ↔ Secure

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Vault, ↔ external router, client ↔ notification system, personal ↔ shared surfaces). Data crossing a boundary is validated; authority crossing a boundary is re-established. Owner: Phase 5 security spec. Freshness. The property of an observation that it was taken recently enough to decide on: every live-source reading carries observed_at and fresh_until. A reading past fresh_until is stale and cannot satisfy a guard that requires current truth (INV-S4, INV-E5). Owner: Phase 3 consistency spec (spec/consistency.md). Acceptance test. A bounded check with a stated test, applied against observed output — never against fluent text. Every brief states its acceptance tests; verifying → completed requires them passed and recorded in the evidence packet (INV-C1, INV-C2). Owner: Phase 0 spec. Repair cost. The human effort required to correct an accepted artifact that should not have been accepted: measured in minutes of operator or reviewer time plus the downstream cost of the error. One of the objective function's penalty terms in router evaluation. Owner: Phase 6 router spec (spec/router-policy.md). Attention cost. The operator's scarce resource, measured as interruptions weighted by context-switch severity: a silent routine costs nothing; an approval request costs a decision; an ambiguous alert costs investigation. The estate's first duty is to spend it only where evidence and authority require. Owner: Phase 6 router spec. Shadow mode. The router's current operating posture: it observes decisions and logs recommendations without overriding orchestrator judgment. Shadow mode is how the estate gathers labelled decision history for the Phase 6 evaluation without risking live routing authority. Owner: Phase 6 router spec. Escalation. The controlled transfer of a work item from autonomous handling to operator attention, with the observed state, the blocked decision, and the next safe option attached. Escalation is never silent and never a bare error string; it is a handoff of a decision the estate cannot make alone. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Chaos test. An experiment that injects a defined fault — device offline during approval, duplicate tap, stale live source, digest mismatch — into a running work item and asserts that the invariants hold and the failure is visible. Chaos tests are Layer 3 of the Phase 2 verification strategy. Owner: Phase 2 invariant spec (spec/invariants.md, tests/chaos/). Property test. A test that asserts an invariant over generated inputs: random approval mutations, expiry times, duplicate events, network loss, subagent claims, stale sources. A property test fails if any generated case violates the invariant; it is Layer 2 of the Phase 2 strategy. Owner: Phase 2 invariant spec (tests/properties/). Traceability matrix. The table binding each invariant to its doctrine source, the code or contract that implements it, and the tests that verify it (invariant → doctrine → code → test). The matrix is the audit trail a doctoral committee follows from claim to evidence. Owner: Phase 2 invariant spec. Scorecard. The operator's instrument for measuring estate performance against attention and authority: accepted work, repair cost, attention cost, and related metrics, populated from router logs, evidence packets, and decision records. Paper I proposes the instrument; this programme's later phases schedule its population. Owner: Phase 7 doctoral evidence. Unknown state. The rendering required when observation is insufficient: not failure, not a guess, but the explicit statement that the state cannot be established. Unknown is forced by observation_unavailable and can never be rendered as operational (INV-S1). Owner: Phase 0 spec. Paused state. The operator-intent stop: the operator paused the service or work item. Paused is not an outage, not a failure, and never renders as failed (INV-S2). Resumption re-validates the plan against live sources before work continues. Owner: Phase 0 spec. Degraded state. Observed impairment threatening the outcome: the service runs but a defined health signal — source freshness, error rate, latency — has crossed its threshold. Degraded requires observed impairment, never inference (INV-S3). Owner: Phase 0 spec.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Completed state. The terminal state entered only from verifying when the acceptance test has passed against observed output with the evidence packet attached. Completion supported by fluent text alone is optimistic completion and is unreachable by construction (INV-C1, INV-C5). Owner: Phase 0 spec.

5.7 Why One Vocabulary Is a Precondition for Proof A model checker does not read intentions; it reads a transition relation over named states. If "completed" is also called "done," "handed off," and "accepted" in different documents, the property "no completion without evidence" is not one property but four — and the checker, the test author, and the committee will each prove a different one. The failure mode is not disagreement but the appearance of agreement: three teams certifying three different properties under one name, each correct about its own referent and none of them about the system. Doctoral evidence cannot be built on homonyms. There is a second, subtler reason. The invariants of §8 are the testable expressions of the machine's guards (§7), and the guards are the formal expressions of the doctrine chain. That chain — doctrine to guard to invariant to test — is the traceability the thesis depends on, and it is a chain of names. If the name changes at any link, the trace breaks, and the committee is left with a test that cannot be shown to test the doctrine it claims. Freezing the vocabulary is therefore not administrative tidiness. It is the act that makes the rest of the programme refer to something — the precondition without which proof, testing, and measurement are performances rather than evidence. There is a third reason, and it belongs to the trilogy's main vision. The frozen glossary is the vocabulary of the open specification: it is the document a community builds against, the file in which a stranger's pull request either conforms or does not. A programme that intends its control plane to be open-sourced cannot afford a private language; Phase 0's one normative spec is therefore also the publication contract — the first artifact the open-source release will carry, and the standard against which every later contribution is judged.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

6. Phase 0 — The Canonical Work-State Machine The twelve states, the legal transitions with their guards, the event vocabulary, and the specification repository. This section uses the glossary of §5 and defines the machine that §7 proves and §8 tests.

6.1 The Twelve States Each state carries one normative definition. A work item is in exactly one state at a time; the state is a property of the work item, not of any client's rendering of it. Where Papers I–III used nearby words ("done," "approved," "stuck"), those words are now retired in favour of the names below. received. A brief exists and has been recorded, but the orchestrator has not yet accepted scope. The work item is a

submission, not a commitment: it may be accepted, declined as out of scope, or held for one focused clarifying question. No tools have run; no plan exists. This is the only state in which the estate has taken on nothing. accepted. The orchestrator has accepted the brief's scope and the work item is committed to planning. Acceptance

is a scope decision, not a quality verdict: it means the request is well-formed, in remit, and from an authorised source — the operator (HgX) or an authorised routine. No execution has occurred. in_progress. A bounded plan exists, tools are selected, and work is underway. All non-consequential execution —

reading live sources, drafting, computing, delegating bounded sub-tasks — happens inside this state (RD-1, §5.4). The state also covers re-planning after a blockage clears or an intent expires. A work item may remain here indefinitely, but it may never complete from here. awaiting_approval. The next operation is consequential and the estate is waiting for the operator's decision on an

exact approval intent. Waiting is the state's defining property: the intent carries an expiry, and expiry is a transition, not a lingering condition (OQ-3, §5.5). No consequential effect may occur while a work item is in this state except via the single guarded exit to executing. executing. A valid, unexpired, digest-matched approval exists and the approved consequential action is underway.

This state is entered only through the approval gate; it is the only state from which consequential effects may be produced. Non-consequential work never enters here. The state ends when the result is observed — success or failure — never when the action is merely dispatched. verifying. The work claims completeness: an artifact exists, an evidence packet exists, and the acceptance test is

being applied against observed output. Verification is adversarial by design — the question is whether the output passes the stated test, not whether it reads convincingly. This is the only state from which completed is reachable. completed. The acceptance test has passed against observed output with the evidence packet attached. A terminal

state: nothing further happens to the work item itself, though its artifact may be handed off and its method promoted. Completion supported by fluent text alone is optimistic completion and is unreachable by construction. blocked. Progress is stalled on a condition outside the work item's control — an unreachable live source, a missing

permission, an unanswered clarifying question — but recovery is plausible and the plan remains valid. Blocked is distinguished from failed by recoverability: the estate expects to resume, not to abandon. The blockage cause is recorded in the execution record (OQ-2, §5.5). failed. A required source, permission, validation, or retry budget is unavailable and the work item cannot proceed.

Failed is terminal for the work item but informative for the estate: the failure is observed, recorded with its evidence, and escalated with the next safe option. Failure is visible by doctrine, and this state is its canonical form. paused. The operator has stopped the work item or its service by intent. Paused is not an outage and not a failure; it

is the machine's representation of "the operator said stop." A paused work item holds its state and revision; resumption re-validates the plan against live sources before work continues, because the world may have moved while it slept.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

unknown. The work item's state cannot be established through observation. Unknown is not failure and not a guess

— it is the explicit, rendered statement of insufficient observation, forced by the observation_unavailable event. It exists so that the alternative — rendering a guess as a state — is never the default. Unknown never renders as operational. cancelled. The work item has ended without completion by decision: the brief was declined, the operator denied

the approval intent, or the operator cancelled the work directly. Cancelled is terminal and deliberate. It is distinguished from failed by agency — somebody chose this — and the reason is recorded.

6.2 The Transition Table Every legal transition, with its triggering event and its guard stated in prose. Guards are the informal expression of the formal guards of §7; where §7 gives can_enter_completed as a formula, this table says what must be true in words. Transitions marked with an asterisk are Phase 0 additions to the roadmap skeleton, adopted under the reconciliation decisions of §5.4. #

Three notes on the table. First, T9's idempotency: a second approval_granted for the same intent is not a second transition. The server returns the recorded decision and the existing execution state — this is the machine-level expression of INV-A6 and the idempotency key of Phase 4. Second, T18's interaction with executing is deliberately narrow: pausing halts the initiation of further consequential steps, but an approved action already in flight is governed by the saga semantics of Phase 4, not by the pause. Open question: the exact safe-point semantics for pausing

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

mid-executing — whether the in-flight step runs to observation or is held — is owned by the Phase 4 saga specification, which must define it before the guard can be formalised. Third, T20/T21 make unknown a one-way door with a single keyed exit: the machine can always admit ignorance, and it can only leave ignorance through verified observation.

6.3 Forbidden Transitions The following transitions are explicitly forbidden. Each is the machine-level form of an invariant in §8; the parenthetical names the invariant it would violate. • F1. Any state → completed except via verifying with acceptance_passed. (INV-C1, INV-C5.) There is exactly one road into completed, and it passes through verification. • F2. awaiting_approval → executing without a valid, unexpired, digest-matched approval recorded by the server. (INV-A1.) The gate is the transition; there is no side entrance. • F3. executing → completed. Execution is not verification; an observed dispatch is not an accepted result. (INV-C5, INV-E1.) • F4. in_progress → executing. Consequential work cannot bypass the approval state, even when the operator is in a hurry and especially then. (INV-A1.) • F5. paused → executing. Resumption re-validates before anything consequential happens; a paused item resumes into in_progress, never into execution. (INV-A1, INV-E5.) • F6. unknown → any state except via observation_restored. Leaving ignorance requires evidence, not elapsed time or optimism. (INV-S1, INV-S4.) • F7. completed, failed, cancelled → any state. Terminal states are absorbing. New work is a new revision or a new work item, never a resurrection. (INV-C1.) • F8. failed → completed. Failure cannot silently become completion; the only forward path from failure is a new work item with its own evidence. (INV-C2.) • F9. verifying → executing. A failed verification never retries execution directly; the path is verifying → failed, or back through awaiting_approval with a fresh intent binding the retry. (INV-A2, INV-A7.) F7 deserves emphasis because it is the most commonly violated in informal systems: the temptation to "reopen" a completed item when a defect is found. The canonical model forbids it. A defect in a completed artifact is a new brief — "fix the defect" — with its own plan, evidence, and acceptance test. The completed item's evidence packet remains immutable, which is exactly what makes it evidence.

6.6 Contract Schema Overview Five JSON schemas carry the machine's data contracts. This section gives one paragraph on each — the key fields and their purpose. Full field-level sketches are deferred to Appendix C (§16), which owns the definitive field lists. The specification repository this section describes is itself a publication artifact. The machine, the event vocabulary, and the five schemas are the estate's open contract surface: versioned, reviewable, and produced to be published — the system public, the principal's private life never. When the reference implementation is released, this repository is what it is released against; a stranger should be able to reimplement the machine from this section alone. That is the standard Phase 0's freeze must meet: not merely internally consistent, but externally legible. watchtower.schema.json. The rendering contract for service state: service_id, state (enum restricted to operational, paused, degraded, unknown — the four Watchtower states, with paused explicitly distinct from

failed), last_observed_at, fresh_until, confidence, and evidence_ref. Key fields are the freshness pair and the closed state enum: the schema makes INV-S1 through INV-S4 structurally expressible, because a renderer validating against this schema cannot emit a state the enum does not contain or a reading past its fresh_until without downgrading confidence. evidence-packet.schema.json. The proof bundle: packet_id, work_id, revision, artifact_refs (each a

digest bound to a revision), source_observations (each with observed_at and fresh_until), test_results, approval_refs, gaps (explicitly listed, never empty-by-default), created_at, and a packet checksum. The key design decision is that gaps are a required field: INV-E4 (gaps stay visible) is enforced by making omission a schema violation rather than a documentation lapse. approval-intent.schema.json. The exact intent: intent_id, work_id, action, target, effect, scope, revision, expiry, risk_class, and intent_digest over the canonical serialisation of the rest. The digest field

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

is what makes INV-A2 and INV-A3 mechanical: the server recomputes the digest at decision time, and any mutation — however small — fails the match. decision-packet.schema.json. The recorded decision: intent_id, intent_digest (as presented), decision

(granted or denied), device_session_id, timestamp, auth_method, and server_recorded_at. The key fields are the digest echo and the server timestamp: together they are the definition of "authoritative" (INV-A5), and the duplicate-tap path returns this packet instead of creating a second one (INV-A6). artifact-manifest.schema.json. The digest manifest: artifact_id, work_id, revision, digest, media_type, location, and provenance (which worker produced it, from which sources, under which brief revi‐

sion). The manifest is what binds INV-E2 (packet revision matches artifact revision) to bytes: the packet references the manifest, the manifest references the digest, and the digest references the content.

6.7 Phase 0 Exit Criteria Phase 0 is complete when each of the following holds. The checklist is the roadmap's, expanded with the acceptance test for each item. • [ ] One normative spec. All three papers reconciled: the glossary (§5), the state machine (§6.1–§6.4), the event vocabulary, and the contract overviews exist in exactly one version each, in the specification repository. • [ ] One owner per concept. Every state, event, contract, and invariant names its owning phase and document; no concept is owned twice. • [ ] No duplicate definitions. No two documents define the same term differently — checked, where mechanically possible, by CI over the glossary and schema files. • [ ] Versioned and reviewable. The repository is under version control; changes to normative files carry rationales; the paper's version history records the freeze. • [ ] Reconciliation decisions recorded. RD-1 through RD-6 are adopted in the machine (§6.2) and the glossary (§5.6); open questions OQ-1 through OQ-3 carry recommendations and named owners for resolution. • [ ] Contract sketches complete. Appendix C (§16) holds the full field-level sketches of all five schemas, consistent with the overviews in §6.6. When this checklist is green, the programme may proceed to Phase 1. Nothing is proven yet — that is the point. What exists is something provable: a machine with named states, guarded transitions, and contracts with owners. The proof is §7's work.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

7. Phase 1 — Formal State Machine Proof Proving that the canonical machine of §6 cannot enter illegal or unsafe states under the defined failure model. This section uses the glossary of §5 and proves the machine of §6; it specifies no new states and no new transitions.

7.1 Objective and Method Choice The objective is stated exactly by the roadmap: prove that the work-state machine cannot enter illegal or unsafe states under the defined assumptions. "Fixed" has ten concrete meanings (§7.6 turns them into proof obligations): no completion without acceptance evidence; no consequential execution without valid approval; mutated or expired approvals cannot lead to execution; unknown cannot render as operational; paused cannot be confused with failed; failure events cannot silently become completion; optimistic completion is unreachable; offline approval cannot become authoritative; duplicate taps cannot duplicate execution; stale state cannot masquerade as current. The first decision is the modelling language. The roadmap recommends TLA+ or Alloy, notes Coq/Lean for stronger proof, and allows property-based state-machine testing if formal tools prove too heavy. The comparison below is the basis for the recommendation. Method

Expressiveness

Learning curve

Tooling

Fit for this system

TLA+

Temporal logic over state machines; native liveness ("every request eventually resolves") and safety ("no bad state reachable")

Moderate; PlusCal lowers the entry cost

TLC explicit-state model checker; mature, scriptable

Excellent: the artefact is a 12state machine with interleaved events (duplicate taps, out-oforder delivery); TLA+ is designed for exactly this

Alloy

Relational first-order logic; strong for structural constraints and bounded scope checking

Moderate; relational idiom is unfamiliar to most engineers

Alloy Analyzer; automatic counterexample generation

Good for contract structure (digest relations, revision binding) but weaker on temporal behaviour and liveness

Promela/ SPIN

Process algebra; strong for concurrent interleavings and protocol verification

Moderate; C-like syntax

SPIN model checker; excellent for message-passing protocols

Good, but the machine is a single state machine with event interleavings, not a network of communicating processes; the fit is adequate, not natural

Coq/Lean

Full dependent-type proof assistants; the strongest guarantees available

Steep; months to productive proof engineering

Superb but labourintensive; every lemma is manual

Overkill for the safety properties at issue and disproportionate to a 12-state machine; the cost would consume the programme

Propertybased statemachine testing

Executable: generates event sequences and asserts invariants

Low; standard engineering practice

Hypothesis (stateful), fast-check; integrates with CI

Excellent as engineering fallback and as Phase 2 Layer 2, but it tests rather than proves — it finds counterexamples without certifying absence

Recommendation: TLA+ as the primary modelling language, Alloy as a secondary structural check, propertybased state-machine testing as the engineering fallback. The defence is as follows. The properties to be shown are temporal: safety ("no reachable illegal state") and liveness ("every approval_requested eventually resolves to granted, denied, or expired"; "no work item waits in awaiting_approval past expiry without a transition"). TLA+ expresses both natively, and TLC checks both over the finite state space — twelve work states, six approval states, boolean and small-enum auxiliaries — which is well with‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

in explicit-state enumeration once the model abstracts timestamps to an ordered now and digests to symbolic values. PlusCal makes the model readable to engineers who will never learn raw TLA+, which matters because the model must be reviewed, not merely run. Alloy earns its secondary place for the structural half of the problem: the digestbinding relation (intent → digest → decision), revision immutability, and the contract schemas' relational constraints are precisely the bounded relational properties Alloy checks best. The fallback is honest and already scheduled: if TLA+ proves too heavy for the team, the property-based state-machine tests of Phase 2, Layer 2 become the primary evidence, and the doctoral claim is downgraded from "model-checked proof" to "systematic falsification attempted and failed" — a weaker but still substantive claim, and one the limitations section (§15) must then state plainly. Open question OQ-4 — TLA+ or Alloy as the single primary language, if the programme can fund only one? The roadmap leans toward either. Recommendation: TLA+, because the failure model (§7.5) is dominated by event interleavings (duplicate taps, out-of-order events, expiry racing a tap), and interleavings are TLA+'s home ground. Alloy remains the recommended second tool for contract structure, not a rival primary.

7.2 State Variables The model carries ten state variables — the roadmap's minimum set — each with a type and a meaning. Abstraction discipline: timestamps are modelled as integers on a totally ordered now; digests are modelled as symbolic values where equality is the only operation (the model checks binding, not cryptography — collision resistance of the hash function is an assumed lemma, stated in §7.6). Variable

Type

Meaning

work_state

Enum of the 12 canonical states

The work item's current state (§6.1); exactly one value at a time

approval_state

Enum: none, requested, granted, denied, expired, recorded

The lifecycle position of the current approval intent; recorded means the server has persisted the decision

intent_digest

Symbolic digest value

The digest of the canonical intent as presented to the operator; the binding anchor

intent_expiry

Integer timestamp

The instant after which the intent is dead; compared against now

evidence_exists

Boolean

Whether a complete evidence packet is attached to the current revision

acceptance_passed

Boolean

Whether the stated acceptance test passed against observed output

execution_observed

Enum: none, success, failure, unknown

What observation says about the consequential action's outcome

Enum: fresh, stale, unknown

Whether live-source readings are within their

source_freshness

fresh_until operator_pause

Boolean

Whether the operator has paused the work item or service

observation_status

Enum: observed, unavailable

Whether the work item's state can currently be established

Two variables deserve a note on why they are separate. evidence_exists and acceptance_passed are not the same fact: a packet can exist and be incomplete (missing tests), and a test can pass while its packet is malformed. The guard for completion requires both, and the separation is what lets the model distinguish "no evidence" from "bad evidence" — different failures, different tests (§8). Similarly, approval_state separates granted (the operat‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

or tapped) from recorded (the server persisted): the gap between those two values is exactly where INV-A5 (offline approval) lives, and a model that merged them could not express the illegal state at all.

7.3 Transition Guards Each safety-critical transition carries a guard — the formal version of the prose guards in §6.2. The guards are written here in predicate style for readability; the TLA+ model renders them as enabling conditions on actions. now is the model's current time; digest(i) is the recomputed digest of intent i. can_enter_completed ≜ work_state = verifying ∧ evidence_exists ∧ acceptance_passed ∧ ¬failure_observed. Completion requires the conjunction: the machine must be in verifying (F1), a complete

packet must exist (INV-C1), the test must have passed (INV-C2), and no failure may have been observed (INV-C2's second half). Weakening any conjunct must produce a counterexample (§7.7). can_enter_executing ≜ work_state = awaiting_approval ∧ approval_state = recorded ∧ now < intent_expiry ∧ digest(current_intent) = intent_digest ∧ ¬mutated ∧ ¬operator_pause. Exe‐

cution requires the recorded — not merely tapped — grant (INV-A5), a live intent (INV-A4), a digest match against the current intent (INV-A3), and no pause. Note that digest(current_intent) = intent_digest is evaluated at transition time, not cached from request time: this is the guard-level expression of "changed target/effect invalidates prior approval." can_render_operational ≜ observation_status = observed ∧ source_freshness = fresh ∧ ¬operator_pause ∧ work_state ∉ {failed, paused, unknown, cancelled, blocked}. Rendering is a

guarded act, not a default: operational is shown only when observation is available, sources are fresh, no pause is in effect, and the machine is in a state consistent with operation. This guard is the formal content of INV-S1 and INVS4. Rendering guards for the non-operational states complete the picture, because the illegal states of §7.4 include rendering failures: • render_paused ≜ operator_pause ∧ observation_status = observed — paused is rendered only when the pause itself is observed, never inferred from silence (INV-S2). • render_failed ≜ failure_observed ∧ observation_status = observed ∧ ¬operator_pause — failure rendering requires observed failure and yields to pause when both are true, because the operator's intent outranks the machine's diagnosis. • render_unknown ≜ observation_status = unavailable ∨ source_freshness = unknown — unknown is forced, not chosen; whenever observation fails or freshness is unknown, the other rendering guards' preconditions fail and this one holds (INV-S1). • render_degraded ≜ observed_impairment ∧ observation_status = observed — degraded requires a defined, observed impairment signal crossing its threshold (INV-S3); suspicion is not impairment. The guards compose: exactly one rendering guard holds in every reachable state, which the model checks as a coverage property — there is no reachable configuration in which the renderer has no legal rendering and no reachable configuration in which two renderings are simultaneously legal.

7.4 The Illegal-State Catalogue Each illegal state from the roadmap §1.4, with a plain-language account of the harm if it were reachable. These are the states the proof must show unreachable; §8 turns each into at least one executable test. 1. Completed without evidence packet. The machine reaches completed with evidence_exists = false. Harm: the operator — and any downstream routine — acts on output whose provenance, tests, and gaps were

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

never recorded. Optimistic completion becomes indistinguishable from verified completion, and the doctrine "no proof, no done" becomes unenforceable because there is no artefact to audit. 2. Executing without valid approval. The machine reaches executing with approval_state ≠ recorded, an expired intent, or a digest mismatch. Harm: consequential effects — sends, purchases, publications, deletions — occur without the operator's authority. This is the single most severe illegal state: every other property is about truthfulness, but this one is about agency. 3. Approved intent with changed target/effect. digest(current_intent) ≠ intent_digest while approval_state = recorded. Harm: the bait-and-switch — the operator approves sending a draft to one recipient and the machine sends a different text to another, or approves a $15 stake and the machine places $150. The approval ceremony is performed and the authority it confers is spent on something else. 4. Unknown rendered as operational. observation_status = unavailable while the renderer shows operational. Harm: the operator trusts a guess. A service whose state cannot be established is shown as healthy; the operator takes no corrective action precisely when corrective action is most needed. This is how outages hide inside green dashboards. 5. Paused rendered as failed. operator_pause = true while the renderer shows failed. Harm: the operator "fixes" an outage that is actually their own stop — restarting a service they meant stopped, re-running work they meant halted, and generating the exact consequential effects the pause was meant to prevent. 6. Optimistic completion from delegated claim. completed reached on a subagent's fluent claim with acceptance_passed = false. Harm: delegation becomes abdication. The orchestrator's accountability for acceptance (INV-C3) collapses, and the estate learns to report done when work merely sounds done — the failure mode the entire evidence discipline exists to prevent. 7. Offline approval becoming authoritative. approval_state transitions to recorded from a client gesture without server validation. Harm: a tap made in a tunnel, on a stale card, or by a replayed message becomes consent. The consequence boundary — the one place where the operator's authority is absolute — becomes forgeable.

7.5 The Failure Model Thirteen failure conditions, per the roadmap §1.5. For each: how the model represents it, and which illegal states it threatens. The model does not assume these failures away; it assumes them in and shows the guards hold regardless. 1. Network interruption. Modelled as event loss and delay: execution_observed remains none while the action may or may not have occurred, and observation_status may flip to unavailable. Threatens illegal states 1 and 7 — the temptation is to complete on dispatch ("it probably went through") or to treat a retried tap as a fresh grant. 2. Expired approval. Modelled as now advancing past intent_expiry while approval_state = requested. Threatens illegal state 2: a grant arriving after expiry must not open the gate. The model checks that the expiry transition (T10) is taken and that a late approval_granted is rejected, not queued. 3. Mutated intent. Modelled as a change to any intent field between request and decision, making digest(current_intent) ≠ intent_digest. Threatens illegal state 3 directly. The model checks the digest comparison at transition time (T9), including the adversarial case where the mutation arrives with the grant in the same event batch. 4. Duplicate tap. Modelled as two approval_granted events for the same intent, possibly with network duplication multiplying them further. Threatens a double-execution variant of illegal state 2. The model checks that the second grant is idempotent — it returns the recorded decision and produces no new transition — per the idempotency key of Phase 4. 5. Stale live source. Modelled as source_freshness = stale: readings exist but are past fresh_until. Threatens illegal state 4 — the renderer showing operational on expired readings — and illegal state 1, where

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

verification passes against yesterday's source. The model checks that stale freshness forces unknown rendering and blocks the completion guard's source-dependent conjuncts. 6. Missing evidence. Modelled as evidence_exists = false at the verifying → completed boundary, including the partial case (packet exists, tests missing). Threatens illegal state 1. The model checks that the completion guard's conjunction fails closed — missing evidence is not "evidence pending," it is a guard failure. 7. Subagent completion without proof. Modelled as a worker emitting a completion claim while acceptance_passed = false and evidence_exists = false. Threatens illegal state 6. The model checks that the claim is treated as verification input — it can move in_progress → verifying — but can never satisfy can_enter_completed. 8. Router failure. Modelled as the usage router (currently shadow mode, local policy) producing no recommendation or a corrupt one. Threatens no illegal state directly — the router recommends paths, never approves consequences (INV-R1) — but the model checks the fallback: routing failure degrades to direct orchestration, never to an unguarded transition. This is the formal shadow of INV-R5. 9. Device clock skew. Modelled as now differing between the client surface and the server by a bounded skew Δ. Threatens illegal states 2 and 4: a skewed client may present an "unexpired" intent the server considers dead, or a "fresh" reading the server considers stale. The model checks that all time comparisons use server now and that the skew bound is explicit — open question OQ-5: the acceptable skew bound Δ (recommendation: 60 seconds, with any decision inside the skew window re-validated rather than trusted). 10. Out-of-order events. Modelled as event permutation: approval_expired arriving before approval_granted, execution_observed arriving before execution_started. Threatens illegal states 2 and 7 — a grant processed after its expiry, or an observation credited to the wrong execution. The model checks event histories against the revision counter: stale events (revision behind current) are rejected, not applied. 11. Missing permissions. Modelled as a required capability absent at execution time. Threatens the executing → failed path being skipped in favour of silent partial execution. The model checks that missing permission produces failure_observed and the any-state transition to failed — visible failure, never silent degradation into a false success. 12. Credential challenge. Modelled as an authentication demand (re-authentication, Secure Vault unlock) arriving mid-flow, e.g. between approval_granted and execution_started. Threatens illegal state 7's cousin: execution proceeding on a grant whose authentication has lapsed. The model checks that the challenge suspends the transition — the grant is held, not honoured, until authentication is re-established — and that a denied challenge moves the work item to blocked, not failed. 13. Operator pause. Modelled as operator_paused arriving in any state, including mid-executing. Threatens illegal state 5 (pause rendered as failure) and a pause-during-execution race: the pause must prevent new consequential steps without corrupting the in-flight step's observation. The model checks T18's guard and the rendering precedence (render_paused outranks render_failed).

7.6 Proof Obligations Numbered, traceable, and each falsifiable — §7.7 explains what falsifiability demands of them. • PO-1. No reachable state satisfies work_state = completed ∧ ¬(evidence_exists ∧ acceptance_passed). (Fixed-meaning 1; illegal state 1.) • PO-2. No reachable state satisfies work_state = executing ∧ ¬(approval_state = recorded ∧ now < intent_expiry ∧ digest(current_intent) = intent_digest). (Fixed-meaning 2; illegal state 2.) • PO-3. For every reachable transition into executing, the intent digest at decision time equals the digest at request time; any mutation forces rejection. (Fixed-meaning 3; illegal state 3.) • PO-4. In every reachable state with observation_status = unavailable, the only legal rendering is unknown. (Fixed-meaning 4; illegal state 4.)

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

• PO-5. In every reachable state with operator_pause = true, the legal rendering is paused and never failed. (Fixed-meaning 5; illegal state 5.) • PO-6. evidence_of_failure from any state leads to failed or blocked; no event sequence containing failure_observed after verifying can reach completed. (Fixed-meaning 6.) • PO-7. A worker's completion claim with acceptance_passed = false can never satisfy can_enter_completed. (Fixed-meaning 7; illegal state 6.) • PO-8. No transition to approval_state = recorded is enabled by a client gesture alone; recording requires server validation of intent existence, expiry, digest, permissibility, and session. (Fixed-meaning 8; illegal state 7.) • PO-9. Two approval_granted events for one intent produce at most one executing entry and one consequential effect. (Fixed-meaning 9.) • PO-10. No guard that depends on source truth is satisfiable when source_freshness ≠ fresh. (Fixedmeaning 10.) • PO-11. completed, failed, and cancelled are absorbing: no transition leaves them. (F7, §6.3.) • PO-12 (liveness). Every approval_requested is eventually followed by exactly one of approval_granted, approval_denied, or approval_expired; no work item remains in awaiting_approval past intent_expiry without a transition. (The machine must not only be safe but responsive — a gate that never opens or closes is a denial of service against the operator's attention.) Assumed lemmas, stated explicitly because a proof is only as honest as its assumptions: (a) the digest function is collision-resistant — the model checks binding given distinct digests, not the cryptography; (b) the server's now is monotonic within the skew bound of OQ-5; (c) the event history is append-only — the model does not defend against history rewriting, which is a Phase 5 tamper-evidence concern, not a Phase 1 transition concern.

7.7 Counterexample Discipline A model checker that finds nothing has either verified the system or failed to look. The programme distinguishes the two by requiring that every accepted guard earn its place: for each guard in §7.3, the modelling team produces a weakened twin — the guard with one conjunct removed — and runs the checker against it. If the weakened guard yields no counterexample, the guard is suspect: either the conjunct is genuinely redundant (and should be removed, simplifying the model) or the failure model is too weak to exercise it (and the failure model must be strengthened). If the weakened guard yields a reachable illegal state, the counterexample is filed as evidence — the trace, the violated obligation, the illegal state reached — and the full guard is accepted as load-bearing. This is falsifiability as engineering practice, and it is what makes the Phase 1 results doctoral evidence rather than a certificate printed by a friendly tool. A guard that has never been shown to fail when weakened is an assertion; a guard whose weakened twin produces a concrete trace to executing without approval is a demonstrated necessity. The counterexample report (§4 deliverables) therefore contains two halves: the illegal states the full model excludes, and the illegal states the weakened models reach. The second half is the more valuable one — it is the proof that the proof was trying.
