7.9 Phase 1 Exit Criteria • [ ] Formal model exists. A TLA+ (or Alloy, per OQ-4) model of the §6 machine, with the ten state variables of §7.2 and the guards of §7.3, is committed under proofs/state-machine/ and reviewed. • [ ] No reachable illegal state. The checker finds no reachable instance of any §7.4 illegal state under the §7.5 failure model; PO-1 through PO-11 hold. • [ ] Liveness shown. PO-12 holds under stated fairness assumptions; the assumptions are documented, not buried. • [ ] Weakened guards produce counterexamples. For each guard, the weakened twin reaches an illegal state and the counterexample is filed; any guard that cannot be made to fail is either removed or its failure model strengthened, with the reasoning recorded. • [ ] Proof obligations documented. PO-1 through PO-12, the assumed lemmas, and the OQ-5 skew recommendation are written down as the proof's contract with its readers. • [ ] Results artefact produced. A results table in the form of §7.8 — property, outcome, interpretation — with the checker's raw output archived alongside it. • [ ] Model and results published. The formal model, the checker's raw output, and the results table are committed as publishable artifacts — the verification reproducible by anyone with the model checker, not merely asserted by the estate. When this checklist is green, the machine is proven under its failure model — and the programme turns to the harder question of whether the running estate honours the machine. That is Phase 2.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

8. Phase 2 — Invariant Verification Converting the estate's architectural promises into executable invariants, tested in three layers. This section uses the glossary of §5 and tests the machine of §6 against the guards of §7; it proves nothing new and defines no new states — it executes.

8.1 The Invariant Catalogue Twenty-seven invariants, every ID from the roadmap, grouped in five families. For each: the normative statement (the roadmap's wording, verbatim), the rationale (which doctrine it defends, in two to three sentences), and a concrete violation scenario (a short narrative of what goes wrong if it breaks). The violation scenarios are deliberately mundane — the failures this programme guards against are ordinary Tuesdays, not exotic attacks. Test-name convention used throughout: CT-<id> are Layer 1 contract tests, PT-<n> Layer 2 property tests (§8.2), CH-<n> Layer 3 chaos scenarios (§8.2).

A. Completion INV-C1 — No completion without evidence packet. Rationale. This is the executable form of "no proof, no done," the estate's first doctrine. The completion guard's (can_enter_completed, §7.3) first conjunct exists because a work item whose proof bundle is missing has no audit trail — nothing a committee, an operator, or a future routine can inspect to learn why the result was accepted. Violation. A subagent returns a drafted tax summary; the orchestrator, pressed for time, marks the work item completed because the text reads correctly. Months later the operator acts on a figure with no source attached — the figure was a hallucination, and no packet exists to show where it came from. (CT-C1, PT-4, CH-8) INV-C2 — No completion if required tests failed. Rationale. Evidence existing is not evidence passing. INV-C1 asks "is there a packet"; INV-C2 asks "did the test pass." The distinction matters because the most dangerous packet is a complete one that records a failure nobody read — proof attempted is not proof obtained. Violation. A routine's packet is complete — sources listed, artifact attached — but the acceptance test (all four legs resolved to final scores) failed on one leg. The work item completes anyway; the operator sees a graded slip that is wrong, backed by a packet that says so in a field nobody checked. (CT-C2, PT-4, CH-10) INV-C3 — No delegated claim produces completion without verification. Rationale. The delegation boundary (Paper II): subagent output is verification input, never a completion signal, and the orchestrator remains accountable for acceptance. Without this invariant, delegation becomes abdication — the estate learns to report done when work merely sounds done. Violation. A subagent claims the Dropbox upload succeeded and returns a share URL. The orchestrator completes without resolving the URL. The operator posts a dead link on X; the upload had failed silently, and the claim was fluent, confident, and false. (CT-C3, PT-4, CH-8) INV-C4 — No optimistic UI state overrides authoritative backend state. Rationale. Paper III's surfaces are fast and helpful; helpfulness must never rewrite the machine. Rendering is a guarded act (§7.3): what the operator sees is derived from server-recorded state, and a client's local optimism — "sent," "saved," "done" — has no authority until the server confirms it. Violation. The iPhone shows a payment as "Sent" because the tap registered locally, while the server never recorded the decision. The operator, seeing "Sent," tells the recipient the money is on its way. It is not, and the estate's own surface is the source of the falsehood. (CT-C4, CH-4, CH-5) INV-C5 — Completion supported by observed output, not fluent text. Rationale. The language model is the adversary of its own verification: it can describe a completed migration so convincingly that the description passes for the migration. Acceptance applies to observed output — bytes, responses, resolved URLs — never to narration about output. Violation. A subagent writes a beautiful paragraph describing the completed migration of files to the new directory layout. The orchestrator, reading the paragraph, marks the work complete. The files were never moved; the paragraph was the whole of the work. (CT-C5, PT-4, CH-8)

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

B. Approval INV-A1 — Consequential action cannot execute without approved intent. Rationale. The consequence boundary is the estate's load-bearing wall: sending, publishing, purchasing, deleting, and transferring stop for the operator's explicit consent. Every other invariant is about truthfulness; this one is about agency, and it is the single most severe to violate. Violation. A routine, acting on a standing instruction from months ago, publishes a post to X without a fresh approval intent. The post contains an outdated figure. The operator's authority was bypassed not by malice but by a stale permission nobody re-examined. (CT-A1, PT-4) INV-A2 — Approval binds to action, target, effect, scope, revision, expiry. Rationale. An approval is exact, never general. The six bound fields plus the intent digest (§5.6) are what make "the operator approved this" a checkable claim rather than a vibe — and what make INV-A3 mechanically enforceable instead of aspirationally stated. Violation. The operator approves "send the draft to the accountant." The recorded intent binds the action but leaves the target underspecified; the estate sends it to a stale address from an old contact entry. The approval ceremony was performed for a consequence that was never specified. (CT-A2, PT-1) INV-A3 — Changed target/effect invalidates prior approval. Rationale. The bait-and-switch guard: the digest is recomputed at decision time against the current intent, so any mutation — however small — voids the approval. Approval is granted to a specific future, not to a category of futures. Violation. The operator approves a slip purchase at stated legs; between approval and execution, one line drifts in the app builder. Without digest invalidation, the estate buys a different slip than the one approved — same ceremony, different consequence, and the operator's consent attached to something they never saw. (CT-A3, PT-1, CH-3) INV-A4 — Expired approval unusable. Rationale. Approval happens at the last safe moment precisely because the world moves; a decision made for Monday's world is not a decision about Friday's. Expiry is not a suggestion — the intent is dead, and the dead do not authorise. Violation. The operator approves a time-sensitive action on Monday; execution is delayed; the estate executes on Friday on Monday's approval. Prices moved, the context changed, and an expired consent produced a fresh consequence. (CT-A4, PT-2, CH-2) INV-A5 — Offline approval cannot create authoritative consent. Rationale. "Authoritative" has a definition: recorded by the server, digest-matched, timestamped (§5.6). A tap made in a tunnel, on a stale card, is a gesture until the server says otherwise. The client may queue the gesture; it may never mint the authority. Violation. The operator taps "Approve" on the Watch in a subway tunnel. The client, trying to be helpful, treats the queued tap as consent and the estate executes on reconnect. An unvalidated gesture became authority, and the consequence boundary was crossed by a radio shadow. (CT-A5, CH-1) INV-A6 — Duplicate taps must not duplicate execution. Rationale. The double-tap double-charge story: the operator taps "Pay," the screen does not confirm, the operator taps again. Human beings tap twice; the machine must execute once. The idempotency key (Phase 4) is the mechanism; this invariant is the requirement. Violation. The second tap is treated as a second decision and the transfer executes twice. The operator is charged double for one intent, and the estate's defence — "you tapped twice" — blames the victim of its own missing idempotency. (CT-A6, PT-3, CH-4) INV-A7 — Approval cannot authorize adjacent or future actions. Rationale. An approval binds one intent, one revision. Without this invariant, each approval is a potential blanket permission — consent creep by convenience, where "yes to this" quietly becomes "yes to things like this." Violation. The operator approves this week's slip purchase. The estate treats it as standing permission and buys next week's slip too. One consequence's approval became a subscription, and the operator discovers the doctrine breach on their statement. (CT-A7, PT-1)

C. Evidence INV-E1 — Every completed consequential run has an evidence packet. Rationale. Consequential effects demand the highest proof standard because they are the hardest to undo. A send cannot be unsent; the packet is what makes it reviewable after the fact — which sources were read, which approval bound it, what the gaps were. Violation. A publication goes out — consequential — and the work item completes with the artifact but no packet. When the operator

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

asks "why did we publish this figure," there is nothing to show: no sources, no approval reference, no gaps. The effect is real; its justification is vapour. (CT-E1, PT-4) INV-E2 — Evidence packet revision matches artifact revision. Rationale. Proof of yesterday's artifact is not proof of today's. Revision binding (§5.6) ties the packet to the exact bytes it describes; a revised artifact without a revised packet is an unverified artifact wearing a verified packet's clothes. Violation. A figure in the artifact is corrected after verification; the packet still describes the old revision. The operator reviews proof that no longer describes the thing it proves, and the correction — the most important fact — is the one thing unverified. (CT-E2, CH-5) INV-E3 — Missing evidence renders as missing. Rationale. A packet with a hole must look like a packet with a hole. Smoothing over a failed observation — omitting the section rather than marking it absent — converts ignorance into apparent completeness, which is the exact failure INV-E4's required gaps field exists to prevent structurally. Violation. A source observation failed; the packet's source section is silently omitted rather than marked missing. The operator reads a complete-looking packet and never learns that a leg of the proof is absent. (CT-E3, CH-7) INV-E4 — Gaps stay visible. Rationale. The companion to INV-E3 at the structural level: gaps are a required field, and omission is a schema violation, not a documentation lapse. Honesty about limitations is enforced by the contract, not requested by the culture. Violation. The estate knows the credential challenge was never resolved and lists no gap. The packet looks airtight; it is not, and the one fact the operator most needed — what was not proven — is the one fact not written down. (CT-E4, CH-10) INV-E5 — Sources current where live truth is required. Rationale. The memory/live-source separation (Paper I): changing facts are read at decision time, with freshness stamps. A packet that cites a stale reading as current truth is not evidence; it is a record of yesterday's world presented as today's. Violation. A decision is made on a price read cached yesterday; the live price moved overnight. The packet cites the stale reading with no freshness stamp, and the operator decides on a world that no longer exists. (CT-E5, PT-5, CH-7)

D. State rendering INV-S1 — Unknown never renders as operational. Rationale. The forced-unknown guard (§7.3): when observation fails, the renderer has exactly one legal output. A green dashboard that means "we cannot see" instead of "all is well" is how outages hide and how operators get blindsided. Violation. The Watchtower loses observation of a routine; the dashboard keeps showing green. The operator believes the morning briefing ran. It did not — the state was unknowable, not healthy — and the silence was rendered as success. (CT-S1, PT-5, CH-7) INV-S2 — Paused never renders as failed. Rationale. Pause is the operator's intent; failure is the machine's diagnosis, and intent outranks diagnosis. Confusing the two spends the operator's attention fixing their own deliberate stop — and risks restarting what was meant to stay stopped. Violation. The operator pauses all autonomous services while offline. The status page shows twelve red "failed" badges. On return, an hour goes to "fixing" an outage that was a decision — and one restarted service performs the exact consequential check the pause was meant to prevent. (CT-S2, CH-1) INV-S3 — Degraded requires observed impairment. Rationale. Suspicion is not impairment. Degraded is a strong claim — "this service is impaired" — and strong claims require a defined signal crossing a defined threshold. Otherwise every slow Tuesday becomes an incident and attention is spent on noise. Violation. A routine is marked degraded after one slow run, with no threshold crossed. The operator investigates a degradation that was never observed, only inferred — and the real, unobserved impairment elsewhere goes uninvestigated. (CT-S3) INV-S4 — Stale data shows reduced confidence or unknown. Rationale. Freshness is a rendering obligation, not a metadata nicety. Data past its fresh_until must either be re-read or be shown for what it is: old. Full-confidence display of stale data is a lie the schema could have prevented. Violation. The iPhone shows yesterday's figure at full confidence; the reading expired overnight. The operator makes a decision on a number the estate knew was stale but displayed as current — the interface lied by omission. (CT-S4, PT-5, CH-5) INV-S5 — Shared surfaces never render restricted content. Rationale. Trust boundary 10 (personal ↔ shared surfaces, §5.6): the TV is ambient and the room is shared. What is shown where the operator did not choose to show

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

it is a disclosure, and disclosures of financial, health, or legal content are consequential. Violation. The TV ambient display cycles estate status and renders the full text of an approval intent containing financial details. A guest in the room reads what was never meant for a shared surface — the estate disclosed by screensaver. (CT-S5)

E. Router INV-R1 — Router recommends path only, never approves consequence. Rationale. The router is an advisor; authority belongs to the orchestrator and the operator. This is the invariant that lets the router fail safely: a corrupt or compromised recommendation can waste money or time, but it cannot cross the consequence boundary, because the boundary does not listen to the router. Violation. The router, in a future non-shadow mode, selects "execute directly" for a consequential action and the estate treats the recommendation as approval. The consequence boundary is crossed by a heuristic — the cheapest path was also the unauthorised one. (CT-R1, CH-6) INV-R2 — Repeated identical error triggers escalation or path change. Rationale. The loop guard: doing the same failing thing twice is an experiment; doing it six times is a waste fire. The router must recognise the identical error signature and either change path or escalate with the evidence — never fund a third identical attempt on hope. Violation. The router keeps recommending the browser path for a site that blocks automation; each attempt fails identically. Six attempts burn cost and latency before anyone notices, because no invariant forced the second failure to become an escalation. (CT-R2) INV-R3 — Repeated no-progress action denied. Rationale. The stronger sibling of INV-R2: where R2 demands a path change, R3 permits the router to say no. A plan that has made no progress across repeated attempts is not a plan; it is a loop wearing a plan's name, and the router must deny it rather than fund it. Violation. A subagent keeps replanning the same failed approach; the router keeps approving the plan. Nothing progresses; the retry budget and the operator's patience drain together, because the router never exercised the one power that would have stopped it: refusal. (CT-R3) INV-R4 — Fresh cache may suppress refetch when sufficient. Rationale. The one router invariant that permits less work: when the cached reading is fresh and the decision does not require live truth, refetching is waste. This invariant is the licence for the cache — bounded, explicit, and revocable the moment freshness lapses. Violation. The estate refetches a live source every thirty seconds for a figure that changes daily, because no invariant permits the cache. Cost, latency, and source goodwill burn on freshness nobody needed — the absence of the invariant is itself the failure. (CT-R4) INV-R5 — Router fallback preserves safety if external backend fails. Rationale. The external router backend is optional; local policy is the fallback, and the fallback must be a policy, not an absence. "No router" is not a safe state — it is ungoverned routing, which is worse than expensive routing. Violation. The external backend goes down; with no fallback specified, the estate either freezes all routing or routes without any policy — sending consequential work down unguarded paths at the worst possible moment. The fallback must be local policy, never no policy. (CT-R5, CH-6)

8.2 The Three-Layer Verification Strategy Proving the machine (§7) shows the design is safe; testing the estate shows the running system honours the design. Three layers, each catching what the others cannot.

Layer 1 — Static contract tests Schema validation on every write path: no evidence packet, approval intent, decision packet, artifact manifest, or Watchtower rendering is persisted or emitted without validating against its contract (§6.6, full sketches in §16). The fail-closed rules are explicit and non-negotiable: 1. Unknown enums fail closed. A renderer receiving a state value outside the closed enum renders unknown; it never guesses the nearest match.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

2. Missing required fields fail. A packet without its gaps field, an intent without expiry, a decision without server_recorded_at — each is rejected at the write boundary, not patched downstream. 3. Expired approvals fail. Any decision presented against an intent with now ≥ intent_expiry is rejected before digest comparison — expiry is checked first because a dead intent's digest is irrelevant. 4. Mutated digests fail. The server recomputes the digest over the canonical serialisation at decision time; any mismatch rejects the decision, whatever the client claims. 5. Incomplete evidence cannot produce completion. The completion writer validates packet completeness against the brief's acceptance tests before the verifying → completed transition is recorded — the transition and the validation are one atomic step. Layer 1 runs in CI on every commit to the specification repository and on every estate deployment. It is the cheapest layer and catches the most embarrassing failures: the malformed packet, the missing field, the expired intent that a tired code path forgot to check.

Layer 2 — Property-based tests Generated inputs, asserted invariants. The generators: random approval mutations (field-level bit-flips and semantic swaps), expiry times straddling the now-boundary, duplicate and reordered event sequences, network loss windows, subagent completion claims with and without evidence, stale source readings with expired freshness. Five concrete property tests, each with its assertion: • PT-1 — Approval mutation fuzz. Generates: random mutations to intent fields (target, effect, scope, expiry, revision) applied between request and decision. Asserts: every mutated intent fails the digest match and no executing transition occurs. Covers INV-A2, INV-A3, INV-A7. • PT-2 — Expiry sweep. Generates: expiry timestamps from one year past to one year future, with decision attempts at random offsets. Asserts: any attempt with now ≥ intent_expiry is rejected; no late grant is honoured or queued. Covers INV-A4. • PT-3 — Duplicate decision storm. Generates: 1–50 duplicate approval_granted events per intent, with network-level duplication multiplying them further. Asserts: exactly one recorded decision per intent and at most one consequential execution. Covers INV-A6, PO-9. • PT-4 — Random event sequences. Generates: arbitrary event sequences over the §6.4 vocabulary, including out-of-order delivery, failure injection, and worker claims. Asserts: the machine never reaches completed without evidence_exists ∧ acceptance_passed, never reaches executing without a recorded valid approval, and terminal states are never left. Covers INV-C1–C5, INV-A1, INV-E1, PO-1, PO-2, PO-11. • PT-5 — Stale source injection. Generates: source readings with fresh_until in the past, mixed with fresh readings. Asserts: the renderer outputs unknown or reduced-confidence for every stale reading — never operational at full confidence — and no source-dependent guard is satisfied by stale data. Covers INV-S1, INV-S4, INV-E5, PO-10. Layer 2 is also the engineering fallback named in §7.1: if the TLA+ model proves too heavy, these tests — run at volume, with shrinking and seed archiving — become the primary falsification evidence, and the doctoral claim is stated accordingly.

Layer 3 — Chaos tests Fault injection into running work items, asserting that the invariants hold and the failure is visible. Ten scenarios, per the roadmap: • CH-1 — Device offline during approval. Setup: work item in awaiting_approval; the operator's device loses connectivity mid-decision. Fault: the tap is queued offline and replayed on reconnect. Expected: the server validates the replayed decision against current intent state — if the intent expired or mutated meanwhile, the decision is rejected; offline gestures never become authority (INV-A5), and pause-vs-failure rendering stays correct (INV-S2).

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

• CH-2 — Approval expiry mid-review. Setup: the operator is reading the approval card when intent_expiry passes. Fault: the grant arrives one second after expiry. Expected: the grant is rejected, the work item transitions to in_progress per T10, and a fresh intent may be issued — the operator is told the intent lapsed, not that the action executed (INV-A4). • CH-3 — Intent change after card render. Setup: the approval card is rendered from intent revision N. Fault: the intent is revised to N+1 (target corrected) before the operator taps. Expected: the tap carries digest(N); the server recomputes against N+1, the digests differ, the decision is rejected, and the new card is presented (INVA3). • CH-4 — Duplicate tap. Setup: consequential approval card; the first tap's confirmation is delayed. Fault: the operator taps twice; the network duplicates one tap, delivering three grants. Expected: one recorded decision, one execution at most; the second and third grants return the recorded outcome (INV-A6). • CH-5 — Backend change before client refresh. Setup: the client holds a cached operational rendering. Fault: the backend transitions the service to failed (or the artifact is revised) before the client refreshes. Expected: the client's next read carries a revision token; the server rejects the stale view, the client renders the authoritative state, and no optimistic override survives (INV-C4, INV-S4, INV-E2). • CH-6 — Router backend down. Setup: the external router backend is the active policy. Fault: the backend becomes unreachable mid-session. Expected: routing falls back to local policy with the fallback logged; no work item is routed without a policy, and no recommendation crosses the consequence boundary (INV-R1, INV-R5). • CH-7 — Stale live source. Setup: a work item depends on a live-source reading. Fault: the source stops updating; readings age past fresh_until during verification. Expected: the renderer downgrades to unknown or reduced confidence, source-dependent guards fail closed, and the packet marks the observation stale rather than omitting it (INV-S1, INV-S4, INV-E3, INV-E5). • CH-8 — Subagent completion without evidence. Setup: a delegated sub-task nears its end. Fault: the worker emits a completion claim with fluent summary but no artifact digest and no packet. Expected: the claim moves the item to verifying at most; can_enter_completed fails; the orchestrator is escalated with "claim without proof" rather than a completion (INV-C3, INV-C5). • CH-9 — Digest mismatch. Setup: a valid approval flow in progress. Fault: a single bit flips in the intent's target field in transit. Expected: the server's recomputed digest mismatches, the decision is rejected, the attempt is logged as a tamper signal, and no execution occurs (INV-A3 — and a Phase 5 tripwire fires). • CH-10 — Missing tests in evidence packet. Setup: a work item with three acceptance tests. Fault: the packet arrives with results for two tests and the third section absent. Expected: the completion writer rejects the transition; the packet is returned for completion, not accepted as complete — missing tests are a guard failure, not a documentation gap (INV-C2, INV-E4).

8.3 The Traceability Matrix The audit trail from claim to evidence: each invariant → the doctrine it defends → the test layer(s) → the test names. A doctoral committee follows this table the way an auditor follows a ledger. Invariant

Doctrine source

Layer

Tests

8.4 Phase 2 Exit Criteria • [ ] Every major doctrine statement has a testable invariant. The catalogue above is complete against the roadmap's 27 IDs; any doctrine sentence without an invariant is listed as a gap, not assumed covered. • [ ] All tests automated. Layers 1–3 run in CI; property tests archive their seeds; chaos scenarios run against a staging estate on a schedule, not by hand. • [ ] Mutated/expired approval cannot pass. PT-1, PT-2, CH-2, CH-3, and CH-9 demonstrate rejection; the rejection logs are archived as evidence, not the test names as promises. • [ ] Optimistic completion fails tests. PT-4 and CH-8 demonstrate that fluent claims without evidence cannot reach completed; the traces are filed. • [ ] Suite citable as empirical validation. The traceability matrix (§8.3) is complete and current: every invariant resolves to doctrine, code or contract, and at least one executable test. This is the artefact that moves the programme's empirical-validation score off 6.0. • [ ] Suite runnable by third parties. The invariant suite, its fixtures, and the traceability matrix are publishable as-is: a stranger can clone, run, and watch the suite reject the same violations — open verification, not witnessed verification.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

When this checklist is green, the estate does not merely promise its doctrine — it demonstrates it, on every commit, under injected faults, with the failures filed alongside the passes. Phase 3 then takes the proven machine across devices.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Phase 3 — Consistency Model: One Estate, Five Observers Paper IV, §9. Companion to the Phase 3 specification in the J.A.R.V.I.S. remediation roadmap.

9.1 The Problem: Five Surfaces, One Estate Papers I through III describe an estate observed through five device surfaces: the iPhone as command surface, the Apple Watch as a five-second wrist gate, the iPad as review desk, the Apple TV as ambient wall, and — later — Vision Pro as spatial estate (Paper III). Each surface renders estate state. Each surface accepts input. Each surface caches. The question this phase answers is therefore structural, not cosmetic: when five surfaces observe one estate, what prevents them from becoming five sources of truth? Without a stated consistency contract, the failure modes are predictable and have all been observed in consumer software: 1. Divergent approval. Two devices render the same approval card; the operator (HgX) taps both; the consequence executes twice, or — worse — each device believes a different consequence was authorized. 2. Stale authority. A device renders state from a morning cache; the operator acts on it at noon; the action was valid at render time and invalid at action time. 3. Optimistic fiction. A client renders its own tap as success before the server confirms, and the operator walks away believing a consequence occurred that never did. 4. Silent divergence. Two devices show different statuses for the same work item, and there is no rule for which one the operator should believe. The consistency model below exists to make each of these impossible by construction rather than by discipline. Discipline is what the estate has today (Paper I is honest about this); this phase converts it into mechanism.

9.2 The Hybrid Consistency Model No single consistency level fits all estate operations. Status viewing can tolerate staleness; approval decisions cannot. The model is therefore hybrid — the guarantee follows the operation class, not the device: Operation Class

Guarantee

Status viewing

Eventual consistency with freshness stamps

Evidence viewing

Revision-stable reads

Approval decision

Strong authoritative consistency

Consequential execution

Linearized, single-writer execution

Memory correction

Orchestrator-mediated reconciliation

Live-source facts

Read-current at decision time

Artifact download

Digest-verified bytes

Notification delivery

At-least-once hints, never authoritative state

Each row is defended below in terms of what the guarantee permits and, more importantly, what it forbids.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Status viewing — eventual consistency with freshness stamps. A Watchtower status rendered on the television may lag the orchestrator by minutes; nothing consequential follows from a status render, so the cost of staleness is confusion, not harm. The freshness stamp (last_observed_at, fresh_until) bounds the confusion: a stale render must say it is stale, or degrade to unknown (INV-S4). The stamp is the mechanism; "eventual" without the stamp would be a license to mislead. Evidence viewing — revision-stable reads. An evidence packet is immutable once created (§9.7); a read of evidence must return the packet the operator asked for, byte-identical, regardless of what newer revisions exist. This is stronger than eventual consistency and weaker than linearizability: the guarantee is stability of the addressed revision, enforced by content digests. It forbids the quiet substitution of a revised packet under an old reference — the exact failure that would let a doctored artifact inherit a prior approval. Approval decision — strong authoritative consistency. There is exactly one authority that can record an approval decision: the orchestrator (or its authorized execution layer). A client's tap is a proposal until the server validates and records it (§9.6). No local quorum, no offline accumulation, no "the button said approved." The guarantee forbids every path by which two devices could hold two different beliefs about whether consent was given. Consequential execution — linearized, single-writer execution. Execution of an approved intent proceeds through one writer, in one order, with each step's precondition checked against current authoritative state. Two executions of the same intent cannot interleave; the second attempt finds the first's record and returns it (§10). This forbids the race in which a retry and a tap both reach the same external system. Memory correction — orchestrator-mediated reconciliation. Durable memory is the operator's long-term record; two surfaces could hold contradictory corrections. Corrections are therefore not writes — they are requests the orchestrator reconciles against provenance (§9.7). This forbids silent contradiction in the record the estate reasons from. Live-source facts — read-current at decision time. Wherever a decision depends on the world as it is now (a price, a schedule, a balance), the fact must be re-read from the live source at the moment of decision, not taken from cache. Memory may say X and the live source may say Y; for current facts, Y wins (§9.7). This forbids decisions made on cached reality — the precise failure behind the stale-state manipulation threat in §11.5. Artifact download — digest-verified bytes. A download is not complete when the bytes arrive; it is complete when the digest matches the manifest. This forbids truncated or substituted artifacts from entering the estate under a trusted name. Notification delivery — at-least-once hints, never authoritative state. Notifications may be duplicated or delayed; they are permitted to be, because they carry no authority. A notification says "something may have changed — look"; it never says "this is the state." This forbids the failure in which a delayed push and a current render disagree and the operator trusts the push.

The Core Principle, Defended Status may be eventually consistent; consequence must be strongly consistent. The first half exists because strong consistency is expensive — in latency, in complexity, and in the operator's attention. Forcing every status render through a synchronous authoritative read would make the ambient wall flicker, the Watch lag, and the estate feel broken. Eventual consistency with honest freshness stamps is the correct price: the operator sees slightly old state labeled as such, and the label is itself a guarantee (INV-S4). The second half exists because the cost of inconsistency at the consequence boundary is not confusion but unauthorized effect: money moved, messages sent, artifacts published. These are the actions the doctrine chain exists to govern (Paper I: Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine), and the chain's Approval link is meaningless if two devices can disagree about whether approval happened. Strong consistency at the consequence boundary is therefore not an optimization — it is the load-bearing wall of the entire governance model.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

What breaks if the principle is inverted? Invert it — make status strongly consistent and consequence eventually consistent — and the estate gets the worst of both: a sluggish ambient wall that still occasionally double-publishes, double-sends, or approves from a stale intent. The inversion is not merely suboptimal; it concentrates expense where harm is impossible and removes protection where harm is certain. Any implementation found to have drifted toward the inversion has failed Phase 3 regardless of its test results.

9.3 Single Authoritative Writer The roadmap names the orchestrator (or the authorized execution layer) as the single authoritative writer. Concretely, the orchestrator owns: • the canonical work-item record and its revision counter (§9.4); • the approval decision log — which intents were granted, denied, or expired, and by whom; • the execution record — which approved intents have executed, and with what observed result (§10); • the reconciliation of memory corrections; • the issuance and validation of revision tokens (§9.5). Clients — all five surfaces — are deliberately and permanently weaker. What clients may do: • submit briefs (requests for work) to the orchestrator; • request evidence packets and artifacts by revision; • render status from cached or fetched state, with freshness stamps; • present approval cards rendered from server-issued hints; • transmit approval decisions (proposals) with full revision tokens; • open artifacts for inspection. What clients may not do — the prohibitions are the actual content of this section: • approve locally without server validation; • infer completion from a tap, a notification, or a subagent's claim (INV-C3, INV-C4); • mutate evidence packets or their references; • reinterpret an approval intent (narrowing, widening, or retargeting it); • bypass the approval boundary for any consequential action; • become a second orchestrator — no client may accept briefs from another client, coordinate subagents, or maintain its own authoritative work-item records. The last prohibition deserves emphasis because it is the one most likely to be violated by convenience. A future Vision Pro spatial client will be powerful enough to feel like an orchestrator; the contract must still hold. Capability is not authority.

9.4 Event-Sourced Work State Every work item carries the following record: Field

Meaning

work_id

Stable identity of the work item across its lifetime

revision

Monotonic counter; increments on every meaningful transition

state

Current canonical state (from the Phase 1 state machine)

history

Append-only list of transition events, each with timestamp and actor

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Field

Meaning

evidence_refs

References (with digests) to evidence packets attached to this work

approval_refs

References to approval intents and their recorded decisions

last_observed_at

When the authoritative record was last written

fresh_until

Bound after which readers must re-fetch before acting

Transitions append events; they never rewrite history. The record is the log, and the current state is a fold over the log — which is what makes "stale data cannot masquerade as current" (§9.9) a structural property rather than a hope. Worked example. A brief arrives: "prepare the day-plan briefing." The record evolves: work_id:

wi-2026-10-08-041

revision:

1

state:

received

history: - { event: brief_received, at: 08:00:11Z, actor: tickler-bot, note: "brief accepted from scheduled routine" }

The orchestrator accepts scope; revision 2 appends brief_accepted: revision:

2

state:

accepted

history: - { event: brief_received,

at: 08:00:11Z, actor: tickler-bot }

- { event: brief_accepted,

at: 08:00:12Z, actor: orchestrator,

note: "scope bounded: calendar + inbox + priorities" }

A bounded plan exists; revision 3 appends plan_created and the state moves to in_progress: revision:

3

state:

in_progress

history: - { event: brief_received,

at: 08:00:11Z, actor: tickler-bot }

- { event: brief_accepted,

at: 08:00:12Z, actor: orchestrator }

- { event: plan_created,

at: 08:00:19Z, actor: orchestrator,

note: "3 sources, 7 items, 1 approval expected" } evidence_refs:

[]

approval_refs:

[]

last_observed_at: 08:00:19Z fresh_until:

08:05:19Z

Three properties to note. First, nothing was overwritten: a reader at revision 1 sees exactly what revision 1 saw. Second, fresh_until bounds how long any client may treat this snapshot as actionable — after 08:05:19Z, a client holding revision 3 must re-fetch before submitting any mutation. Third, the history is the audit trail the evidence packet will later cite; Phase 7's doctoral evidence package is, in part, a corpus of such histories.

9.5 Revision Tokens: The Stale-Mutation Rejection Rule Every client request that could change authoritative state carries a revision token:

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Field

Purpose

work_id

Which work item the request addresses

revision

The revision the client believes is current

intent_id

Which approval intent the request acts under (for approvals)

intent_digest

Digest binding the exact action, target, effect, scope, and expiry

device_session_id

Which device session issued the request (spoofing control, §11.4)

timestamp

Client-observed time of the request (clock-skew input, not authority)

The rejection rule. The server compares the presented revision against the authoritative revision: • presented == current → the request is fresh; validate the remaining fields and apply. • presented < current → the request is stale; reject with stale_revision. The client must re-fetch authoritative state and, if the operator still wishes to act, present the new intent. The mutation is never applied, never queued, never "applied when convenient." • presented > current → the request is impossible; reject and flag. A client cannot be ahead of the single writer; the condition indicates a corrupt cache, a replayed token, or a forged request, and it is logged as a security event (§11.4). Worked conflict example. The iPad renders an approval card for work item wi-2026-10-08-041 at revision 4 (intent: publish the white-paper PDF to Dropbox). The operator sets the iPad down and picks up the iPhone, where a newer brief advances the same work item to revision 5 (the artifact was regenerated; the old intent's digest no longer matches). The operator returns to the iPad and taps "Approve." The iPad transmits {work_id: wi-2026-10-08-041, revision: 4, intent_id: ai-9917, intent_digest: 9f2c…}. The server holds revision 5. Presented (4) < current (5) → reject stale_revision. The iPad discards its card, fetches revision 5, and discovers the artifact changed after the card was rendered. Per §9.7, the old approval intent is invalid; a new intent is presented; the operator reviews the new artifact. The thirty seconds of re-reading are the entire price of preventing an approval from attaching to an artifact the operator never saw.

9.6 The Approval Flow The approval flow is the consequence boundary made executable. Seven steps, in order, with no shortcuts: 1. Client renders the card from a server-issued hint. The card displays the exact action, target, effect, scope, expiry, and digest. The card is a view of a pending intent, not the intent itself. 2. The operator taps. The tap is a local gesture. It authorizes nothing. The client must not render any success state at this point — rendering "Approved ✓" before step 6 is optimistic fiction (INV-C4) and a Phase 3 failure. 3. The client transmits the decision with intent_id, intent_digest, and the full revision token (§9.5). 4. The server validates: the intent exists; it has not expired; the digest matches the current intent (unchanged target, effect, scope); the action is permissible for the requesting session; the session is authorized and devicebound. 5. The server records the decision exactly once. The decision record is idempotent: a retransmitted decision returns the existing record, never a second one (§10.5). 6. The server returns the authoritative result. Only now does a decision exist in the estate. 7. The client renders the authoritative result. What the operator sees after step 7 is truth; what the operator saw before it was a proposal. Three failure branches, each with its scenario:

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Branch A — intent changed. If the digest presented does not match the current intent (target, effect, or scope changed after the card rendered), the server rejects and the client presents the new intent. Scenario: the white-paper PDF is regenerated with a corrected figure between card render and tap. The tap's digest references the old bytes. The server rejects; the card re-renders against the new digest; the operator approves — or doesn't — the artifact as it actually is. No approval ever attaches to unseen bytes. Branch B — intent expired. If the intent's expiry has passed, the client disables the control and refreshes. Scenario: an approval to post the hourly campaign meme sits open on the iPad while the operator takes a call; the intent expires. The button greys with "this request expired — a fresh intent has been prepared." The operator reviews the fresh intent, which re-binds the current artifact digest. Expiry is not an error; it is the mechanism by which "approval at the last safe moment" (Paper I) is enforced. Branch C — offline. If the device has no path to the server, there is no local approval — only a pending gesture. Scenario: the operator taps "Approve" on the Watch in a tunnel. The Watch stores "the operator wishes to review intent ai-9917" — not consent, and labeled as such. On reconnect, the client fetches the current intent and presents it fresh; the operator confirms again. An offline tap can never become an approval, because the tap was never validated against the intent as it stands at decision time. This is the direct implementation of INV-A5.

9.7 Conflict Resolution Conflict

Resolution

Rationale

Two devices show different status

Server state wins

Only the single writer's record is authoritative; clients hold views, not votes.

Two devices attempt approval

First valid decision recorded wins

Validation is atomic at the server; the loser's tap returns the winner's record (§10.5). Determinism beats fairness here — the operator sees one outcome.

Client cache says operational, source expired

Render unknown / reduced confidence

A freshness stamp that has lapsed is a fact about ignorance, and ignorance must be visible (INV-S1, INV-S4).

Memory says X, live source says Y

Live source wins for current facts

Memory is the durable record of what was true; the live source is what is true. Conflating them is how stale-state manipulation succeeds (§11.5).

Durable memory contradiction

Orchestrator reconciles

Contradictions in the long-term record need provenance judgment, not last-write-wins; only the orchestrator has the full history.

Notification arrives after state changed

Notification is a hint, not truth

Hints carry no sequence authority; the client re-reads before acting. A late hint that contradicts current state is discarded, not reconciled.

Evidence packet updated

New revision; old immutable

Evidence is append-only. "Updating" a packet creates a successor revision; the original remains addressable forever, so no approval's basis can be retroactively altered.

Artifact changed after approval

Approval invalid; new approval required

The digest binds approval to exact bytes (INV-A2, INV-A3). New bytes, new intent, new decision. No exceptions, including "trivial" changes.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

9.8 Per-Device Guarantee Matrix Device

Read behaviour

Approval behaviour

Failure behaviour

Cache policy

Explicit unknown states; retry with fresh token, never replay of stale token

Short-TTL status cache; no caching of intents past

Status only; minimal payload

Confirm-or-defer on pre-validated intents; offline → pending gesture only

Silent failure forbidden: every tap gets a visible outcome or a visible deferral
