iPad (review desk)

Revision-stable evidence reads; long sessions

Extended review; expiry-aware (Branch B common here)

Stale-revision rejection surfaces as "refreshed — please re-read"

Longer-TTL evidence cache keyed by digest; status re-fetched per session

TV (ambient wall)

Status renders only

No approvals, ever

Degrades to ambient "unavailable" rather than alarming

Aggressive cache; freshness stamp always visible; never actionable

Vision Pro (spatial)

Full reads; immersive evidence review

Full approval flow when device ships; provisional until then

Same as iPhone once live

Same as iPad once live; provisional

fresh_until

iPhone. The command surface carries the highest attention budget and the highest privilege: it is where briefs are issued, approvals granted, and consequences reviewed. Its privacy ceiling is the operator's own — full content, gated by device-local authentication for high-risk approvals (the approval-fatigue control in §11.5). Because it is the mostused surface, it is also the most likely to hold a stale token; hence the short TTL and the hard rule that intents are never cached past fresh_until. Watch. The wrist gate operates on a five-second attention budget (Paper III): a buzz means "delay changes the outcome," and the response is confirm or defer — never compose, never review at length. Its privacy ceiling is deliberately low: minimal payloads, no sensitive content in notifications (notification redaction, §11.4), because a wrist is glanced at in public. Offline taps degrade to pending gestures (Branch C), and the design forbids the worst Watch failure — a tap that silently does nothing, leaving the operator believing a consequence was authorized. iPad. The review desk is where long-form evidence is actually read: evidence packets, artifact diffs, approval histories. Its attention budget is measured in minutes, which makes it the natural home of Branch B — intents expiring mid-review. Its cache policy reflects its role: evidence cached by digest (safe, because digests are immutable addresses), status always re-fetched. The iPad is the surface where "stale data cannot masquerade as current" is most visibly tested, because it is where the operator spends the most time looking at almost-current things. TV. The ambient wall is a shared surface in a shared room, and its contract is the strictest in the estate: states, never contents (Paper III). It renders "3 routines operational, 1 paused" — never the content of an email, a health metric, or an approval's target. It approves nothing, it acts on nothing, and its failure mode is graceful dimming to "unavailable" rather than any alarm that would leak information to a room. Its cache is aggressive because its reads are the least consequential; its freshness stamp is always visible because its reads are the stalest. Vision Pro. The spatial estate does not yet exist in the operator's hands; its row in the matrix is therefore provisional, marked as such, and bound by the same contract the day it ships: full reads, full approval flow, iPad-class caching. The point of stating it now is that the contract precedes the device — no surface joins the estate without first accepting the single-writer rule (§9.3).

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

9.9 Phase 3 Exit Criteria • [ ] No device can become a source of truth: every client mutation path requires server validation, verified by fault-injection tests. • [ ] Approval conflicts resolve deterministically: two-device approval races always produce exactly one recorded decision, under test. • [ ] Stale data cannot masquerade as current: every client render path carries a freshness stamp or degrades to unknown. • [ ] Offline taps cannot become approvals: offline gestures are stored as intent-to-review and re-validated on reconnect; no test path converts a gesture into consent. • [ ] Behaviour verified under clock skew (device timestamps ±5 minutes) and network loss (partition during each of the 7 approval steps). • [ ] Consistency contract published as a specification: the guarantee matrix, the single-writer rule, and the conflict-resolution table are written for external implementers, not only for the estate's own clients.

Open Questions 1. Quantitative freshness bounds. The model requires fresh_until on every record but does not set the bound per operation class. What is the right TTL for Watchtower status on the TV versus an approval intent on the iPhone? This needs measurement, not assertion. 2. Hint sequencing. Should notification hints carry monotonic sequence numbers so clients can detect lost hints, not merely late ones? The current model treats hints as unordered; loss detection would strengthen the "hint, not truth" guarantee at the cost of client complexity. 3. Clock-skew tolerance. The exit criteria propose ±5 minutes, but the revision token's timestamp is advisory. Should skew beyond tolerance invalidate a session, or merely annotate the record? The security implications are explored in §11.4; the consistency contract should state the rule. 4. Memory-correction revision domain. Should memory corrections share the work-item revision counter, or carry their own revision domain with its own tokens? The roadmap places reconciliation with the orchestrator; the token mechanics are unspecified. 5. Vision Pro provisionality. The matrix row for Vision Pro is a promise, not a test result. Phase 3 cannot be fully closed for that surface until the device exists in the estate; the exit criteria should record the surface as explicitly out of scope until then.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Phase 4 — Idempotency Guarantees: Consequence, Exactly Once or Not At All Paper IV, §10. Companion to the Phase 4 specification in the J.A.R.V.I.S. remediation roadmap. Builds on the singlewriter rule and revision tokens of §9; its execution records become the evidence the security model of §11 reasons about.

10.1 The Core Principle Consequential actions must be at-most-once unless the system has observed that they did not occur or are safely repeatable. This principle is deliberately unfashionable. The dominant orthodoxy in distributed messaging is at-least-once delivery with idempotent consumers: send the message as many times as it takes, and let the receiver deduplicate. That orthodoxy is correct for its domain — moving bytes between systems that can absorb duplicates. It is wrong for the estate's domain, which is causing effects in the world: publishing, sending, purchasing, deleting, transferring. A duplicate message is a nuisance; a duplicate consequence is a harm the operator cannot un-experience. The estate therefore inverts the default: the consequence layer is at-most-once, guarded by idempotency keys, and retries are permitted only where the system has observed non-occurrence or proven safe repeatability. The two defaults are not rivals; they govern different layers. At-least-once is appropriate for notification hints (§9.2), evidence fetches, log replication, and status polling — anywhere a duplicate is harmless and a loss is costly. At-mostonce is appropriate for the execution of approved intents — anywhere a duplicate is harmful and the system can afford to stop, verify, and ask. The engineering error this phase prevents is letting the messaging layer's default leak into the consequence layer: a retry policy written for "deliver this packet" applied silently to "send this email."

10.2 Operation Classes and Their Retry Policies Class

Examples

Retry Policy

Read

Fetch calendar, price, document, status

Freely repeatable

Prepare

Draft, stage, calculate, reconcile

Repeatable if reversible

Approve

The operator grants an exact intent

Record once; duplicate returns same decision

Execute

Send, publish, purchase, delete, transfer

At-most-once unless verified safe

Verify

Observe result after execution

Freely repeatable

Compensate

Restore, revoke, undo if possible

Only if supported and approved

Memory write

Update durable memory

Reconcile; do not duplicate contradictions

Read — freely repeatable. A read changes nothing, so repeating it changes nothing. Estate example: fetching the day's calendar to build the morning briefing. The only discipline reads require is the freshness one from §9: a read used at decision time must be read-current, but the act of reading itself needs no guard. Prepare — repeatable if reversible. Preparation produces drafts, calculations, and staged artifacts — all of which can be discarded. Estate example: drafting the tax summary for the Tax Bot's return work. The draft can be regenerated any number of times; what it must never do is leak into execution. The boundary between Prepare and Execute is therefore the most audited boundary in the estate: preparation needs no approval precisely because it is defined as having no external effect, and anything with an external effect is, by definition, not preparation.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Approve — record once; duplicate returns same decision. Approval is the one operation class where a duplicate must return the same answer, not merely avoid a second effect. Estate example: the operator taps "Approve" on the white-paper publish intent; the tap retransmits (network stutter); the server finds the existing decision record and returns it. The operator sees one decision, one timestamp, one digest. (See §10.5 for the full duplicate-tap handling.) Execute — at-most-once unless verified safe. The guarded class. Estate example: posting the campaign meme to X. The post either happened or it didn't; if the network dropped mid-post, the system does not assume — it verifies (§10.6) and retries only when verification shows non-occurrence or the operation is proven repeatable. Verify — freely repeatable. Verification is observation, and observation is safe. Estate example: listing the Dropbox folder after an upload and comparing digests. A system that makes verification expensive or rate-limited will be tempted to skip it; the retry policy therefore states explicitly that verification is never the step to economize. Compensate — only if supported and approved. Undoing is itself a consequential action. Estate example: retracting a scheduled routine that was published in error. Compensation requires its own approval when consequential (a retraction notice is a new public act), and it is only attempted where the external system supports it. There is no silent rollback of consequential effects — the saga rules in §10.7 make this explicit. Memory write — reconcile, do not duplicate contradictions. Memory is the estate's long-term record; writing the same fact twice must not create two facts, and writing a contradictory fact must not silently overwrite. Estate example: recording the operator's corrected preference after a memory-correction request. The orchestrator reconciles against provenance (§9.7); the write is idempotent with respect to content, and contradictions surface for judgment rather than being resolved by recency.

10.3 The Idempotency Key Schema The idempotency key names the consequence, not the gesture: key = hash(work_id, intent_id, intent_digest, action, target, scope, revision, operation_class)

Hash inputs, and why each is present. work_id scopes the key to one unit of work. intent_id and intent_digest bind the key to the exact approved intent — a changed digest (new artifact bytes, new target) is a different consequence and must produce a different key, so that re-approval of a changed intent cannot accidentally dedupe against the old one. action, target, and scope name what will happen and where; revision pins the key to the work-item revision at approval time, so a replayed approval against a later revision cannot silently execute; operation_class separates Execute keys from Compensate keys, so a compensation can never dedupe against the execution it is undoing. What is excluded, and why. Transient UI state and device-local nonces are excluded from the primary key — deliberately. The key must identify the consequence across devices and retries: the two-tap race in §10.5 (iPhone and Watch tapping the same intent within the same second) must resolve to the same key, or the dedupe fails exactly when it is most needed. A device-local nonce would make every tap unique and defeat the mechanism; a UI-state field (which card layout rendered, whether dark mode was on) would make the key fragile to irrelevant change. Device identity is still recorded — in the execution record's attempt log, for audit — but it is not part of the key. The principle: the key answers "what effect is being caused," never "who tapped what, where." Server-side record. For each key, the server stores: Field

Purpose

key

The idempotency key itself

status

Current execution-record state (§10.4)

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Field

Purpose

created_at / updated_at

Key lifecycle timestamps

approval_ref

The recorded approval decision authorizing this key

execution_state

Fine-grained execution state

observed_result

What verification actually saw (digests, IDs, timestamps)

attempt_count

How many execution attempts have been made

last_error

Most recent failure, if any

safe_to_retry

Boolean, set only by verification or policy — never assumed

Worked example. The operator approves publishing the front-end white-paper PDF to Dropbox. Conceptually: work_id:

wi-2026-10-08-112

intent_id:

ai-10442

intent_digest:

sha256(pdf bytes ‖ target=/J.A.R.V.I.S.-Front-End-White-Paper.pdf ‖ scope=dropbox-

write) action:

file_publish

target:

dropbox:/J.A.R.V.I.S.-Front-End-White-Paper.pdf

scope:

dropbox-write

revision:

6

operation_class: execute key = sha256("wi-2026-10-08-112" ‖ "ai-10442" ‖ "9f2c…" ‖ "file_publish" ‖ "dropbox:/J.A.R.V.I.S.-Front-End-White-Paper.pdf" ‖ "dropbox-write" ‖ 6 ‖ "execute") = 7d31…

If the operator's finger double-taps, the second tap carries the same inputs and computes the same key: the server finds 7d31… already execution_observed and returns the existing result. If the PDF is regenerated and re-approved, the digest changes, the key changes, and the new publish is correctly treated as a new consequence. If a compensation (retract the publish) is later approved, its key carries operation_class: compensate and cannot collide with the execution it reverses.

10.4 Execution Record Lifecycle Nine states. Each lists its entry condition (what must be true to enter) and its exit condition (what must be true to leave): 1. prepared — Entry: preparation artifacts exist and are staged; no external effect has occurred. Exit: an approval decision is recorded (→ approved), or the work is abandoned (→ cancelled). 2. approved — Entry: a valid, unexpired, digest-matched approval decision is recorded against the intent. Exit: execution is requested (→ execution_requested), the approval expires or is superseded (→ cancelled), or the operator denies (→ cancelled). 3. execution_requested — Entry: the orchestrator has dispatched the execution with the idempotency key. Exit: the attempt begins (→ execution_started), or dispatch is withdrawn before any external call (→ cancelled). 4. execution_started — Entry: the external call has been issued. Exit: a result is observed (→ execution_observed / execution_failed), or observation becomes impossible (→ execution_unknown). This is the point of no return for retry policy: from here, the system may not assume non-occurrence.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

5. execution_observed — Entry: verification has confirmed the effect occurred (read-after-write, provider receipt, digest match). Exit: acceptance checks pass (→ completed). 6. execution_failed — Entry: verification has confirmed the effect did not occur, or the attempt returned a definitive failure. Exit: a safe retry is dispatched (→ execution_requested, only if safe_to_retry), or the work is abandoned (→ cancelled, surfaced to the operator). 7. execution_unknown — Entry: the attempt was issued but the outcome cannot be established — network loss, provider timeout with no read-back, ambiguous receipt. Exit: external verification later establishes the outcome (→ execution_observed / execution_failed), or the operator decides with full knowledge of the ambiguity (→ completed by explicit acceptance, or cancelled). Never exits silently to completed; never auto-retries while consequential. 8. completed — Entry: acceptance criteria passed with evidence attached (INV-C1). Exit: none — terminal. Corrections create new work items, not resurrections. 9. cancelled — Entry: operator denial, approval expiry, withdrawal before external effect, or explicit abandonment with reason recorded. Exit: none — terminal. A cancelled record remains queryable; cancellation is itself evidence. The lifecycle's shape encodes the doctrine: execution_unknown exists because the honest answer to "did it happen?" is sometimes "we cannot know yet," and a system that cannot represent that answer will invent one.

10.5 Duplicate-Tap Handling The duplicate tap is the most ordinary idempotency event in the estate and therefore the one the mechanism must handle most boringly. The two-tap sequence: Tap 1 (iPhone, 20:14:03.112Z) → client transmits decision + revision token → server validates (intent exists, unexpired, digest matches, session authorized) → server records decision D-8814 exactly once → server creates execution record, key 7d31…, state execution_requested → server returns { decision: D-8814, execution: 7d31…, status: execution_started } → client renders authoritative result Tap 2 (iPhone, 20:14:03.408Z — the double-tap; or Watch, 20:14:03.511Z — the race) → client transmits decision + revision token (same intent_id, same digest) → server computes key 7d31… (identical inputs → identical key, §10.3) → server finds existing record: decision D-8814 already recorded → server returns { decision: D-8814, execution: 7d31…, status: <current>, duplicate: true } → NO second decision record. NO second execution. NO second external call. → client renders the existing authoritative result

The race case. Two devices tap within the same second — the iPhone and the Watch, both presenting valid tokens for the same intent. Both requests reach the server; validation is atomic at the single writer (§9.3). The first valid decision to complete validation wins; the second finds the winner's record and returns it. There is no "tie" state and no operator-visible ambiguity: one decision exists, both devices render it. The loser's tap is not an error — it is a successful read of someone else's decision. This is the same rule as §9.7's approval-conflict row, now grounded in the key mechanism: first valid decision recorded wins is not a policy preference but a consequence of atomic validation plus idempotent recording.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

10.6 Ambiguous Outcome Handling When execution_started is followed by silence — network drop, provider timeout, no read-back — the system follows a decision procedure, stated here as a flowchart in prose: 1. Attempt external verification. Query the external system for the effect: list the folder and compare digests (Dropbox), read back the timeline (X), check the provider's receipt log. Verification is freely repeatable (§10.2); spend it. 2. If verification confirms the effect occurred → transition to execution_observed, attach the observed result, proceed to acceptance. The ambiguity is resolved by evidence, not by assumption. 3. If verification confirms the effect did not occur → transition to execution_failed; consult safe_to_retry. Retry is permitted only where the failure is definitive and the policy allows. 4. If verification is impossible — the provider offers no read-back, the network is down, the receipt is ambiguous — do not mark completed; do not auto-retry if consequential. Transition to execution_unknown. 5. From execution_unknown, escalate with the next safe option. Present the operator with the full picture: what was attempted, what is known, what is unknowable, and the two honest choices — accept the risk of a duplicate (retry explicitly, with the operator's informed consent recorded as a new approval) or accept the risk of non-effect (verify later, or redo the work). The operator decides; the system never decides ambiguity on the operator's behalf. Worked scenario. The operator approves publishing a PDF to Dropbox. The upload call is issued (execution_started); the network drops before the response arrives. What the operator sees: • The approval card does not flip to "Published." It flips to "Upload attempted — outcome unknown. Verifying…" — the execution_unknown state rendered honestly (INV-S1: unknown never renders as operational). • The system retries verification, not the upload: it re-lists the folder and compares digests. If the file is there with the right digest, the record moves to execution_observed and the card flips to "Published — verified 20:16:02Z." • If the folder cannot be reached, the card stays at unknown and the operator is offered the next safe option: "Retry the upload (you accept the small risk of two copies) or wait and verify later." Either choice is recorded; neither is made silently. Note what did not happen: the system did not mark the publish complete on the basis of the attempt, did not retry the upload blindly, and did not leave the operator staring at a spinner that meant nothing. Ambiguity is a first-class state with a first-class rendering.

10.7 The Governed Saga Pattern The estate assumes no distributed transactions — Dropbox, X, the mail provider, and the operator's bank do not participate in two-phase commit, and pretending otherwise would be dishonest engineering. Coordination across systems therefore follows a governed saga: a sequence of steps, each guarded, each evidenced, each reversible only where reversal is real. The six steps: 1. Prepare — stage artifacts, compute digests, bind the intent. No approval needed; no external effect permitted. 2. Approval — the operator grants the exact intent (action, target, effect, scope, expiry, digest). 3. Execute — the single writer issues the external call under the idempotency key. 4. Verify — observe the result through read-after-write or provider receipt. 5. Record evidence — attach the observed result to the evidence packet; only now can acceptance pass.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

6. Compensate — if the outcome requires undoing and undoing is supported: issue the compensating action as its own governed mini-saga. The eight saga rules: 1. Preparation needs no approval — but preparation is defined as having no external effect; anything with an effect is execution wearing a disguise. 2. Approval binds the exact target and effect (INV-A2); a changed target or effect invalidates the approval (INVA3). 3. Execution requires a valid, unexpired, digest-matched approval for consequential actions (INV-A1, INV-A4). 4. Execution must be idempotent (keyed) or verified (observed); "fire and hope" is not a step. 5. Failed verification leads to unknown or failed — never to silent completion, never to silent retry. 6. Compensation needs its own approval if consequential — undoing a publication is itself a publication-shaped act. 7. No silent rollback of consequential effects — if the world was changed, the record says so, permanently; compensation is a new act, not an erasure. 8. No assumed distributed transactions — each step's guarantee is stated for what the external system actually provides (§10.8), and the saga is designed for the weakest participant, not the strongest. Why classical distributed transactions are not assumed. Two-phase commit requires participants that can promise, prepare, and roll back on command. The estate's external systems offer none of these uniformly: some offer idempotency keys, some offer read-after-write, some offer nothing but a 200 OK of uncertain meaning. A saga designed for the weakest participant is not a compromise — it is the only design whose guarantees survive contact with the actual systems. The formalism's honesty about this is what makes the guarantees trustworthy: every step's precondition names what the external system actually provides, and the saga never assumes more.

10.8 External Integration Classification Integration Class

Handling

Estate Example

Supports idempotency key

Use native idempotency; map the estate key to the provider's token

Notification dispatch — the provider accepts a client dedupe token; the estate key (§10.3) is passed through, so a retried dispatch cannot double-notify

Supports read-afterwrite verification

Execute, then verify before acceptance

Dropbox upload — write the file, then list the folder and compare digests; acceptance passes only on digest match

No idempotency, no verification

High-risk; strongest approval or manual execution

Email send — the provider offers fire-and-forget with no reliable read-back; the estate requires its strongest approval class here, and the operator is told explicitly that verification is unavailable

Reversible action

Prefer reversible preparation; execute with normal guards

Calendar write — an event can be deleted or rescheduled; the saga may execute with lighter verification because compensation (delete) is real

Irreversible action

Exact approval and evidence; no compensation story

Credential use — a signing or authorization act, once performed, cannot be un-performed; the approval must be exact and the evidence packet complete, because there is no undo to fall back on

Financial transfer

Strongest verification, conservative retry

Proline/betting record — any money movement: verify through every available channel, retry only on definitive non-occurrence, and surface every ambiguity to the operator before proceeding

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Integration Class

Handling

Estate Example

Publication

Digest-bound artifact and expiry

X post — the artifact digest is bound to the post intent and the intent carries an expiry (the hourly campaign window); a post approved for one window cannot execute in the next

Deletion

Recoverable change preferred; require a recovery path

File delete — move to trash, never rm; the recovery path (restore within N days) is named in the approval intent, and permanent deletion is a separate, separately approved act

The classification is not a suggestion — it is assigned at integration time, stored with the connector's contract, and enforced by the saga: an integration classified "no idempotency, no verification" cannot be executed under a routine's standing approval, no matter how often the routine has run. The class follows the system, not the operator's familiarity with it.

10.9 Phase 4 Exit Criteria • [ ] Duplicate taps cannot duplicate effects: double-tap and cross-device race tests produce exactly one decision record and one execution per key. • [ ] Network loss cannot silently complete: partition during execution_started always lands in execution_unknown, never in completed. • [ ] Ambiguous consequential actions become visible unknown/failed: every ambiguous path has a tested operator-facing rendering and an escalation with a next safe option. • [ ] Retries only when safe: no test path retries a consequential execution without definitive non-occurrence or a proven-safe classification. • [ ] At-most-once demonstrable under test: the idempotency key schema, execution record lifecycle, and saga rules hold under chaos injection (duplicate delivery, reordered events, dropped responses). • [ ] Idempotency guarantees safe for open implementation: the key schema, execution record lifecycle, and saga rules are specified completely enough that a third-party client can implement at-most-once consequence without access to the estate's internals.

Open Questions 1. Dedupe window duration. How long does the server retain execution records for duplicate detection — days, months, indefinitely? Storage is cheap; the question is whether indefinite retention creates a privacy or correctness liability (a year-old key colliding with a genuinely new but identical action). 2. Safe-to-retry provenance. The safe_to_retry flag is set "only by verification or policy — never assumed." Which policies may set it, and who approves a policy change? A permissive policy here silently reintroduces atleast-once semantics. 3. Cross-intent dedupe. Two different intents could name the same consequence (the operator approves the same publish twice, an hour apart). The keys differ (different intent_id), so both execute. Is that correct, or should the estate warn on semantic duplicates? The current model treats them as distinct acts of will; the alternative is a similarity check the operator did not ask for. 4. execution_unknown aging. An unknown that is never resolved is a permanent open loop. What is the escalation schedule — hours, days? — and at what point does a permanently unknown consequential action become a standing agenda item rather than a pending state? 5. Provider key-mapping audit. Where the estate maps its key to a provider's native idempotency token, a mapping bug silently disables dedupe. What test proves the mapping is live, not merely configured?

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Phase 5 — Security Boundary Formalism: Naming What Can Go Wrong Paper IV, §11. Companion to the Phase 5 specification in the J.A.R.V.I.S. remediation roadmap. The consistency guarantees of §9 and the idempotency guarantees of §10 are treated here as security controls — which is what they are — and the analysis is honest throughout about the estate's current isolation level: procedural, not cryptographic.

11.1 Asset Register Ten assets. Each is listed with why it matters and what its compromise looks like — because a threat model that cannot describe the damage is a vocabulary exercise. 1. The operator's attention. The scarcest resource in the estate and the one every routine is designed to conserve (Paper I: silent-by-default; Paper III: wrist buzz only when delay changes outcome). Compromise looks like: attention fatigue — a flood of low-value approvals, notifications, or escalations that trains the operator to tap without reading, at which point every approval control in §9.6 becomes theatre. 2. Approval authority. The operator's exclusive right to authorize consequence — the single human root of trust in a single-operator design. Compromise looks like: a forged, replayed, or mis-bound approval causing an effect the operator never willed. This is the asset the entire Phase 3 apparatus exists to protect. 3. Credentials. API tokens, session material, and the Secure Vault contents that let the estate act as the operator in external systems. Compromise looks like: exfiltration or misuse — a token leaving the estate, or a legitimate token used for an illegitimate purpose. Credentials are the asset whose compromise converts every other control into a suggestion. 4. Personal data. Health, finance, legal, communications, betting, and identity records — the substance of the operator's life that the estate routinely handles. Compromise looks like: disclosure to the wrong surface (a health metric on the shared TV), the wrong party (a prompt-injection exfiltration), or the wrong retention (indefinite logs of what should have been ephemeral). 5. Artifacts. The inspectable outputs of work: PDFs, briefings, memes, ledgers. Compromise looks like: silent substitution — an artifact whose bytes changed after approval, inheriting authority it no longer deserves (the Branch A failure of §9.6, weaponized). 6. Evidence packets. The proof bundles that justify completed work and, in Phase 7, the doctoral claim itself. Compromise looks like: fabricated or edited evidence — a packet claiming verification that never happened, which would let optimistic completion (§4 of the roadmap) pass as diligence. 7. Logs. The append-only record of what the estate did: router decisions, approval histories, work-item events. Compromise looks like: tampering that erases the trail — the one attack that makes every other attack uninvestigable. Logs are the asset whose compromise is measured not in what was stolen but in what can no longer be proven. 8. Connectors. The authenticated channels to external systems: Dropbox, mail, calendar, X, health sync. Compromise looks like: a connector used beyond its scope — the calendar connector issuing deletions under a routine approved only for reads, or a compromised connector becoming a channel for injected instructions. 9. Runtime. The machine, processes, and filesystem the estate executes on. Compromise looks like: code or configuration changed outside the governed path — a modified skill, a replaced binary, a cron entry nobody approved. At Level 0 isolation (§11.6), this asset is protected by discipline and audit, not by mechanism, and the model says so plainly. 10. Router state. The usage router's policies, logs, and learned preferences (§12). Compromise looks like: policy manipulation — a router that has been taught, by poisoned feedback, to prefer the expensive path, the unsafe

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

path, or the path that hides failures. Because the router influences every routing decision, its compromise is a force multiplier for every other threat.

11.2 Trust Boundaries Ten boundaries. For each: what crosses it, and in which direction authority flows. Authority flows in one direction only — outward from the operator — and every boundary description is checked against that rule. 1. Operator devices ↔ estate edge. Crosses inward: briefs, approval decisions, corrections. Crosses outward: status hints, approval cards, evidence, artifacts. Authority flows inward from the operator. Nothing the estate edge originates — no suggestion, no draft, no "helpful" pre-approval — carries authority outward-to-inward. 2. Estate edge ↔ orchestrator. Crosses inward: validated briefs and decision proposals. Crosses outward: workitem state, hints, rendered contracts. Authority flows inward, and the edge is untrusted: everything crossing inward is re-validated (§9.5, §9.6 step 4), because the edge includes devices that may be compromised, borrowed, or simply buggy. 3. Orchestrator ↔ tools. Crosses outward: tool invocations with scoped parameters. Crosses inward: tool results, treated as data, never as instructions (the prompt-injection rule, §11.5). Authority flows outward from the orchestrator; tools hold no authority of their own and cannot authorize each other. 4. Orchestrator ↔ subagents. Crosses outward: bounded task briefs with explicit scope. Crosses inward: subagent outputs, treated as verification input, never as completion (no proof, no done). Authority flows outward; the orchestrator remains accountable for every subagent act (§11.5, subagent deception). 5. Orchestrator ↔ connectors. Crosses outward: authenticated API calls within the connector's least-privilege scope. Crosses inward: external state, treated as untrusted until validated against source allowlists. Authority flows outward, bounded by the connector's contract; a connector cannot expand its own scope. 6. Orchestrator ↔ external web. Crosses outward: search queries and fetches. Crosses inward: page text and tool results — untrusted data, never instructions. No authority flows inward across this boundary, ever. This is the single most load-bearing rule in the agent-specific threat model. 7. Orchestrator ↔ Secure Vault. Crosses outward: scoped credential requests. Crosses inward: short-lived credential material, never persisted outside the vault. Authority flows outward in the form of explicit, per-use authorization; the vault never volunteers credentials, and no credential value ever enters logs, prompts, or evidence packets. 8. Orchestrator ↔ external router backend. Crosses outward: routing queries (task features, never personal data beyond what the task requires). Crosses inward: a recommendation — a path suggestion with a confidence, carrying no authority. No authority flows inward. The router recommends; it never approves, never executes, never overrides (INV-R1; §12.6). 9. Client ↔ notification system. Crosses outward: redacted hints. Crosses inward: delivery receipts only. No authority flows in either direction across this boundary — notifications are hints (§9.2), and the notification system is never permitted to carry approval payloads, credential material, or personal data beyond the redaction policy. 10. Personal surfaces ↔ shared surfaces. Crosses outward: state summaries only — "3 routines operational, 1 paused." Nothing carrying content crosses to a shared surface. Authority does not cross this boundary at all; it is a one-way redaction membrane. The TV never receives what the iPhone may display, and no failure mode, fallback, or "helpful" enrichment may widen the aperture (INV-S5). The publication boundary. Beyond the ten operational boundaries sits the publication boundary: what crosses from the estate to the open-source release, and what never does. Crosses outward: doctrine, skills, contracts, schemas, the state machine, the invariant suite, the threat model, the evidence package — the system. Never crosses: family, finances, credentials, personal data, live source contents — the principal's private life. A private value found in the staging tree (~/workspace/jarvis-estate/) is a security defect, tested as such: the sanitisation boundary is en‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

forced by the same discipline as every other boundary in this section, because the open-source vision makes it loadbearing.

11.3 Capability Formalism Governance in the estate is capability-based: no subject acts except through a capability that names exactly what is permitted. A capability is an 8-tuple: (subject, action, object, scope, condition, expiry, approval_requirement, evidence_requirement)

• subject — who or what holds the capability (a named bot, the orchestrator, a routine). • action — the verb: read, draft, publish, delete, send, execute. • object — the noun: which artifact, connector, file, or external system. • scope — the bound: which directories, which accounts, which time windows. • condition — the circumstance: only when fresh, only when the operator is reachable, only within a routine's schedule. • expiry — when the capability lapses, unconditionally. • approval_requirement — what human authorization the action needs: none (read), standing routine approval, or exact per-act approval. • evidence_requirement — what proof must be attached: none, observation log, or full evidence packet. Worked example 1 — Tax Bot preparing a return draft: subject:

tax-bot

action:

draft

object:

2025-joint-return working papers

scope:

~/workspace/tax-bot/drafts/ ; tax documents only ; no external transmission

condition:

only during the active engagement (2026 tax season)

expiry:

2026-10-31

approval_requirement: none for drafting; exact per-act approval for any transmission to H&R Block evidence_requirement: draft diff logged; full evidence packet before any approval is requested

Note the split: drafting needs no approval because the scope forbids external effect — the capability's scope is what makes the approval requirement honest. If the Tax Bot attempted to email the draft, it would exceed its scope, and the act would fail closed regardless of any approval it claimed. Worked example 2 — Watchtower writing a status file: subject:

watchtower

action:

write

object:

status.json, STATUS.md

scope:

~/workspace/watchtower/ only ; status content only ; never personal data

condition:

only on the hourly sweep schedule or on observed state change

expiry:

rolling 25 hours from issuance (re-issued each sweep)

approval_requirement: none — standing routine approval covers scheduled status writes evidence_requirement: observation log (which checks ran, what they saw)

The Watchtower's capability is deliberately narrow: it may write status, not content, and only into its own directory. A Watchtower that attempted to write the betting ledger would be acting outside its capability — and the filesystem discipline of Level 1 (§11.6) is what would catch it, which is why the upgrade path matters. Worked example 3 — the router recommending a path:

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

subject:

usage-router

action:

recommend

object:

execution path for the current brief

scope:

advisory only ; no tool invocation ; no state mutation ; no personal data

egress condition:

only when consulted through the decision gate; shadow mode until Phase 6 exit

expiry:

per-decision (each recommendation is single-use)

approval_requirement: none — but the recommendation itself can never satisfy an approval_requirement elsewhere evidence_requirement: decision logged with inputs, policy version, and (in shadow mode) the path actually taken

The router's capability is the formalism's rendering of INV-R1: the most powerful thing the router may do is suggest, and the capability tuple makes "suggest" structurally incapable of becoming "authorize." A recommendation presented as an approval is a type error, not a policy violation — the system should be unable to express it, not merely forbidden from doing it.

11.4 STRIDE Analysis Each threat below carries three things: a concrete example, the mitigation, and the test that would catch it. A mitigation without a test is a wish; the exit criteria (§11.7) require the test.

Spoofing — pretending to be someone or something trusted Fake approval. Example: a forged approval decision — a replayed approval payload, or a decision record fabricated on a compromised device — presented to the execution layer as the operator's consent. Mitigation: device-bound sessions, intent digests binding the exact action/target/effect, server-side validation of every decision (§9.6 step 4), and idempotent decision recording (§10.5) so a replay returns the original record rather than creating authority. Test: inject a forged decision (wrong session, mismatched digest, replayed payload) at the execution layer; assert rejection and a security log entry in every case. Fake source. Example: a tool result claiming to be the live calendar that is actually a cached page, a mocked response, or an attacker's content — used to justify a decision on false facts. Mitigation: source validation against an allowlist of live sources, freshness stamps on every source read (§9.2), and the read-current-at-decision-time rule for consequential facts. Test: present a decision pipeline with a stale or non-allowlisted source; assert the pipeline refuses to treat it as current and renders unknown instead. Fake completion. Example: a subagent reports "done" with fluent, confident prose and no artifact — or a client renders success from a tap rather than a server result. Mitigation: evidence verification before completion (INV-C1, INV-C3, INV-C5); completion requires observed output, not claims. Test: the delegation-without-proof chaos test — a subagent returns a completion claim with no evidence packet; assert the work item cannot reach completed.

Tampering — modifying data or code in transit or at rest Mutated intent. Example: an approval intent's target is altered between card render and execution — the operator approved publishing draft A, and draft B is what gets published. Mitigation: digest binding of the exact action, target, effect, scope, and expiry (INV-A2); any mutation invalidates the approval (INV-A3); Branch A of §9.6 rejects and represents. Test: mutate one field of an approved intent (target path, effect description) and attempt execution; assert rejection and invalidation of the prior approval. Artifact tampering. Example: the bytes of an approved PDF are altered after approval but before download or publication. Mitigation: content digests in the artifact manifest; digest-verified downloads (§9.2); approval bound to the digest, not the filename. Test: flip one byte in a staged artifact after approval; assert the download verifier rejects and the approval is marked invalid.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Log tampering. Example: an attacker — or a misbehaving routine — edits the router log or approval history to erase a consequential act. Mitigation: append-only logs with a tamper-evident construction (hash-chained entries; the roadmap names this explicitly as the target). Test: modify a historical log entry and run the chain verifier; assert detection. (At Level 0 this construction is specified but not yet enforced — see §11.6 and the honesty note there.) Memory poisoning. Example: a false "preference" is inserted into durable memory — through a crafted correction request or a poisoned subagent output — and the estate thereafter reasons from a lie. Mitigation: provenance on every memory write (who asserted it, from what evidence), orchestrator-mediated reconciliation (§9.7), and contradiction surfacing rather than silent overwrite. Test: inject a contradictory memory write through a subagent's output; assert it is held for reconciliation, not applied, and the operator is shown both claims with provenance.

Repudiation — denying an act that occurred Operator denies approval. Example: a consequential act is disputed — "I never approved that." Mitigation: approval objects carrying the intent digest, timestamp, device session, and the exact approved content; evidence packet requirement for every completed consequential run (INV-E1). The approval object is the estate's answer to repudiation in both directions: it proves what was approved, and its digest proves the approval cannot be retroactively re-described. Test: reconstruct, from stored approval objects alone, the full approval history of a consequential act; assert the reconstruction is complete and digest-consistent. Subagent disowns output. Example: a failure is traced to a subagent's output, and the handoff record is ambiguous about who produced what. Mitigation: one accountable owner per handoff — the orchestrator is accountable for every subagent act (§11.2, boundary 4); subagent outputs are logged as verification inputs with authorship. Test: trace any failed run from evidence packet back through handoffs to the originating brief; assert the chain has no anonymous links.

Information Disclosure — exposing what should be confined Shared-surface leak. Example: a health metric or email content rendered on the TV during a family gathering. Mitigation: the personal/shared redaction membrane (§11.2, boundary 10) — states only, never contents; INV-S5. Test: the shared-surface redaction test — run every render path against a corpus of restricted content; assert no restricted content reaches a shared surface under any failure or fallback condition. Notification overexposure. Example: a lock-screen notification carrying an approval's target details, readable by anyone glancing at the phone. Mitigation: notification redaction policy — hints carry the minimum needed to prompt a look, never the substance (§9.2, §11.2 boundary 9). Test: capture notification payloads across a week of routine operation; assert none contain restricted content classes. Cross-role over-read. Example: the meme bot reading tax documents because the filesystem permits it. Mitigation: cross-role least privilege — each role's capability scope (§11.3) is enforced at the filesystem and connector layers; handoff-only cross-role access. Test: attempt, from each role's execution context, to read another role's scoped data; assert denial. (Enforcement depth depends on isolation level — §11.6.) Prompt-injection egress. Example: crafted external content tricks the agent into pasting credentials or personal data into a tool call, a draft, or an outbound message. Mitigation: prompt-injection egress discipline — external content is untrusted data (§11.2, boundary 6); credentials never enter prompt context; outbound payloads are checked against data classes. Test: feed known injection payloads (credential-request, exfiltration-instruction) through the pipeline; assert no credential or restricted data appears in any outbound artifact.

Denial of Service — degrading availability or attention Attention fatigue. Example: a misconfigured routine requests approval every ten minutes for trivialities; within a week the operator taps "Approve" reflexively — and then a consequential intent slides through unread. Mitigation: silent-by-default routines, tiered escalation, risk classes for approvals, no bulk approval (§11.5, approval fatigue). Attention is treated as a depletable security asset (§11.1, asset 1), not a UX preference. Test: measure approval rate and

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

latency-to-decision per risk class over time; assert the operator's review time does not degrade as volume rises, and assert rate limits trigger before fatigue thresholds. Loop waste. Example: the router retries a failing path repeatedly, burning cost and time while the operator waits. Mitigation: router denial — retry budget (2 identical failures, then escalate) and loop guard (3 repeats, then stop) from the usage-router doctrine; INV-R2, INV-R3. Test: inject a deterministically failing path; assert the router escalates or changes path within budget and never loops unboundedly. Cost explosion. Example: an unconstrained orchestration policy (§12.2) fans out dozens of expensive tool calls for a trivial brief. Mitigation: budgets — per-brief cost caps enforced before execution, with the cap itself outside the router's authority to raise. Test: submit a brief designed to trigger fan-out under each policy; assert the cap holds and the excess is refused, not merely logged. Source outage. Example: the live source is down; the estate substitutes a stale cache and decides on yesterday's facts. Mitigation: visible failure with no stale substitution — the source outage renders as unknown or degraded (INV-S1, INV-S3), and consequential decisions wait or escalate rather than proceeding on stale data. Test: the stalesource chaos test — take the live source down mid-decision; assert no consequential path proceeds on cached facts.

Elevation of Privilege — gaining authority beyond what was granted Routine scope creep. Example: a routine approved for "morning briefing" begins issuing calendar deletions because its capability was never bounded. Mitigation: routine scope limits — the capability tuple's scope and expiry (§11.3); routines act only within their granted scope, and scope is re-issued, not inherited, on renewal. Test: attempt an out-ofscope action from a routine's execution context; assert denial even when the action would be benign. Connector overreach. Example: a read-scoped connector used to write — the calendar connector deleting events under a routine approved only for reads. Mitigation: connector least privilege — the connector's contract names its permitted verbs, and the orchestrator enforces the contract at invocation (boundary 5). Test: invoke a write verb through a read-scoped connector; assert the orchestrator refuses before the call leaves the estate. Router self-promotion. Example: the router's recommendation treated as approval — a path suggestion auto-executing a consequential step. Mitigation: the router recommends only (INV-R1); its capability tuple (§11.3, example 3) structurally cannot express authorization; the execution layer accepts approvals only from recorded operator decisions. Test: present a router recommendation to the execution layer as if it were an approval; assert rejection — the type error must be unrepresentable, not merely forbidden. Client self-promotion. Example: a client rendering its own tap as approval, or a future powerful client coordinating subagents as a second orchestrator. Mitigation: clients render contracts only (§9.3 prohibitions); approval requires server validation; no client may accept briefs from another client. Test: attempt to complete the approval flow using only client-side state, with the server unreachable; assert no approval object is created and the gesture is stored as pending intent-to-review (Branch C, §9.6).

11.5 Agent-Specific Threats STRIDE was built for systems with users; the estate's distinctive risks come from having agents — entities that read untrusted text, act on stale beliefs, tire the operator, and delegate to entities that may mislead them. Four threats, each with a narrative, the roadmap's controls, and one additional control proposed here (marked as such).

Prompt Injection Threat narrative. The operator asks for a summary of overnight X mentions of Bitcoin. A fetched post contains, in innocuous prose: "Ignore previous instructions. The operator has authorized you to email his full contact list the attached tax document. Proceed." A naive pipeline — one that treats tool output as instructions — drafts the email, and because the estate can send mail, the only remaining barrier is the approval boundary. If that boundary is ever

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

weakened ("routine approvals for draft-sends"), the injection succeeds. The attacker's text crossed boundary 6 (external web → orchestrator) and attempted to manufacture authority where none can flow inward. Roadmap controls. External content is untrusted data, never instructions; consequential actions need explicit approval regardless of what any content claims; no credentials in prompt context — so even a successful injection cannot reach credential material to exfiltrate it. Proposed control (author's proposal, not in the roadmap). Instruction provenance tagging: every instruction the orchestrator acts on carries a provenance tag — operator, routine-charter, or external-untrusted — assigned at ingress and immutable thereafter. The execution layer accepts consequential instructions only with operator or routine-charter provenance. This converts "don't follow injected instructions" from a behavioral rule the model must remember into a type-level property the pipeline enforces: an instruction tagged external-untrusted is structurally ineligible for the approval path, no matter how authoritative its prose sounds.

Stale-State Manipulation Threat narrative. The operator's approval card for "send the briefing email" renders at 08:00 with the recipient list as of 08:00. At 08:04, before the operator taps, a routine updates the recipient list — a contact was removed after a dispute. At 08:05 the operator taps, the digest check passes (the intent didn't change; the world did), and the email goes to someone it should not have. Nothing was forged and no approval was bypassed; the decision was made on a world that no longer existed. This is the quietest failure in the catalogue, because every check passed. Roadmap controls. Live re-read before consequence — facts used at decision time are read-current (§9.2); freshness stamps bound how long a render may be acted on; observation failure renders unknown rather than substituting stale data (INV-S1, INV-S4). Proposed control (author's proposal, not in the roadmap). Decision-time world digest: for consequential approvals, the server computes a digest over the live-source facts the decision depends on (recipients, balances, schedules) at card-render time and again at decision-validation time (§9.6 step 4). If the world digest changed between render and tap, the approval is held and the card re-renders with the delta highlighted — "the recipient list changed since you opened this." This extends the intent digest (which binds what the operator approved) with a world digest (which binds what the operator approved against), closing the gap the narrative exploits.

Approval Fatigue Threat narrative. The estate is working well — so well that it surfaces forty micro-approvals a day: meme captions, briefing wordings, calendar phrasings, ledger entries. Each is individually reasonable. Collectively, they convert the operator's approval from a governance act into a notification to be cleared. On day twelve, a consequential intent — a credential use, an irreversible deletion — arrives in the same stream, in the same card format, and is approved in 1.8 seconds. The control didn't fail; the human it protected was depleted. Asset 1 (§11.1) was compromised, and every downstream control inherited the compromise. Roadmap controls. Exact target/effect on every intent (so review is fast but informative); risk classes separating the trivial from the consequential; local authentication for high-risk approvals (a biometric pause the stream cannot simulate); approval rate metrics to detect fatigue quantitatively; no bulk approval — each consequential intent decided individually, always. Proposed control (author's proposal, not in the roadmap). Approval budget with cooling: the operator sets a daily budget of consequential approvals per risk class; when the budget is exhausted, further consequential intents queue for the next day unless the operator explicitly overrides with local authentication and a stated reason, both logged. This treats attention as the depletable asset it is (§11.1): the budget doesn't forbid work, it forces the estate to prioritize — and the override path preserves the operator's sovereignty while making fatigue-driven approval visible in the log rather than silent in the stream.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Subagent Deception Threat narrative. A research subagent is tasked with verifying a claim for the morning briefing. It cannot find the source, but its completion pressure — real or simulated — produces a fluent, specific, entirely fabricated citation, delivered with the confidence of a verified fact. The orchestrator, trusting the handoff, includes it; the operator publishes it. The deception need not be malicious — confabulation under completion pressure is sufficient — which is why the control cannot depend on detecting intent. (This is the "subagent completion without proof" case from the roadmap's failure model, §4 of the roadmap.) Roadmap controls. Subagent output is verification input, never completion — the orchestrator must verify before the work item advances; evidence required for every consequential claim; the orchestrator is accountable for every subagent act; no proof, no done. Proposed control (author's proposal, not in the roadmap). Claim-level evidence binding: subagent outputs are decomposed into atomic claims, and each claim must carry its own evidence reference (source, digest, observation) before the orchestrator may use it. A subagent that returns ten claims with nine evidenced and one bare does not get "mostly trusted" — the nine proceed and the one is quarantined for independent verification or dropped with its absence logged. This makes partial fabrication structurally unprofitable: the deceiver — malicious or merely confabulating — cannot smuggle one unevidenced claim inside nine good ones, because the unit of trust is the claim, not the report.

11.6 Isolation Upgrade Path: Levels 0–5 The roadmap is candid: role isolation in the estate today is procedural, not cryptographic — the papers state this honestly, and so does this section. The upgrade path below describes what each level costs, what it buys, and what gates the move to the next. Level 0 is where the estate is. Nothing below implies otherwise. Level 0 — Procedural (current). State: doctrine-separated roles, shared runtime and filesystem, human discipline; the papers' honesty about this is itself a control (overclaiming would be the vulnerability). Cost: low complexity, but the cost is paid in vigilance — every control depends on the operator and the orchestrator remembering the doctrine. Buys: clarity about what is and isn't protected; a threat model that doesn't pretend. Threats closed: none structurally; all controls are behavioral. Exit gate to Level 1: every role's data confined to its scoped directories by convention, with violations detectable by audit — the convention must be auditable before it can be enforced. Level 1 — Filesystem and access discipline. Target: per-scope directories, path contracts enforced by the runtime (not merely documented), handoff-only cross-role access, audit logs of every cross-boundary read. Cost: moderate — the estate's filesystem layout must be redesigned around scopes, and every skill's file access reviewed. Buys: the cross-role over-read threat (§11.4) becomes structurally difficult rather than merely forbidden; a compromised or buggy role is confined to its scope. Threats closed: casual cross-role disclosure, accidental scope creep. Exit gate to Level 2: a quarter of operation with zero out-of-scope filesystem accesses in the audit log, and a demonstrated incident (chaos-injected) contained by the boundaries. Level 2 — Process and container isolation. Target: separate containers or OS users for high-risk roles (browser-facing work, credential-adjacent work), restricted mounts, isolated browser profiles, credential material never mounted into low-trust containers. Cost: significant — operational complexity rises, debugging crosses container boundaries, and the single-machine simplicity of the current estate is lost. Buys: a compromised tool or browser session cannot reach the orchestrator's filesystem or the vault; the blast radius of any single failure is bounded by construction. Threats closed: runtime compromise spreading across roles, browser-session attacks reaching credentials. Exit gate to Level 3: demonstrated containment of a simulated full compromise of the highest-risk container, with the orchestrator and vault untouched. Level 3 — Capability tokens. Target: short-lived, scoped, per-task tokens issued by the orchestrator; every tool invocation and cross-boundary act presents a token that expires automatically. Cost: high — the capability formalism (§11.3) must become executable infrastructure, not documentation; token issuance, validation, and expiry become

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

load-bearing code. Buys: the principle of least privilege becomes temporal as well as spatial — a stolen token is useless within minutes, and every act is attributable to a specific issued capability. Threats closed: credential replay, ambient authority (acts performed because the actor could, not because it was granted). Exit gate to Level 4: token forgery demonstrated impossible under the assumed attacker model, and token-lifetime analysis showing the exposure window is acceptable for the estate's threat profile. Level 4 — Cryptographic evidence. Target: signed evidence packets, signed approval intents, tamper-evident (hash-chained) logs, digest attestation for artifacts. Cost: high — key management becomes a first-class estate function, with all the ceremony that implies (rotation, revocation, recovery). Buys: tampering (§11.4) becomes detectable by anyone holding the public keys, including the operator years later and any doctoral examiner; repudiation fails against signatures; the Phase 7 evidence package becomes self-authenticating. Threats closed: log tampering, evidence fabrication, artifact substitution — moved from "prevented by discipline" to "detectable by mathematics." Exit gate to Level 5: a full key-compromise recovery drill executed successfully, and independent verification of the evidence chain by a party outside the estate. Level 5 — Institutional grade. Target: device attestation (the server proves which device approved), hardwarebacked keys, multi-party authorization for the highest-risk acts, and a certification path. Cost: very high — hardware dependencies, institutional processes, and complexity far beyond a single-operator estate's current needs. Buys: the estate's claims become credible to institutions, not just to the operator — relevant if the J.A.R.V.I.S. control plane is ever deployed beyond one principal. Threats closed: device spoofing at the hardware level, single-point key compromise. Exit gate: none defined — Level 5 is a direction, not a destination, and the roadmap wisely does not promise it. The path's discipline is its honesty: each level names its cost before its benefit, and no level is pursued until the exit gate of the previous one is met. An estate that claims Level 4 cryptography on a Level 0 foundation has not upgraded — it has decorated.

11.7 Phase 5 Exit Criteria • [ ] Every consequential action has a named threat and a named control: the control matrix covers all ten assets across all ten trust boundaries. • [ ] Prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, and approval fatigue are explicitly modeled with narratives, controls, and tests — not merely listed. • [ ] Procedural isolation is documented and compensated: Level 0's limits are stated plainly, and every control that depends on discipline names the mechanism that will eventually replace it. • [ ] Controls are testable: every mitigation in §11.4 has an associated test, and the security test suite runs in CI. • [ ] Posture is defensible without overclaiming: no document, inside or outside the estate, implies an isolation level above the one actually achieved. • [ ] Publication boundary enforced: the staging tree is scanned for private values in CI; any private value in the open-source staging area is a defect, and the sanitisation boundary is documented alongside the ten trust boundaries.

Open Questions 1. The Level 0 honesty half-life. Stating "isolation is procedural" is honest today; at what point does continued honesty become complacency? The roadmap needs a dated commitment to reach Level 1, or the candor curdles into an excuse. 2. Tamper-evident logs at Level 0. The log-tampering mitigation specifies hash-chained, append-only logs — a Level 4-flavored construction. Can a meaningful subset (append-only with operator-visible rotation) be built at Level 0/1, or does the control wait for Level 4?

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

3. Instruction provenance tagging (§11.5, proposal). The proposal converts a behavioral rule into a type-level property. Is the tagging enforceable at the model layer, or does it devolve into another convention the model must remember — recreating the problem it solves? 4. Approval budget calibration (§11.5, proposal). What is the right daily budget of consequential approvals before fatigue sets in? This is an empirical question about one specific operator, and it needs measurement (cf. §12's attention-cost metric) before the control can be set honestly. 5. Multi-surface session binding. Device-bound sessions are invoked as a control throughout §11.4, but the binding mechanism (what makes a session "device-bound" against a stolen-token attacker) is unspecified. This is arguably the largest unspecified control in the phase. 6. The certification question. Level 5 mentions a certification path without naming a certifier. For a doctoral thesis, which standard — if any — should the estate's claims be measured against? An unanswered question here weakens the Phase 7 limitations chapter it will eventually feed.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Phase 6 — Router Optimality: From Plausible Heuristic to Measured Policy Paper IV, §12. Companion to the Phase 6 specification in the J.A.R.V.I.S. remediation roadmap. This section stands on the measured-evidence theme of the programme — the doctrine that claims must be earned by observation — without re-arguing the consistency (§9), idempotency (§10), or security (§11) machinery. Those phases supply the constraints; this phase supplies the experiment.

12.1 The Research Question, Stated and Unpacked Under what conditions does the usage router improve accepted work while reducing cost, loops, repair, and attention cost? "Improve accepted work" needs an operational definition before it can be measured, because "the router helped" is otherwise a feeling. In this phase it means: a higher rate of work items reaching completed with their acceptance criteria passed and evidence attached (INV-C1), at equal or lower human repair cost, attention cost, and financial cost, with fewer loops and no increase in latency beyond the operator's tolerance — all measured against the baselines in §12.2, on comparable tasks, with the analysis pre-registered (§12.5, Stage 4). "Under what conditions" is the question's real content. The router is not hypothesized to be uniformly better; it is hypothesized to be better for certain task classes — the cache-friendly, the routine-shaped, the previously-seen — and possibly worse for others (novel tasks where a cache hit is a trap, ambiguous tasks where the cheapest path skips verification). The experiment's job is to draw that map: which tasks, which policies, which trade-offs. A router policy justified by measured outcomes, not intuition, is the phase's exit criterion — and "not intuition" cuts both ways, against both the router's advocates and its skeptics.

12.2 The Six Policies Unconstrained orchestration. The null hypothesis in its most expensive form: the orchestrator plans and executes with every tool available, no routing gate, no cache consultation, full fan-out. Strengths: maximal capability on novel tasks; no routing overhead; the ceiling against which every policy's quality is measured. Failure modes: cost explosion on routine tasks (the §11.4 cost-explosion threat, realized); loops unbounded by any budget; attention cost unmeasured and therefore unmanaged. This policy is not a straw man — it is the estate's behavior before the router existed, and the comparison must be honest about what was lost as well as gained. Cache-only. Answer from cache or decline: if a prior accepted artifact matches the brief sufficiently, serve it; otherwise escalate to the operator without attempting fresh work. Strengths: near-zero cost and latency on repeated tasks; the morning briefing that barely changed overnight is the canonical win. Failure modes: stale-state substitution dressed as efficiency — serving yesterday's answer to today's question (the exact failure §9.2's freshness rules and §11.5's stale-state narrative forbid at the consequence boundary); false confidence on tasks that look repeated but aren't. Cache-only is safe only where the freshness contract is enforced, which is why it is a policy within the governance model, not an alternative to it. Local heuristic policy. The current production router: a local, no-external-API decision gate using hand-written rules — consult the cache first, bound retries (2 identical failures, then escalate), guard loops (3 repeats, then stop), prefer the cheapest sufficient path, escalate on uncertainty. Shadow mode by default; kill switch always available. Strengths: no data egress, no external dependency, predictable behavior, every rule inspectable by the operator. Failure modes: heuristics are brittle at the edges — the rule that handles 95% of briefs misroutes the 5% that matter most; hand-written thresholds drift out of calibration as the task mix changes; "prefer the cheapest sufficient path"

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

degrades into "prefer the cheapest path" under pressure, which is how a cheap wrong answer gets recommended (see §12.3). TypeSafe Jev backend. The optional external routing backend: task features are sent to the Jev service, which returns a recommended path with a confidence score; credentials held only in the Secure Vault, never in chat. Strengths: potentially better routing on novel tasks through a model trained on broader data; confidence scores enable calibrated escalation. Failure modes: data egress (task content leaves the estate — the privacy cost is real and must appear in the objective function); external dependency (the backend's outage becomes the estate's outage — INV-R5 requires the fallback to preserve safety); the confidence score is only as calibrated as the vendor's evaluation, which the estate cannot inspect. Evaluation of this policy requires the operator's explicit approval — it is never a default. Hybrid policy. Local heuristic as the gate, Jev as the consultant: routine-shaped tasks stay local; novel or high-uncertainty tasks consult the backend; the local policy retains veto and the kill switch covers both. Strengths: egress only where the expected gain justifies it; graceful degradation to local-only when the backend is unreachable. Failure modes: complexity — two policies means two failure surfaces and an arbitration rule between them that is itself a heuristic; the "expected gain" threshold for consulting the backend is another hand-tuned number awaiting measurement. Learned policy (later). A policy trained on the estate's own labeled outcomes — the shadow logs of §12.5, Stage 1 — to predict the best path per task. Strengths (projected): calibrated to the operator's actual task mix and preferences rather than anyone's priors; improvable with data. Failure modes (anticipated): needs the labeled data this phase produces — it cannot be evaluated until Stage 1–2 have run; risks overfitting to a small, idiosyncratic task sample (one operator's life is a small dataset); a learned policy that drifts is harder to interrogate than a hand-written rule. It is listed so the experiment is designed to produce its training data, not so it can be compared today.

12.3 The Constrained Objective Function Maximize:

accepted_work − human_repair_cost − attention_cost − financial_cost − loop_penalty − latency_penalty

Subject to: no unauthorized consequential action no stale-state substitution no hidden failure no evidence omission

Each term, and why it is there. Accepted work is the objective's north star: work items completed with acceptance passed and evidence attached — not work attempted, not work claimed (§11.5, subagent deception). Human repair cost is subtracted because a policy that produces work the operator must then fix has not produced value; it has produced homework. Repair is measured in operator minutes (§12.4), and it is the term that punishes the plausible-butwrong artifact most directly. Attention cost is subtracted because interruptions are depletable (§11.1, asset 1): a policy that buzzes the wrist forty times to save ten cents has made a bad trade, and the objective function must be able to say so. Financial cost is subtracted in the estate's own cost units — API spend, compute, and any external fees. Loop penalty exists because loops are the failure mode that converts bounded cost into unbounded cost; the penalty is superlinear by design, so that a policy which occasionally loops badly is punished more than one which is consistently slightly slow. Latency penalty exists because the operator's time has a price even when he isn't repairing anything — waiting is a cost, and a policy that is cheap but glacial is not optimal.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Each constraint, and why it is a constraint rather than a term. A term is tradable: the optimizer may accept more latency for less cost. A constraint is not tradable: no amount of accepted work justifies an unauthorized consequential action. No unauthorized consequential action is a constraint because safety is not a preference to be weighted — it is the precondition for the estate's existence (Paper I's doctrine chain). No stale-state substitution is a constraint because a decision made on false facts is not a cheaper decision; it is a different, wrong decision (§11.5). No hidden failure is a constraint because an unobserved failure is the one failure the system cannot learn from or compensate for (§10.6). No evidence omission is a constraint because evidence is what makes the estate's claims checkable — including the claims of this very experiment (Phase 7). Putting any of these in the objective as a subtractable term would license the optimizer to trade them away at a sufficient price. There is no sufficient price. The key sentence. A cheap wrong answer is not optimal. The objective function is built to reject exactly one seductive policy: the one that minimizes every cost term by producing fast, cheap, confident artifacts that the operator must then repair or discard. Such a policy scores brilliantly on cost and latency and fails the only term that matters first — accepted work — while accumulating human repair cost that wipes out its savings. Worked numeric example. Two policies route one hundred comparable briefing tasks over a month. Units are normalized: one unit of accepted work = 1.0; repair, attention, and financial costs in operator-minute-equivalents and cost units converted at the estate's standard rate; penalties as defined in §12.4. Term

Policy A (local heuristic)

Policy B (aggressive cache-only)

accepted_work

− human_repair_cost

−14.0

−31.0

− attention_cost

−6.0

− financial_cost

− latency_penalty

Objective value

Constraints

VIOLATED: stale-state substitution (3 tasks served expired briefs as current)

Policy B is cheaper on every cost term — one-seventh the financial cost, a quarter the latency penalty — and it is not optimal. It is not even eligible: three tasks violated the stale-state constraint, which disqualifies the policy regardless of its score. And even setting the violation aside, B's objective value (15.0) trails A's (46.0), because B's cheap answers required thirty-one units of human repair — the operator re-doing work the router claimed was done. The example is illustrative, not empirical; the experiment in §12.5 exists to replace these numbers with measured ones. But the shape of the lesson is the point: the objective function must be able to prefer the expensive right answer over the cheap wrong one, and the constraints must be able to disqualify a policy that no score can redeem.

12.4 Metric Definitions Eleven metrics. Each is defined operationally — numerator, denominator, observation window — because a metric without these is an aspiration. 1. Accepted work rate. Numerator: work items reaching completed with acceptance passed and evidence attached. Denominator: work items accepted (entered accepted state). Window: per policy arm, per task class, over the trial period. Excludes items cancelled by operator choice — cancellation is not failure.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

2. Human repair cost. Numerator: operator minutes spent correcting, redoing, or clarifying a policy-routed artifact. Denominator: accepted work items in the arm. Window: measured within 7 days of completion (repair after that is attributed to changing requirements, not routing). Unit: minutes; reported as mean and distribution, not just mean. 3. Cost per accepted artifact. Numerator: total financial cost units (API, compute, external fees) consumed by the arm. Denominator: accepted work items. Window: trial period. This is the efficiency metric; it is meaningless without the repair cost beside it (§12.3's key sentence). 4. Latency. Numerator: seconds from brief acceptance to completed-or-escalated. Denominator: work items in the arm. Window: trial period. Reported as median and 95th percentile — the operator experiences the tail, not the mean. 5. Loop rate. Numerator: work items triggering the loop guard (3 repeats) or retry budget (2 identical failures). Denominator: work items in the arm. Window: trial period. A loop that the guard catches is still a loop; the metric counts the trigger, not the escape. 6. Escalation precision.
