Numerator: escalations where the operator's intervention changed the outcome (the escalation was necessary). Denominator: all escalations. Window: trial period. High precision means the router escalates only when it should. 7. Escalation recall. Numerator: cases where operator intervention was necessary and the router escalated. Denominator: all cases where intervention was necessary (including those found by audit). Window: trial period. High recall means the router doesn't silently bungle what it should have escalated. Precision and recall are reported separately because the failure modes differ: low precision wastes attention; low recall causes harm. 8. False denial rate. Numerator: tasks the router declined or over-escalated that a baseline policy would have completed acceptably. Denominator: tasks handled by the arm. Window: trial period. The timidity metric — a router that never fails because it never tries is not optimal either. 9. Stale-source error rate. Numerator: decisions later shown to rest on stale facts (audit-detected). Denominator: consequential decisions in the arm. Window: trial period, with a 30-day audit tail. Any nonzero value here is also a constraint investigation (§12.3), not just a metric. 10. Attention cost. Numerator: operator interruptions attributable to the arm — notifications, approval requests, escalations, clarifications. Denominator: accepted work items. Window: trial period. Weighted by surface: a wrist buzz counts more than a batched iPad review, because interruption is not fungible (Paper III's notification discipline). 11. Evidence completeness. Numerator: completed items whose evidence packets pass the schema and acceptance checks on first audit. Denominator: completed items. Window: trial period. The metric that guards the experiment's own integrity: a policy that completes work without evidence is manufacturing the appearance of accepted work.

12.5 Experimental Design: Four Stages Stage 1 — Shadow Logging The current estate already supports this: the router runs in shadow mode, logging its recommendations alongside the path actually taken. Stage 1 extends the log schema with outcome labels, so that recommendations can later be joined to results. The extended JSON log record, annotated field by field:

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

{ "ts": "2026-10-08T20:31:00Z",

// decision time; joins to work-item history (§9.4)

"work_id": "wi-2026-10-08-118",

// which work item this decision served

"policy": "local-heuristic",

// policy name + version: "local-heuristic/v3"

"policy_version": "v3",

// version pinned; comparisons are version-to-version

"task_class": "briefing-daily",

// task class, from the controlled vocabulary (§12.5 Stage 3)

"task_features": {

// features the policy saw: novelty, cache age, estimated

cost "novelty": 0.2, "cache_age_hours": 14.0, "estimated_cost_units": 3.0 }, "recommended_path": "cache-refresh", // what the router recommended "confidence": 0.81,

// router's self-reported confidence, if any

"path_taken": "cache-refresh",

// what actually happened (may differ: operator override,

kill switch) "override": false,

// true if the operator or orchestrator overrode the

recommendation "outcome": "accepted",

// OUTCOME LABEL: accepted | repaired | escalated | failed |

cancelled "human_repair": false,

// OUTCOME LABEL: did the operator have to fix the artifact?

"repair_time_minutes": 0,

// OUTCOME LABEL: minutes of repair, 0 if none

"cost_units": 2.7,

// OUTCOME LABEL: actual financial cost consumed

"latency_seconds": 41,

// OUTCOME LABEL: acceptance-to-completion seconds

"attention_interruptions": 1,

// OUTCOME LABEL: wrist buzzes, approvals, escalations caused

"evidence_complete": true,

// OUTCOME LABEL: evidence packet passed first audit

"loop_triggered": false,

// OUTCOME LABEL: loop guard or retry budget fired

"constraint_flags": []

// OUTCOME LABEL: any of the four constraints touched — must

be empty }

Every field with an OUTCOME LABEL is written after the fact — by the orchestrator observing the work item's lifecycle, not by the router grading itself. The router that scores its own homework is the experiment's first confound, and the schema prevents it structurally: recommendation fields are written at decision time, outcome fields at completion time, by different writers.

Stage 2 — Offline Replay Historical shadow logs are replayed through alternate policies: for each logged decision, each candidate policy is asked what it would have recommended given the same task features, and the recommendation is scored against the observed outcome. The evaluation protocol is four questions, asked in order: Q1 — Choice. What would the alternate policy have recommended, given exactly the task features the original policy saw? No peeking at the outcome; the replay harness supplies features only. A policy that cannot answer from features alone is not replayable and is excluded from this stage. Q2 — Outcome delta. Given the observed outcome of the path actually taken, would the alternate recommendation plausibly have changed the outcome class (accepted / repaired / escalated / failed)? Scored conservatively: the alternate wins the point only if its recommended path dominates on the observed evidence (e.g., the taken path looped and the alternate would have served cache). Ties go to the observed path — replay must not manufacture victories. Q3 — Cost delta. What is the estimated difference in cost, latency, and attention between the recommended and taken paths? Estimated from the policy's historical averages on the same task class, not from imagination. This is where cache-only's cheapness and the heuristic's overhead are quantified against each other on identical tasks. Q4 — Robustness. Does the alternate policy's advantage (or disadvantage) hold across task classes, or is it concentrated in one class? A policy that wins overall by dominating one frequent class while failing a rare-but-consequen‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

tial class is not better — it is lucky about the task mix. Q4 is the question that prevents the experiment from recommending a policy that is optimal on average and dangerous at the margins. Replay's known limitation is stated upfront: it measures recommendation quality against observed outcomes, not causal effect — the alternate path was never taken, so its true outcome is counterfactual. Stage 3 exists to supply the causality that Stage 2 cannot.

Stage 3 — Controlled Live Trials Low-risk tasks are randomly assigned across policy arms, and outcomes are measured with the Stage 1 schema. Three design elements need precise definition. Eligibility criteria for low-risk tasks. A task is eligible for randomized routing if and only if all of the following hold: (a) it is non-consequential — no external effect, no publication, no transmission, no state change outside the estate's scratch space; (b) it is reversible — any artifact it produces can be discarded without trace; (c) it requires no approval-path decision — the task never reaches §9.6's seven steps; (d) its bounded cost is below the per-brief cap with 10× headroom (a runaway cannot hurt); (e) its task class has at least 30 historical observations, so the arm comparison has a baseline. Briefings, draft summaries, and cache-refresh reads are the canonical eligible classes. Anything else — anything the operator would notice going wrong — is excluded. The five policy arms. Unconstrained orchestration; cache-only; local heuristic; TypeSafe Jev backend; hybrid. The learned policy is excluded — it does not exist yet, and randomizing tasks to a non-existent arm is not an experiment. Each arm runs the same eligibility filter, the same outcome schema, and the same kill switch. Why consequential actions are never randomized. Random assignment is the experiment's engine and its ethical boundary. Assigning a consequential action — a publication, a send, a deletion — to a policy for the sake of comparison would mean deliberately exposing the operator to a worse-than-necessary risk of harm in order to learn something. The estate's doctrine does not permit using the operator as a test subject: the constraint set of §12.3 binds the experiment as well as the policies. Randomization applies only where every arm's worst case is a wasted few minutes and a discarded draft. The trial measures routing quality on safe tasks and generalizes with stated uncertainty to the rest — it does not purchase certainty with the operator's safety.

Stage 4 — Statistical Evaluation Comparison metrics. Arms are compared on the eleven metrics of §12.4, with accepted work rate, human repair cost, and attention cost as the primary trio — the terms the research question names. Cost per accepted artifact and latency are secondary; the remaining six are diagnostic (they explain why an arm won or lost) and constraint-adjacent (stale-source errors trigger investigation, not just scoring). Small-sample guidance. The estate has one operator and a modest task volume; the trial will not produce thousands of observations per arm. The analysis is therefore designed for small samples from the start: • Bayesian estimation over null-hypothesis testing: report posterior distributions for each metric's arm difference (e.g., "the probability that the hybrid arm's repair cost is lower than the heuristic's is 0.87"), not p-values. With small samples, the question is never "is the difference significant" but "how large is it plausibly, and how sure are we." • Paired task comparisons: where the same task class recurs (the daily briefing is the same task every day), compare arms on matched pairs rather than pooled means — pairing removes the largest source of variance, which is the tasks themselves. • Pre-registered analysis: the primary trio, the comparison method, and the decision rule ("adopt the hybrid arm for eligible tasks if its posterior probability of lower repair cost exceeds 0.9 with no attention-cost regression") are written down before Stage 3 begins. The analyst does not get to shop for the metric that flatters the preferred policy. • What NOT to claim: no claim of significance from underpowered comparisons; no generalization from eligible (safe, routine) tasks to consequential tasks — the trial's writ does not run there; no causal claim from Stage 2

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

replay alone; no "the router is optimal" full stop — only "policy P outperformed policy Q on task class C, under these conditions, with this uncertainty." The experiment's credibility depends on the modesty of its claims matching the size of its data.

12.6 Router Guardrails: Eight Things the Router Must Never Do Each with its one-line rationale — the guardrails are constraints (§12.3) restated as prohibitions, because a constraint the engineers cannot recite is a constraint that will be violated. 1. Never approve a consequential action. Rationale: approval is the operator's exclusive authority (§11.1, asset 2); a router that approves has become a second principal. 2. Never override approval boundaries. Rationale: the seven-step flow (§9.6) and its failure branches are not routing suggestions; no path recommendation may shorten, skip, or reinterpret them. 3. Never authorize spending. Rationale: money movement requires the strongest verification and the operator's explicit consent (§10.8); cost optimization must never become cost authorization. 4. Never authorize sending or deletion. Rationale: external effects are irreversible or near-irreversible (§10.8); the router's writ ends at the consequence boundary. 5. Never hide failures. Rationale: a hidden failure is the one failure the system cannot learn from, compensate for, or show the operator (§12.3, constraint 3); silent-by-default means silent operation, never silent failure. 6. Never suppress evidence. Rationale: evidence is what makes every claim in this paper checkable — including the router's own performance claims (§12.4, metric 11). 7. Never substitute stale data. Rationale: a decision on false facts is not a cheaper decision but a wrong one (§11.5); freshness is a constraint, not a cost term. 8. Never become a second orchestrator. Rationale: the single-writer rule (§9.3) is structural; a router that coordinates subagents, accepts briefs, or maintains authoritative state has violated the architecture no matter how well it routes. (The roadmap's compact list groups spending, sending, and deletion under one item; they are separated here because the rationale differs — money, utterance, and destruction fail differently — while the prohibition is identical.)

12.7 Phase 6 Exit Criteria • [ ] Router decisions are linked to outcomes: every Stage 1 log record carries outcome labels written by the orchestrator, not the router. • [ ] Local, cache-only, and unconstrained baselines are compared on the eleven metrics, with the analysis preregistered before live trials. • [ ] The Jev backend is evaluated if and only if the operator approves: no data egress for experimentation without explicit consent. • [ ] The router policy's value is supported by measured evidence: the adopted policy is named, its winning conditions are stated, its uncertainty is quantified, and its guardrails are tested — not asserted. • [ ] Methodology and results published openly: the pre-registered protocol, the labelled decision history (sanitised), and the analysis are released so the router's measured comparison can be re-analysed by anyone — the evaluation is a public experiment, not a private verdict.

Open Questions 1. The generalization gap. Stage 3 randomizes only safe, routine tasks; the router's value proposition includes novel and consequential-adjacent tasks. How far may the trial's findings be generalized, and what additional evidence (structured operator review? expert audit?) would license generalization to tasks the trial cannot touch?

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

2. Attention-cost weighting. Metric 10 weights a wrist buzz above a batched iPad review — but by how much? The weights encode a theory of the operator's attention that is currently asserted, not measured. Whose calibration study sets them? 3. The cost-unit exchange rate. The objective function converts operator minutes, API spend, and latency into commensurable units. The exchange rate is a value judgment disguised as arithmetic — who sets it, and how often is it re-examined? 4. Learned-policy data sufficiency. Stage 1–2 produce the learned policy's training data as a side effect. How many labeled decisions, across how many task classes, constitute a sufficient training set for one operator's estate — and who decides the policy is ready to join the arms as a sixth? 5. Pre-registration amendment. If Stage 3 reveals a flaw in the pre-registered analysis (a metric misdefined, a confound unanticipated), what is the legitimate procedure for amending the registration without destroying its credibility? The protocol needs a stated amendment rule before the trial begins. 6. Constraint violation as data. If an arm violates a constraint during trials (a stale substitution, a hidden failure), the arm is disqualified — but the violation is also the most informative event of the trial. How are disqualifiedarm data handled in the analysis: excluded entirely, or analyzed separately as failure case studies for Phase 7?

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

13. Phase 7: Integration into Doctoral Evidence 13.1 A System Is Not an Argument There is a difference between a system and an argument, and a doctoral thesis defends the second. A system is a thing that runs: skills under a directory, a runtime that persists, a Watchtower sweep that reports once an hour and stays silent otherwise. An argument is a claim about why some structure of ideas holds, what it predicts, where it breaks, and how a sceptic could tell the difference between it being true and it being an expensive illusion. Phase 7 is where the engineering of Phases 0–6 is converted into that argument — where the J.A.R.V.I.S. estate stops being a codebase with good discipline and becomes a defended contribution to knowledge. The distinction matters because almost everything the estate has built so far is, to a committee, anecdote with excellent habits. The doctrine chain — Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine — is a genuinely disciplined way to run an agent, and Papers I–III describe it well. But discipline is not a thesis. A thesis requires a claim stated precisely enough to be wrong, and evidence structured precisely enough to show whether it is. Phase 7 adds no features. It takes the artifacts Phases 0–6 produce and arranges them into the one thing a committee can examine: a falsifiable claim, a body of evidence that tests it, and an honest account of the gap between the two. This is why Phase 7 has an exit criterion rather than a deadline: "Architecture, proofs, tests, and metrics form one defensible thesis." A package is integrated when an examiner can trace any sentence in the claim back through a chain of evidence to an artifact that could, in principle, have refuted it. Traceability is the deliverable. Everything else is arrangement.

13.2 The Doctoral Claim, Unpacked The roadmap states the doctoral claim verbatim, and Phase 7 takes that wording as fixed. The claim is: A doctrine-governed agent control plane, using formal state constraints, evidence packets, approval binding, consistency rules, and usage routing, can increase accepted work while reducing human repair, attention cost, and unsafe consequential action. Every clause of this claim is a load-bearing joint, and each must be unpacked into something measurable, because a clause that cannot be measured cannot be defended — and a clause that cannot be refuted cannot be claimed. The unpacking below assigns each clause to the phase that evidences it and states, for each, what the committee should expect to see and what would count against it. "Doctrinal-governed." This asserts that the estate's behaviour is governed by an explicit doctrine — the Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine chain and its associated approval and evidence disciplines — rather than by the emergent behaviour of the underlying model. The evidence is the canonical glossary and the doctrine-to-invariant traceability matrix of Phases 0 and 2: every major doctrinal statement has an owner, an executable invariant, and a test. A committee member should look for the absence of doctrinal statements that float free of tests — any sentence beginning "the estate never…" that has no corresponding invariant is a governance claim without governance. What would refute this clause: a consequential action that occurs with no traceable doctrinal justification, or a routine that runs without the evidence packet its doctrine requires. "Formal state constraints." This asserts that the work-state machine is not a diagram but a checked model: illegal transitions are unreachable under the defined failure model. The evidence is the Phase 1 model-checking result — the formal model, the guard table, the failure model, and the proof obligations with their outcomes. The committee should look for two things: that the failure model is stated explicitly enough to be criticized (a proof under unstated assumptions is decoration), and that weakened guards demonstrably produce counterexamples (a model that cannot

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

fail cannot convince). What would refute this clause: a reachable illegal state under the stated failure model, or an assumption in the failure model that silently excludes a realistic fault. "Evidence packets." This asserts that completion is not a claim but a bundle: every completed consequential run carries a structured proof bundle — the evidence packet — whose revision matches the artifact it supports. The evidence is the Phase 2 invariant suite (INV-C1, INV-E1, INV-E2 in particular) and the schema contract frozen in Phase 0. The committee should look for the negative cases: runs that completed without packets, packets whose revisions drift from their artifacts, gaps that were rendered invisible. INV-E3 ("missing evidence renders as missing") and INV-E4 ("gaps stay visible") exist precisely so the committee can check the estate's behaviour when proof is absent, which is where most systems cheat. What would refute this clause: any completed consequential run whose packet is missing, mismatched, or fabricated after the fact. "Approval binding." This asserts that approval is not a gesture but a cryptographic-semantic object: an approval binds to an exact action, target, effect, scope, revision, and expiry, and any mutation of the bound intent invalidates the approval. The evidence is the Phase 1 guard on awaiting_approval → executing, the INV-A1 through INVA7 invariant family, and the mutated/expiry test harnesses of Phase 2 (immediate next actions 10 and 11 in the roadmap). The committee should demand the adversarial cases: a mutated approval, an expired approval, a duplicated approval tap, an offline approval — and watch each fail closed. What would refute this clause: a single execution trace in which a mutated or expired approval led to a consequential effect. "Consistency rules." This asserts the hybrid consistency contract of Phase 3: status may be eventually consistent across the five client surfaces, but consequence is strongly consistent — approval decisions are authoritative, linearised, and single-writer, and no device can become a second orchestrator. The evidence is the consistency specification, the revision protocol, the conflict-resolution table, and the multi-device tests including clock-skew and network-loss injection. The committee should look at the conflict table and ask whether every entry has a test, and should probe the hardest row: two devices attempting the same approval, where exactly one decision must be recorded and the other must be told it lost. What would refute this clause: a conflict scenario in which two inconsistent consequential outcomes both stand, or a device that executed a consequential action on local authority alone. "Usage routing." This asserts that the routing of work — which path a work item takes through the estate — is a policy worth stating and measuring, not an intuition of the orchestrator. The evidence is the Phase 6 experiment: the policy specification, the decision-log schema with outcome labels, the offline replay, and the controlled live trials comparing the local policy, cache-only, unconstrained orchestration, and the hybrid against measured outcomes. The committee should check the router guardrails first — the router must never approve consequence, override approval boundaries, or hide failure — because a router that can bypass the control plane invalidates every other clause of the claim. What would refute this clause: measured evidence that the routing policy performs no better than the cacheonly baseline on the stated objective function, or any trace of the router exercising authority it is denied. "Increase accepted work." This is the positive productivity clause: the governed control plane should let more work reach genuine acceptance, not less. The committee will notice the tension immediately — governance usually slows things down, and the claim asserts the opposite. The evidence is the accepted-work-rate metric across the Phase 6 trials: the governed path should show a higher rate of work accepted without human repair than the unconstrained baseline. If it does not, the thesis does not die, but this clause does, and the claim must be restated. What would refute this clause: trial data showing the governed policy accepting less work than the baseline at equal or higher cost. "Reduce human repair." This asserts that the control plane reduces the operator's corrective labour — measured in repair events and repair time — relative to the unconstrained baseline. Human repair is the costliest line in the objective function because it is the operator's time, and the operator (HgX) is the single scarce resource of the estate. The evidence is the outcome-labelled router logs and the operator scorecard: repair events, repair minutes per accepted artifact, and the trend over the measurement window. What would refute this clause: a rising or flat repair curve under the governed policy. "Reduce attention cost." This is the clause that carries the calm-technology lineage of the literature review: the estate's routines are silent by default, escalation is tiered, and the operator's attention is treated as an architectural in‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

variant, not a user-experience preference. The evidence is the attention-interruption metric in the Phase 6 logs — the count and cost of interruptions the operator had to field — together with the Watchtower discipline (silent unless degraded) and the tiered-escalation rules from the approval model. A committee should ask the hardest version of the question: whether attention cost was measured or merely asserted, and how the measurement distinguishes a genuine interruption from a glanceable notification. Measurement noise in attention cost is a named risk in Section 15, and the thesis must carry it openly. What would refute this clause: measurement showing the governed estate interrupting the operator as often as — or more often than — the baseline, or a measurement method that cannot distinguish interruption from information. "Reduce unsafe consequential action." This is the safety clause and the one the claim cannot survive without. It asserts that under the defined failure model, the control plane produces no unsafe consequential actions — no execution without valid approval, no duplicate effects from duplicate taps, no stale-state substitution, no offline approval becoming authoritative. The evidence is the chaos suite of Phase 2 and the idempotency tests of Phase 4: adversarial cases run against the live semantics, all failing closed. Note the careful scope: the claim is not "the estate is safe" but "unsafe consequential action is reduced relative to the unconstrained baseline under the defined failure model" — a comparative, bounded claim, which is the only kind a committee should accept. What would refute this clause: any demonstrated trace in which a consequential action occurred without a valid, bound, unexpired approval. Taken together, the unpacked claim is deliberately narrower than "we built a personal AI assistant" and deliberately stronger. Narrower, because every clause is bounded by a failure model, a baseline, or a metric. Stronger, because every clause can be checked.

13.3 The Ten-Part Evidence Package The roadmap requires a ten-part evidence package. Each part is described below as what it is, which phase produces it, and what a committee member would look for in it — because an evidence package is written for its examiner, not its author. 1. Formal specification. The canonical spec repository frozen in Phase 0: the glossary, the state machine, the event vocabulary, the invariant catalogue, the consistency contract, the idempotency key schema, the security threat model, and the router policy — versioned, with one owner per concept. The committee will check reconciliation: that no two documents define the same term differently, and that the three white papers' vocabularies are mapped to the single normative model rather than left as competing descriptions. 2. Model checking / proof. The Phase 1 formal model in the chosen language (TLA+ or Alloy — open question, pending the Month 2 decision), the guard table, the failure model, the proof obligations, and the model-checker output including any counterexamples found and resolved during development. The committee will look first at the failure model — whether it is explicit and realistic — and second at whether weakened guards produce counterexamples, which is what separates a genuine proof from a model that was never stressed. 3. Invariant tests. The three-layer verification suite of Phase 2: static contract tests, property-based tests over random event sequences, and chaos tests covering the adversarial cases (mutated approvals, expired approvals, duplicate taps, stale sources, delegation without proof). The committee will run the suite, or ask for it to be run in their presence, and will look specifically for the tests that are supposed to fail closed — a suite in which everything passes on the first run, including the adversarial cases, is a suite that was never adversarial. 4. Consistency tests. The multi-device tests of Phase 3: approval conflicts between devices, stale-cache rendering, clock-skew behaviour, network-loss during approval, and the offline-tap case. The committee will look for determinism — the same conflict producing the same resolution regardless of which device is examined — and for the negative guarantee that no device can become a source of truth. 5. Idempotency tests. The Phase 4 suites: duplicate-tap tests, retry-under-network-loss tests, ambiguous-outcome handling, and the idempotency-key collision and binding tests. The committee will look for the at-most-once demon‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

stration under test conditions that resemble the real failure modes — duplicate taps during a slow network, retries after an ambiguous execution — because those are the cases where consequential systems actually double-spend. 6. Threat model. The Phase 5 deliverables: the asset register, the trust-boundary map across the ten named boundaries, the capability matrix, the threat catalogue (STRIDE plus the agent-specific threats: prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, approval fatigue, subagent deception), the attack trees, and the control matrix. The committee will check that every consequential action has a named threat and a named control, and will pay particular attention to the honest limitation — procedural isolation, not cryptographic — and whether the controls proposed actually compensate for it rather than restating it. 7. Router experiment. The Phase 6 report: the policy specification, the decision-log schema, the outcome labels, the offline replay results, the controlled live-trial protocol and results, and the policy comparison on the stated objective function. The committee will scrutinise the experimental design — randomisation only over eligible low-risk tasks, never over consequential actions; pre-registered metrics; honest handling of small samples via Bayesian estimation — because a router experiment without a design is an anecdote with a dashboard. 8. Operator scorecard. The longitudinal record of the operator's experience: accepted-work rate, repair events and repair time, attention interruptions, approval latency, and the trend of each over the measurement window. The scorecard is the only evidence part that measures the human rather than the machine, and the committee will ask how the measurements were taken, what the baselines were, and whether the operator's behaviour changed because he knew he was being measured. Single-operator design makes this evidence thin by construction — Section 15 names it — and the scorecard must say so. 9. Failure case studies. A small number of worked failures — real ones, from the estate's own history or from the chaos suite — each narrated as: what was attempted, what failed, which invariant caught it (or which gap let it through), and what changed. The committee reads these to judge whether the estate learns. A thesis with no failures is either a thesis about a trivial system or a thesis that is hiding its failures; either way, the limitations section cannot be written without them. 10. Limitations section. The formal statement of where the proofs stop: the failure model does not cover everything, the single-operator measurement is thin, role isolation is procedural, the router evaluation is bounded, the threat model assumes stated attacker capabilities. The committee will read this section first — experienced examiners always do — and will judge the entire thesis by whether its limitations are specific. "Further work is needed" is not a limitation; "the model assumes device clocks skew by at most N seconds, and the estate has no mechanism to enforce this" is.

13.4 The Ten-Chapter Thesis Structure The roadmap suggests a ten-chapter thesis structure. What follows states, for each chapter, what it argues — the position the chapter defends, not merely the material it contains. Chapter 1 — Introduction: attention and authority in personal agent systems. Argues that the central problem of personal agent systems is not capability but governance: as agents gain the power to act consequentially on an operator's behalf, the scarce resources become the operator's attention and the authority to act. The chapter positions the thesis as an answer to the question of how a single operator governs an estate of agents without becoming its fulltime supervisor. Chapter 2 — Literature review: AI agents, human-AI interaction, safety, calm technology. Argues that the existing literature treats agent safety as a property of models and agent usability as a property of interfaces, and that the governance layer between them — the control plane that binds doctrine to execution — is under-theorised. The calm-technology lineage (Weiser & Brown) is claimed as the intellectual ancestry of the attention-cost invariant: technology that respects attention is not a design preference but a measurable system property. Chapter 3 — Thesis: contracts around the model, not model intelligence alone. Argues the central proposition: that reliable agent behaviour comes from explicit contracts — state machines, invariants, evidence packets, approval

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

binding — wrapped around the model, rather than from the model's own intelligence or alignment. This chapter states the doctoral claim verbatim and unpacks it as Section 13.2 does, and it is the chapter the entire examination turns on. Chapter 4 — Architecture: operating, backend, and front-end control planes. Argues that Papers I–III describe one coherent control plane at three levels of abstraction — the operating doctrine, the backend lifecycle, and the front-end operator loop — and that their reconciliation into a single normative specification (Phase 0) is itself a contribution: a worked example of turning a philosophy of operation into a checkable model. Chapter 5 — Formal model: state machine, invariants, consistency, idempotency. Argues that the estate's core safety properties are provable, not merely asserted: the state machine is model-checked, the invariants are executable, the consistency contract is deterministic, and consequential actions are at-most-once. This is the technical heart of the thesis, and it argues by demonstration — the proofs and test results are the argument. Chapter 6 — Security model: threat boundaries and procedural isolation limits. Argues that an honest security model is stronger than an ambitious one: the chapter states the procedural-isolation limitation plainly, models the threats that limitation leaves open, names the compensating controls, and lays out the evaluated upgrade path (Levels 0–5) without claiming to have climbed it. The argument is that defensibility without overclaiming is itself a security property — a system that knows its boundaries can be trusted further than one that pretends it has none. Chapter 7 — Evaluation: routing, accepted work, repair, attention cost. Argues that the usage router is an empirically justified policy: the chapter presents the experimental design, the objective function, the baselines, and the measured comparison, and it argues — with the small-sample statistics handled openly — that the governed routing policy improves accepted work while reducing repair and attention cost relative to the alternatives tested. Chapter 8 — Results. Argues nothing new; it states what was found. The chapter presents the model-checking outcomes, the invariant suite results, the chaos-test results, the router trial data, and the operator scorecard trends as findings, separated cleanly from the interpretation that belongs in the chapters around it. A results chapter that argues is a results chapter that cannot be trusted. Chapter 9 — Limitations. Argues that the thesis knows where it stops — and that this knowledge is part of the contribution. The chapter carries the limitations evidence (package part 10) in full: the failure-model boundaries, the single-operator measurement thinness, the procedural isolation, the bounded router evaluation. The argument is that a control plane whose limits are stated can be operated safely, while one whose limits are unstated cannot, regardless of its proofs. Chapter 10 — Contribution and future work. Argues what is genuinely new (Section 13.5), what is not claimed, and what the work enables next: the isolation upgrade path, the learned router policy, the multi-operator extension, and the open-sourcing of the estate's sanitised specifications. The chapter closes the loop opened in Chapter 1: the operator's attention is still the scarce resource, but it is now governed by a system that can prove what it does with it.

13.5 The Contribution Statement A doctoral thesis must say what is new, and it must say it in a way that survives a sceptical examiner who has read the related work. The J.A.R.V.I.S. programme claims four contributions, each grounded in the phases that produce it, and each stated narrowly enough to be checked. First, the governed control-plane architecture: a complete, specified, and partially verified architecture for governing a personal agent estate by explicit doctrine rather than by model behaviour. The doctrine chain is not new as an idea — the Grok Bot field guide that sourced it is cited honestly in the references — but its conversion into a canonical state machine, a guard table, an executable invariant suite, and a consistency contract is new as engineering, and the thesis argues it is new as a research artifact: a worked, checkable instance of "contracts around the model" as a design discipline. No prior system known to this programme combines the state machine, the approval binding, the evidence packet, and the attention invariant in one specified control plane; if an examiner knows of one, Chapter 2 is where that correction belongs, and the thesis is better for it.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Second, the evidence packet as a completion protocol: the elevation of "no proof, no done" from a team slogan to a formal protocol. In this design, completion is not a status an agent asserts but a state the machine reaches only when a structured proof bundle exists, matches the artifact revision, and passes acceptance — and the absence of the bundle renders visibly as absence, never as silent completion. The contribution is the protocol shape: what a packet contains, what binds it to its artifact, what invalidates it, and how its absence is rendered. The invariant family INV-C and INV-E, together with the packet schema, are the citable artifact. Third, approval at the last safe moment: a formal treatment of human approval as a first-class system object — digest-bound to an exact intent, expiry-bound, single-recorded, invalidated by mutation — rather than as a user-interface gesture that the backend trusts on faith. The multi-device approval flow of Phase 3, in which the client renders a card from a hint and the server alone records the decision after validating digest, expiry, and session, is the concrete mechanism; the contribution is the demonstration that approval can be given the same formal treatment as any other state transition, with the same adversarial test discipline. Fourth, attention as an architectural invariant: the treatment of the operator's attention as a measurable, budgeted, first-class system property — specified in the objective function, logged in the router's outcome labels, tracked on the operator scorecard, and defended through silent-by-default routines and tiered escalation. The thesis does not claim to have solved attention measurement — the noise in the metric is a named risk and a stated limitation — but it claims, and defends, that attention cost belongs in the objective function of an agent control plane alongside latency and financial cost, and it provides a worked measurement apparatus for putting it there. Fifth, the open reference implementation as a research artifact: the demonstration that a formally specified, empirically validated agent control plane can be published — system open, principal's private life closed — with its proofs, tests, and measurements intact. The contribution is the publishability itself: the frozen glossary, the modelchecked machine, the executable invariant suite, the consistency contract, the idempotency schema, the threat model, and the router's measured comparison, released as a coherent, verifiable whole. An open system that cannot be verified is a liability; this programme's evidence package is the demonstration that openness and defensibility compose. What is not claimed matters as much, and the thesis states it plainly. There is no model novelty: no new model architecture, no training contribution, no claim about the capabilities of the underlying language model. There are no operating-system-kernel claims: the runtime is a persistent Linux environment, and the isolation story is procedural, honestly graded at Level 0 with an evaluated path upward. There are no multi-party security claims: the design is single-operator, and the threat model assumes it. There is no claim of absolute router optimality — only of measured comparison against stated baselines under a stated objective function. And there is no claim that the formalism is complete: the failure model is bounded, the proofs are bounded, and Section 13.6 says what follows from that.

13.6 What "Defensible" Means A defensible thesis is a falsifiable thesis. That is the entire definition, and everything else in Phase 7 is its implementation. For every sentence in the doctoral claim, the evidence package must contain the artifact that would refute it: the model-checker output that would have shown a reachable illegal state, the chaos test that would have let a mutated approval execute, the router trial that would have shown the baseline winning. A claim paired with its refutation evidence is science; a claim paired only with supporting evidence is marketing. The Phase 7 integration review — the final gate before the thesis is written — is therefore conducted as an adversarial exercise: for each claim, the reviewer asks what evidence would prove it false, and confirms that the package contains that evidence and that it came out in the claim's favour. Any claim for which the refuting evidence was never collected is not a finding; it is a hope, and it is cut from the claim or downgraded to future work. This is why the limitations section is a strength rather than a concession. A committee does not trust a thesis that claims everything; it trusts a thesis that knows exactly what it has not shown. "No proof, no done," the estate's own rule, applies to the thesis itself: no claim without the evidence that could have refuted it, no evidence without the trace back to the artifact, no artifact without the phase that produced it. That is what defensible means, and it is the standard Phase 7 holds the entire programme to.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

14. The Twelve-Month Programme 14.1 How This Schedule Is Built The roadmap offers three pacing options: a 12-week engineering fix (Option A), a 6-month doctoral validation (Option B), and a 12–18 month full PhD track (Option C). This section maps a concrete twelve-month programme from Option B extended to a full year — the middle path, given the room it needs. The first six months carry the engineering weight: freezing contracts, proving the state machine, verifying invariants, and defining consistency and idempotency. The middle of the year carries the security formalisation and the router evaluation, which need the earlier phases stable before they can be measured honestly. The final quarter integrates the evidence and writes the thesis. Nothing in this schedule adds to the roadmap's phases; it sequences them, names their artifacts, and states what "done" looks like at each month boundary — because a programme without checkable month boundaries is a wish with a calendar. Two conventions govern the whole schedule. First, the estate's own rule — no proof, no done — applies to the programme itself: a month is not complete when its calendar page turns but when its exit criteria are evidenced. Second, every scheduling assumption this paper is unsure about is marked as an Open question in bold, so the operator (HgX) can resolve it rather than discovering it mid-year.

Month 1 — Phase 0: Freeze the Contracts Objective. Produce the single canonical specification that every later phase works from. Month 1 reconciles the three white papers into one normative vocabulary, freezes the canonical glossary, the work-state machine, and the event vocabulary, and stands up the versioned specification repository that will hold every artifact the programme produces. This month also executes the first eight of the roadmap's fifteen immediate next actions: the repository itself, the frozen state machine, the glossary, the doctrine-to-invariant list, the approval-intent digest rule, the consistency guarantee matrix, the idempotency key schema, and the first draft of the threat model document. The discipline of the month is subtraction: every concept gets one definition and one owner, and anything that cannot be defined precisely enough to be tested is cut or marked as an Open question for later resolution. Deliverables. - Canonical glossary (all terms defined once, with owners) committed to spec/. - Canonical workstate machine: the twelve states, the transition skeleton, and the guard table in spec/state-machine.md. - Canonical event vocabulary: all fifteen events with normative definitions, in spec/events.md. - Specification repository with the roadmap's layout (spec/, contracts/, tests/, proofs/, experiments/), versioned and reviewable. Approval-intent digest rule: the exact construction of the intent digest (fields, hash, canonicalisation) in spec/approval-digest.md. - Consistency guarantee matrix and idempotency key schema, drafted for refinement in Months 5–6. - Threat-model document skeleton (asset register and trust-boundary list) for completion in Month 7. Contract schema drafts: contracts/evidence-packet.schema.json, contracts/approval-intent.schema.json, contracts/watchtower.schema.json, contracts/decision-packet.schema.json, contracts/artifact-manifest.schema.json. Exit criteria. - All three white papers reconciled into one normative spec; no two documents define the same concept differently. - Every state, event, contract, and invariant has a named owner. - The repository is versioned, and the operator can review any concept's single definition. - The digest rule is stated precisely enough that two independent implementations produce the same digest for the same intent. Dependencies. The three white papers as source material (Papers I–III). The operator's approval of the reconciliation where the papers' vocabularies genuinely conflict — Open question: whether vocabulary conflicts are resolved by the operator at a Month 1 review or delegated to the chief of staff with operator ratification.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Month 2 — Phase 1: The Formal Model Objective. Convert the frozen state machine into a formal model in the chosen specification language and state the failure model it will be checked against. This is the month where the estate commits to a modelling language — TLA+ or Alloy, per the roadmap's recommendation — writes the state variables, transition guards, and illegal-state definitions, and defines the failure model (network interruption, expired approval, mutated intent, duplicate tap, stale live source, missing evidence, subagent completion without proof, router failure, device clock skew, out-of-order events, missing permissions, credential challenge, operator pause). The ten "what fixed means" statements from the roadmap become the proof obligations. The month's intellectual work is the failure model: a proof under unstated assumptions is decoration, so every assumption is written down where a future examiner can attack it. Deliverables. - Formal model in the chosen language covering the twelve states, the transition skeleton, and the guard table. - Proof obligations document: the ten "what fixed means" statements rendered as checkable properties. Failure model document: the thirteen failure conditions with precise definitions of what each assumes. - Counterexample log (initial): any illegal states found during model development, with resolutions. Exit criteria. - The model type-checks / parses in the chosen tool and covers every transition in the skeleton. - All ten proof obligations are stated as properties the model checker can evaluate. - The failure model is explicit, and each assumption is reviewable by the operator. - The modelling-language decision (TLA+ vs Alloy) is recorded with reasons — Open question until Month 2. Dependencies. Month 1's frozen state machine and event vocabulary; without them the model has nothing to formalise. The language decision blocks all modelling work, so it is taken in the first week.

Month 3 — Phase 1 Results and Phase 2 Contract Tests Objective. Run the model checker to completion and begin the invariant verification with Layer 1: static contract tests. The first half of the month is adversarial work on the model — running the proof obligations, examining counterexamples, weakening guards deliberately to confirm the model can fail, and documenting the results. The second half converts the doctrine-to-invariant list from Month 1 into the Layer 1 contract test suite: schema validation, unknown-enum fail-closed, missing-field rejection, expired-approval rejection, mutated-digest rejection, and the rule that incomplete evidence cannot produce completion. This month also builds two of the roadmap's test harnesses: the mutated/expired approval harness and the duplicate-tap chaos test. Deliverables. - Model-checker results for all ten proof obligations, with the full output archived in proofs/. Counterexample report: every counterexample found, its cause, and its resolution (or the weakened guard that produced it, kept as a regression case). - Layer 1 contract test suite, automated and passing against the current estate semantics. - Test harnesses for mutated/expired approvals and duplicate approval taps (roadmap next actions 10 and 11). Exit criteria. - The model checker finds no reachable illegal state under the stated failure model. - Every weakened guard tested produces a counterexample (the model is genuinely stressed, not decorative). - All Layer 1 contract tests pass, and mutated or expired approvals demonstrably cannot pass them. - The invariant traceability matrix is started: each invariant linked to its doctrine statement and its test. Dependencies. Month 2's model and failure model. The estate's current approval and evidence semantics must be observable enough to test against — if the live estate has drifted from the frozen spec, the drift is recorded rather than silently accommodated (see the estate-drift risk in Section 15).

Month 4 — Phase 2 Property and Chaos Tests Objective. Complete the invariant verification with Layers 2 and 3: property-based tests over random event sequences and the full chaos suite. Where Month 3 tested the contracts statically, Month 4 tests the estate's behaviour

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

under adversarial conditions: approval mutations, expiry timing, duplicate taps, network loss, subagent claims without evidence, stale sources, device offline during approval, intent change after card render, backend change before client refresh, router backend down, digest mismatch, and missing tests in evidence packets. This month also builds the remaining roadmap test harnesses: the stale-source test, the delegation-without-proof test, and the sharedsurface redaction test (next actions 12–14). Deliverables. - Layer 2 property-based test suite: random event-sequence generators with invariant assertions, covering approval mutations, expiry times, duplicates, network loss, subagent claims, and stale sources. - Layer 3 chaos test suite: the full adversarial catalogue from the roadmap, each with setup, fault injection, and expected fail-closed behaviour. - Test harnesses for stale sources, delegation without proof, and shared-surface redaction. - Completed invariant traceability matrix: invariant → doctrine → code → test, for all 27 invariants. Exit criteria. - Every major doctrine statement has a testable invariant, and every invariant has at least one test in each applicable layer. - All tests automated; the suite runs in CI (or the estate's equivalent continuous check) with no manual steps. - Optimistic completion fails the tests: no path to completed exists without evidence and acceptance. - The chaos suite is citable as empirical validation: each test documents the fault, the expected behaviour, and the observed behaviour. Dependencies. Month 3's contract tests and traceability matrix. The chaos tests need a test environment that can inject the faults without touching the live estate — Open question: whether the estate stands up a dedicated test harness environment or runs chaos tests against instrumented live paths with safeguards.

Month 5 — Phase 3: Consistency Objective. Define and test the multi-device consistency contract. Month 5 writes the consistency model specification — the hybrid guarantee table (eventual status, revision-stable evidence, strongly consistent approvals, linearised consequential execution), the single-authoritative-writer architecture, the event-sourced work-state shape, the revisiontoken protocol, and the approval flow in which the client renders from hints and the server alone records decisions. It then tests the contract: approval conflicts between devices, stale-cache rendering, clock skew, network loss during approval, and the offline-tap case. The conflict-resolution table from the roadmap becomes a test matrix: every row gets a test, and the hardest row — two devices attempting the same approval — gets the most attention. Deliverables. - Consistency model specification: guarantee table, writer architecture, event-sourced state shape, revision protocol, approval flow sequence. - Conflict-resolution table with a test for every row. - Multi-device sequence diagrams for the approval flow, the conflict cases, and the offline-tap case. - Device cache policy and freshness policy documents. - Multi-device test suite including clock-skew and network-loss injection. Exit criteria. - No device can become a source of truth under test: every client mutation path is validated serverside. - Approval conflicts resolve deterministically: the same conflict always produces the same resolution. - Stale data cannot masquerade as current: expired freshness renders as unknown or reduced confidence. - Offline taps cannot become approvals: the pending-gesture case is tested and fails closed. Dependencies. Months 1–4: the frozen vocabulary, the proven state machine, and the invariant suite. The consistency tests assume the approval-binding semantics are already verified; Month 5 tests their multi-device behaviour, not the binding itself.

Month 6 — Phase 4: Idempotency Objective. Specify and test the transactional semantics of consequential action: the idempotency key schema, the execution-record lifecycle, the governed saga pattern, the retry policy matrix, and the ambiguity-handling protocol. Month 6 answers the question the estate has never formally answered: what happens when a consequential action is attempted twice, or when its outcome is unknown. The deliverable that matters most is the ambiguous-outcome protocol — the rule that an unverifiable consequential execution becomes visible unknown or failed, never silent

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

completion — because that is the case where real systems double-spend. The external-system classification table (native idempotency, read-after-write, no-idempotency high-risk, reversible, irreversible, financial, publication, deletion) is completed for every integration the estate actually uses. Deliverables. - Idempotency key specification: hash(work_id, intent_id, intent_digest, action, target, scope, revision, operation_class) with canonicalisation rules. - Execution-record schema and the lifecycle states (prepared → approved → execution_requested → execution_started → execution_observed / execution_failed / execution_unknown → completed / cancelled). - Governed saga specification: prepare → approval → execute → verify → record evidence → compensate, with the approval requirements at each step. - Retry policy matrix by operation class; ambiguity-handling protocol. - External-system classification for every live integration. - Duplicate-tap and offline-approval test suites; ambiguous-outcome verification tests. Exit criteria. - Duplicate taps cannot duplicate effects under test: the second tap returns the existing decision and state. - Network loss cannot silently complete: the ambiguous case renders as visible unknown/failed. - Retries occur only when the safe-to-retry flag is set; at-most-once is demonstrable under test. - Every consequential integration is classified, and high-risk integrations (no idempotency, no verification) have their strengthened-approval or manualexecution handling documented. Dependencies. Month 5's consistency contract (the approval flow the saga builds on) and Months 3–4's invariant suite (INV-A6 on duplicate taps, INV-E on evidence). The external-system classification needs an accurate inventory of the estate's integrations — connectors, publishing paths, financial paths — which the operator confirms.

Month 7 — Phase 5: Threat Model and Capability Matrix Objective. Convert the estate's honest limitations into a formal security model. Month 7 writes the asset register (operator attention, approval authority, credentials, personal data across its classes, artifacts, evidence packets, logs, connectors, runtime, router state), draws the trust-boundary map across the ten named boundaries, builds the capability matrix (subject, action, object, scope, condition, expiry, approval requirement, evidence requirement), and writes the threat catalogue: STRIDE plus the four agent-specific threats the roadmap names — prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, approval fatigue, and subagent deception. Attack trees are drawn for the highest-risk paths (approval forgery, intent mutation, evidence fabrication), and the control matrix names the control for every consequential action. The month's discipline is the same as the thesis's: no overclaiming. Procedural isolation is documented as Level 0 and compensated, not disguised. Deliverables. - Asset register and trust-boundary diagram (ten boundaries). - Capability matrix covering every consequential action. - Threat catalogue: STRIDE analysis plus prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, approval fatigue, subagent deception — each with named controls. - Attack trees for the highest-risk paths; control matrix mapping threats to controls to tests. - Isolation upgrade roadmap (Levels 0–5) with the evaluation criteria for each level. Exit criteria. - Every consequential action has a named threat and a named control. - Prompt injection, stale state, and approval fatigue are explicitly modelled with controls and tests, not mentioned in passing. - Procedural isolation is documented with its compensating controls; nothing in the model implies a stronger isolation than exists. - The controls are testable: each control maps to at least one test in the Month 8 suite. Dependencies. Months 1–6: the threat model must describe the system as specified and verified, not as originally imagined. Any drift between the estate and the frozen spec must be reconciled before the model is written, or the model describes a fiction.

Month 8 — Phase 5 Security Tests and Isolation Level 1 Objective. Test the security model and implement the first isolation upgrade. Month 8 builds the security test suite from the control matrix — mutated-intent tests, artifact-tamper tests, log-tamper-evidence tests, memory-poisoning

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

tests, prompt-injection egress tests, approval-fatigue metric tests, subagent-deception tests — and executes the Level 1 isolation upgrade: per-scope directories, path contracts, handoff-only cross-role access, and audit logging. Level 1 is deliberately unglamorous: it is filesystem and access discipline, not cryptography, and its value is that it makes the procedural isolation auditable. The month also evaluates, without yet implementing, whether Levels 2–5 are warranted — the isolation cost-benefit analysis that Section 15 names as a risk. Deliverables. - Security test suite mapped to the control matrix, automated where possible. - Level 1 isolation implementation: per-scope directory layout, path contracts, handoff-only cross-role access rules, audit log format and retention. - Isolation cost-benefit analysis for Levels 2–5: what each level would cost (engineering effort, operational friction, attention cost) against what it would buy. - Updated threat catalogue entries reflecting Level 1 controls. Exit criteria. - Every control in the matrix has at least one passing test; every test maps to a control. - Level 1 is implemented and its audit logs are reviewable by the operator. - The cost-benefit analysis is written and reviewed: the decision to stop at Level 1 (or proceed) is recorded with reasons — Open question until Month 8. - The security posture is defensible without overclaiming: the documentation states Level 1 plainly. Dependencies. Month 7's threat model and control matrix. The Level 1 implementation needs operator approval for the directory and access changes, since they touch the live estate layout.

Month 9 — Phase 6: Shadow Logging and Offline Replay Objective. Begin the router evaluation with the two stages that require no live experimentation: shadow logging with outcome labels, and offline replay. Month 9 extends the router's decision logs — the usage router is already in shadow mode under local policy, so the logging infrastructure exists — with the outcome-label schema from the roadmap (outcome, human_repair, repair_time_minutes, cost_units, latency_seconds, attention_interruptions, evidence_complete), and produces the first router shadow report (roadmap next action 15). It then replays the labelled historical decisions through the alternate policies — cache-only, local heuristic, unconstrained orchestration, the hybrid — to produce the first comparative results. This month is also where the measurement apparatus for attention cost is built and its noise characterised, honestly, before any live trial depends on it. Deliverables. - Outcome-label schema and the extended decision-log format, documented and implemented. - First router shadow report: decisions logged, outcomes labelled, baselines described. - Offline replay harness and the replay results: each historical decision evaluated under each alternate policy. - Attention-cost measurement method with its noise characterisation and stated limitations. - Experiment protocol for the Month 10 live trials, pre-registered: eligible task classes, randomisation method, metrics, and stop rules. Exit criteria. - Router decisions are linked to labelled outcomes in the logs; the link is auditable. - Local, cache-only, and unconstrained baselines are compared on replay data with the stated objective function. - The attention-cost measurement exists, its noise is quantified, and its limitations are written down. - The live-trial protocol is pre-registered and approved by the operator before any live trial runs — no trial begins without this. Dependencies. Months 1–8: the router evaluation must measure the specified, verified system. The shadow logs need several weeks of labelled data, so labelling begins the moment the schema is implemented — Open question: whether Month 9's replay window is long enough to yield usable sample sizes, or whether labelling must begin earlier (in Month 1) as a background activity.

Month 10 — Phase 6: Controlled Live Trials Objective. Run the controlled live trials of routing policies on eligible low-risk tasks. Month 10 executes the pre-registered protocol: random assignment of eligible tasks across the policies under test, with the router guardrails enforced throughout — the router never approves consequential action, never overrides approval boundaries, never hides failure, never suppresses evidence. Consequential actions are never randomised; they always take the governed path. The month's work is operational as much as statistical: monitoring the trials, enforcing the stop rules, labelling

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

outcomes as they land, and protecting the operator's attention throughout — the trial must not become the thing that attention-cost measurement warns about. Deliverables. - Live-trial dataset: task assignments, policy per task, outcomes, repair events, attention interruptions, costs, latencies. - Stop-rule log: any trial arm stopped early, with the reason. - Guardrail compliance record: evidence that no trial task violated the router guardrails. - Preliminary statistical analysis with Bayesian estimation for the small samples. Exit criteria. - The trial ran to the pre-registered protocol or to a pre-registered stop rule — not to a post-hoc one. No consequential action was randomised; the guardrail compliance record is complete. - Every trial task has a labelled outcome; there are no unlabelled tasks in the analysis. - The statistical analysis reports uncertainty honestly: credible intervals, not point claims. Dependencies. Month 9's protocol, labelling infrastructure, and replay baselines. The operator's approval of the eligible task classes — the trial's scope is a consequential decision about the estate's own operation, and it needs the operator's explicit approval.

Month 11 — Phase 7: Evidence Integration Objective. Assemble the ten-part evidence package and establish the traceability the thesis depends on. Month 11 is arrangement, not new research: the formal specification, the model-checking results, the invariant suites, the consistency and idempotency tests, the threat model, the router experiment report, the operator scorecard, the failure case studies, and the limitations section are gathered into one package, and every sentence of the doctoral claim is traced to the evidence that could refute it. The adversarial integration review from Section 13.6 is conducted here: for each claim, the reviewer confirms the refuting evidence exists and came out in the claim's favour, and any claim that cannot meet this bar is cut or downgraded. The failure case studies are written this month — real failures, narrated honestly, each with the invariant that caught it or the gap that let it through. Deliverables. - The ten-part evidence package, assembled and versioned. - Claim-to-evidence traceability matrix: each clause of the doctoral claim linked to its supporting and refuting evidence. - Adversarial integration review report: claims kept, claims cut, claims downgraded, with reasons. - Failure case studies (worked, honest, each with a lesson). - Limitations section, drafted in full. Exit criteria. - Every clause of the doctoral claim has both supporting and refuting evidence in the package. - The traceability matrix has no dangling claims and no unclaimed evidence. - The limitations section is specific: it names failure-model boundaries, measurement thinness, and isolation limits. - The operator reviews the package and approves the claim as stated — or the claim is restated until he does. Dependencies. All prior months: the package assembles their artifacts. Any month whose exit criteria were not evidenced reopens here — Month 11 is the integration gate, and it fails closed.

Month 12 — Thesis Write-Up and Defence Preparation Objective. Write the thesis and prepare to defend it. Month 12 follows the ten-chapter structure from Section 13.4: the introduction and literature review, the thesis chapter stating the claim, the architecture chapter, the formal-model chapter, the security-model chapter, the evaluation chapter, the results, the limitations, and the contribution. The month's discipline is the same one the programme has enforced throughout: every assertive sentence traces to the evidence package, and the limitations chapter is written with the same care as the results. Defence preparation is adversarial by design: the known hard questions — the single-operator measurement, the procedural isolation, the attention-cost noise, the small router samples — are answered in writing before they are asked in person, and the answers point at evidence, not at rhetoric. Deliverables. - Complete thesis draft following the ten-chapter structure. - Claim-evidence appendix: the traceability matrix in publishable form. - Defence preparation document: the hard questions with evidence-backed answers. -

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

Programme retrospective: what the twelve months cost, what they proved, and what remains open — the honest input to any future work. Exit criteria. - The thesis draft is complete and internally consistent: no chapter contradicts another, no claim lacks its evidence trace. - The defence preparation covers every limitation the thesis states — the examiner will find them first. - The operator approves the thesis as the estate's account of itself. - The programme retrospective is written: the work ends with an honest ledger, not a victory lap. Dependencies. Month 11's integrated evidence package. The thesis cannot be written before the package exists, because the thesis is the package's argument — writing it earlier would be writing the conclusion before the experiment.

14.2 Programme Governance The operator as principal and final authority. The operator (HgX) is the principal of this programme in the same sense that he is the principal of the estate: all consequential decisions require his approval, and the programme's consequential decisions are named in advance. They are: the Month 1 vocabulary reconciliation where the papers conflict; the Month 2 modelling-language decision if the chief of staff cannot resolve it on the evidence; the Month 8 isolation-level decision (stop at Level 1 or proceed upward); the Month 9 approval of the live-trial protocol and its eligible task classes; the Month 11 approval of the doctoral claim as stated; and the Month 12 approval of the thesis as the estate's account of itself. Between these gates, the programme runs on delegated authority within the bounds the operator sets — but the bounds are explicit, and anything outside them comes back for approval. This is the approval-at-the-last-safe-moment discipline applied to the programme's own governance: decisions are made when they must be, by the one who bears them, with the evidence on the table. Monthly review cadence. At each month boundary, a review is held with a single question: are this month's exit criteria evidenced? The review is a go/no-go gate, not a status meeting. Go means the exit criteria are met and evidenced, and the next month begins. No-go means the month's work continues until the criteria are met — the schedule slips rather than the standard. A no-go does not require the operator's presence for purely technical shortfalls, but any no-go that changes the programme's end date, scope, or claim comes to the operator as a consequential decision. The review produces a one-page record: the criteria, the evidence, the verdict, and any carry-forward. Twelve months, twelve records, one ledger — the programme is governed the way the estate is governed, because it is the estate's work. Jarvis as chief of staff. Execution runs through the team-deployment pattern: Jarvis acts as chief of staff, issuing one brief per phase to a minimum specialist team — one agent per job, verification mandatory, no agent publishing, sending, spending, or deleting without the operator's approval. The pattern fits this programme exactly: the phases are separable jobs (modelling, test-writing, threat analysis, experiment design, evidence assembly), each needs a specialist's sustained attention, and each needs independent verification before its artifacts enter the package. The chief of staff does not do the specialists' work and does not approve their output on their say-so; verification is a separate job, assigned separately, and "no proof, no done" is enforced by the structure, not by trust. No proof, no done — applied to the programme itself. The estate's rule is the programme's rule. A month is not complete when its artifacts exist but when its exit criteria are evidenced: the model checker output is archived, the test suite runs in CI, the conflict table has its tests, the trial followed its protocol. Month 11's integration gate fails closed: any prior month whose criteria were waived rather than met reopens the gate. This is deliberately strict, because the thesis that emerges from a programme that waived its own standards is a thesis that cannot survive its own limitations section. Publishable by construction — applied to the programme's artifacts. The trilogy's main vision governs the programme's outputs as well as the estate's: every phase's artifacts are produced publishable — the system public, the principal's private life never. The frozen glossary, the formal model, the invariant suite, the consistency contract, the idempotency schema, the threat model, the router's labelled decision history (sanitised), and the evidence package are each releasable without a second production effort. Publication timing remains the operator's consequential de‐

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

cision; publishability is the standing requirement. A phase whose artifacts cannot be published has not finished its work — it has merely finished its writing. Dependencies and the critical path. The programme's critical path runs through Months 1, 2, 3, 11, and 12: the frozen spec, the formal model, the checked results, the integrated evidence, and the written thesis. These months cannot slip without moving the end date, because each is the prerequisite of everything after it. Months 4 through 10 have internal dependencies — the chaos tests assume the contract tests, the idempotency tests assume the consistency contract, the live trials assume the shadow labelling — but they also have some slack against each other: a slow Month 5 can overlap a fast Month 6 without breaking the chain, provided the exit criteria hold. The months with the least slack are Month 2 (the language decision blocks all modelling) and Month 9 (the labelling window cannot be compressed after the fact — outcome labels need calendar time, and no amount of effort creates more of it). The critical-path discipline is therefore: protect Months 1–3 and 9–12 absolutely; use Months 4–8 as the buffer, but never by waiving exit criteria — only by resourcing. Pacing: when to switch tracks. The roadmap's Option A (12-week engineering fix) is the fallback if the full programme cannot be sustained: it keeps the freeze, the property tests, the server-authoritative approvals, the idempotency keys, the threat model, and the shadow evaluation, and it surrenders the model-checked proof, the chaos depth, the live router trials, and the doctoral evidence package. Switch to Option A only on a named constraint — a hard external deadline, a sustained loss of operator availability, or a Month 3 result showing the formal methods are intractable for the estate's complexity — and record the switch as a consequential decision with the operator's approval, because it changes what the thesis can claim. The roadmap's Option C (12–18 month full PhD track) is the extension if the twelve months prove insufficient: it adds the operator study, the attention-cost measurement depth, and the longer router observation windows that a formal doctoral registration might require. Switch to Option C if the Month 9 measurement characterisation shows the attention-cost metric needs a longer baseline, or if the evidence integration of Month 11 reveals gaps that need another experimental cycle. The default is the twelve-month programme as stated. The tracks exist so that changing pace is a decision, not a drift.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

15. Risks and the Minimum Viable Fix 15.1 Risk Register A programme that does not name its risks is not managing them; it is hoping. The nine risks below are the ones most likely to damage the twelve-month programme or the thesis it produces. Each carries a likelihood and an impact, a plain description, and a mitigation that is a real action rather than a sentiment. Likelihoods are assessed against the estate as it actually operates: a single-operator system, built and run by agents under the operator's direction, with no prior formal-methods practice. R1 — Formal-methods learning curve. Likelihood: High. Impact: Medium. Neither the estate nor its chief of staff has prior practice with TLA+, Alloy, or proof assistants; the first formal model will be slow, the first proof obligations will be mis-stated, and Month 2 will likely produce a model that checks something other than what was intended. The risk is not that formal methods are impossible here but that the learning happens on the critical path, where every week's delay pushes the end date. Mitigation: decide the language in Month 2's first week on the evidence (Alloy's lighter learning curve against TLA+'s stronger temporal machinery), scope the first model to the approval-binding guards only — the highest-value properties — before extending to the full machine, and treat the first counterexamples as the curriculum rather than as failures. R2 — Single-operator bottleneck. Likelihood: High. Impact: High. Every consequential decision in the programme flows through the operator (HgX): vocabulary reconciliation, the modelling-language call, the isolation-level decision, the trial protocol, the claim, the thesis. He is also the sole subject of the attention-cost and repair measurements, and the sole approver of the estate's daily operation. If his availability drops for any sustained period, the go/ no-go gates stall, the trial protocol goes unapproved, and Months 9–12 cannot proceed. There is no deputy; the design is single-operator by doctrine.
