Mitigation: batch consequential decisions into the month-boundary reviews so the operator faces them as a set rather than as interruptions; pre-draft every decision with a recommendation and the evidence, so his job is judgement rather than research; and never schedule a gate that needs him during a period he has marked unavailable — the programme's calendar bends to his, not the reverse. R3 — Measurement noise in attention cost. Likelihood: High. Impact: Medium. Attention cost is the softest metric in the objective function: distinguishing a genuine interruption from a glanceable notification, controlling for the operator's day, and separating the estate's interruptions from everything else competing for his attention are all hard, and the measurement will be noisy. A noisy metric can show improvement that is not there, or hide improvement that is. Mitigation: characterise the noise before depending on the metric (Month 9's measurement work exists for exactly this), report attention results with uncertainty rather than as point claims, triangulate with the repair metric (repair events are harder to mis-measure than interruptions), and state the noise plainly in the limitations section rather than defending a precision the method cannot support. R4 — Small-sample statistics for router trials. Likelihood: High. Impact: Medium. The estate's task volume is that of one operator's life, not a data centre's: the controlled live trials of Month 10 will produce tens of tasks per policy arm, not thousands. Classical significance testing on such samples will either say nothing or say it too loudly. The risk is a trial that is technically executed and statistically meaningless — effort spent to produce a number no committee should trust. Mitigation: pre-register the analysis with Bayesian estimation designed for small samples, report credible intervals rather than p-values, lean on the offline replay (which can generate far more comparisons from historical data) as the primary quantitative evidence, and let the live trials carry the qualitative weight — guardrail compliance, protocol fidelity, real-world behaviour — rather than pretending they carry statistical power they do not have. R5 — Scope creep: formalism for its own sake. Likelihood: Medium. Impact: Medium. A programme whose subject is rigour can fall in love with rigour: the model grows properties nobody will test, the threat catalogue grows threats nobody will meet, the invariant list grows invariants that restate each other. Every formalism added beyond what the claim needs is time taken from the measurements the claim actually depends on, and a thesis bloated with

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

untested formalism is weaker, not stronger. Mitigation: the claim is the scope gate — every artifact proposed for the programme must answer which clause of the doctoral claim it evidences, and anything that cannot answer is cut or deferred to future work. The Month 11 adversarial review enforces this from the other end: unclaimed evidence is removed from the package. R6 — Estate drift. Likelihood: High. Impact: Medium. The estate is a live system under daily operation while the programme specifies it: skills change, routines are added, the operator's habits evolve, and the frozen spec of Month 1 describes a system that no longer quite exists by Month 6. Drift does not invalidate the work already done, but it silently narrows what the proofs prove — the model checks the machine as specified, and the machine as specified is last month's machine. Mitigation: the Month 1 freeze includes a drift protocol — the spec is versioned, changes to the live estate are logged against it, and each month boundary includes a drift check: does the spec still describe the estate? Minor drift is reconciled into the spec with a version bump; major drift (a changed approval path, a new consequential integration) reopens the affected phase's exit criteria rather than being waved through. R7 — Isolation upgrade cost vs benefit. Likelihood: Medium. Impact: Low. The isolation upgrade path (Levels 0– 5) is the programme's most open-ended commitment: each level costs real engineering effort and operational friction, and the security benefit to a single-operator estate diminishes past Level 1's filesystem and access discipline. The risk is spending Months 8's successor effort climbing a ladder whose upper rungs serve a threat model the estate does not have — or, conversely, stopping at Level 1 without the cost-benefit analysis that justifies stopping. Mitigation: Month 8 produces the cost-benefit analysis as a deliverable, not as an afterthought; the decision to stop at Level 1 is recorded with reasons and reviewed by the operator; and the thesis claims only what was built, with the higher levels carried honestly as evaluated future work. R8 — Thesis-committee scepticism of practitioner work. Likelihood: Medium. Impact: High. A thesis built from the operator's own working estate — practitioner research, single-operator, no laboratory, no control group beyond the estate's own baselines — will meet examiners who regard it as engineering with footnotes rather than research. This scepticism is not prejudice; it is the correct stance toward work that has not yet shown its generalisable claim, and the programme must answer it rather than resent it. Mitigation: the thesis leads with its generalisable artifacts — the control-plane architecture, the evidence-packet protocol, the approval-binding mechanism, the attention-cost measurement apparatus — and treats the estate as the worked instance, not the contribution. The literature review (Chapter 2) positions the work against the field rather than inside the estate, and the limitations section concedes exactly what practitioner work cannot show, which is what earns the right to claim what it can. R9 — Open-source publication hazards. Likelihood: Medium. Impact: High. The trilogy's main vision makes publication the programme's horizon, and publication has failure modes the other risks do not cover: a private value escaping into the staging tree (family detail, credential, live-source content), premature release of an unproven claim that the community then treats as established, and adversarial reuse of the published control plane (the threat model, published openly, teaches attackers as well as defenders). Mitigation: the sanitisation boundary is a tested Phase 5 control, not a manual review — CI scans the staging tree for private values and any finding is a defect; nothing is published before its phase's exit criteria are evidenced (the programme's publishability rule exists precisely to prevent premature release); and the threat model explicitly includes the published-artifact adversary — §11's catalogue treats "the attacker has read our papers" as a standing assumption, because after publication, they have.

15.2 Minimum Viable Fix vs Full PhD Fix The roadmap's comparison table is reproduced here as the normative statement, followed by a paragraph per gap on what the minimum-viable fix actually delivers and what is lost by stopping there. Gap

Gap

Consistency

State machine. The minimum-viable fix — a documented transition table plus property-based tests over the transitions — delivers the practical safety the estate needs: the guards are written down, the common violations are tested, and the approval-binding paths get adversarial coverage. What is lost is the proof: without model checking under a stated failure model, the estate cannot claim that illegal transitions are unreachable, only that none have been found. The doctoral claim's "formal state constraints" clause cannot survive on the minimum-viable fix; it would have to be restated as "tested state constraints," which is an engineering claim, not a research one. Invariants. CI tests for the major invariants deliver continuous protection: every change to the estate is checked against the doctrine's core promises, and regressions are caught mechanically. What is lost is the adversarial depth — the property-based generators and the chaos suite that probe the invariants under fault conditions rather than under normal operation. The estate would know its invariants hold when nothing is going wrong; it would not know they hold when things go wrong, which is when invariants matter. The thesis loses its citable empirical validation layer and keeps only its regression suite. Consistency. Making the server authoritative for approvals while leaving status eventually consistent delivers the safety property that matters most: no device can approve on its own authority, and the single-writer rule holds. What is lost is the determinism guarantee across the full conflict space — the formal contract, the revision protocol's edge cases, the fault-injection evidence that conflicts resolve the same way under clock skew and network loss. The estate would be safe in practice and unproven in theory; the thesis could claim the architecture but not the contract. Idempotency. Idempotency keys for consequential actions deliver the at-most-once guarantee for the cases the estate can foresee: duplicate taps return the existing decision, retries carry the same key, the key binds the intent. What is lost is the ambiguous-outcome handling — the saga model and the verification tests for the case where the outcome is genuinely unknown. That case is rare and is precisely the case where consequential systems fail badly; the minimum-viable fix covers the common duplicates and leaves the rare catastrophe to manual handling. The thesis loses its strongest safety evidence and keeps its most common. Security. A threat model and control matrix deliver the essential honesty: the assets are named, the boundaries are drawn, every consequential action has a threat and a control, and the procedural-isolation limitation is stated rather than hidden. What is lost is the depth — the attack trees that show how threats compose, the capability formalism that makes authorisation checkable, and the evaluated isolation upgrade path. The estate would know what it fears and what it does about it; it would not have the formal structure that lets a committee verify the reasoning. The security chapter of the thesis would read as competent practice rather than as a modelled contribution. Router. Shadow logs with heuristic review deliver the operational value: the router's decisions are recorded, outcomes are labelled, and a human can see whether the routing is sensible and improve it by judgement. What is lost is the empirical justification — the controlled comparison against baselines on measured outcomes that turns "the router seems to help" into "the router improves accepted work while reducing repair and attention cost." The doctoral claim's entire second half — increase accepted work, reduce repair, reduce attention cost — rests on this comparison; without it, the router is a well-instrumented heuristic, and the claim must drop its quantitative clauses.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

15.3 What Gets Cut Under Time Pressure If the programme must be cut, it should be cut deliberately — named cuts, with the consequences stated in advance, approved by the operator as consequential decisions. Three cut scenarios are defined here. Each states what is removed, what the thesis loses, and whether the doctoral claim survives. Cut scenario 1: collapse to the 12-week engineering fix. What is removed: the model-checked proof (replaced by property tests), the chaos depth, the formal consistency contract, the saga model, the attack trees and capability formalism, the controlled live router trials, and the doctoral evidence package — essentially everything that makes the programme doctoral rather than engineering. What the thesis loses: it ceases to be a thesis. The twelve weeks produce a hardened, well-tested, honestly documented estate — a genuine engineering achievement — but no defensible research contribution. Does the doctoral claim survive? No. The claim's clauses on formal state constraints, measured router comparison, and quantitative improvement all require the work that was cut. The correct output of this scenario is not a weaker thesis but an engineering report and a deferred doctorate. Cut scenario 2: cut the live router trials. What is removed: Month 10's controlled live trials — the randomised comparison of routing policies on live low-risk tasks. What remains: the shadow logging with outcome labels, the offline replay against alternate policies, the pre-registered protocol, and the full measurement apparatus. What the thesis loses: causal evidence. Replay can show that alternate policies would have decided differently on historical tasks, but it cannot show what would have happened — the outcomes under the alternate policy are simulated, not observed. The quantitative clauses of the claim survive in weakened form: "the governed policy outperforms baselines on replayed historical decisions" rather than "on live tasks." Does the doctoral claim survive? Partially — restated with the weaker evidence, and with the limitation carried openly. This is the most survivable cut, because the replay evidence is real evidence, honestly bounded. Cut scenario 3: cut isolation beyond Level 1. What is removed: the evaluation and any implementation of isolation Levels 2–5 — no process/container isolation, no capability tokens, no cryptographic evidence, no institutional-grade controls. What remains: Level 1's filesystem and access discipline, the full threat model, the control matrix, the attack trees, and the evaluated upgrade path as future work. What the thesis loses: very little, because the thesis never claimed more than procedural isolation honestly modelled. The security chapter argues that defensibility without overclaiming is itself a contribution, and that argument is intact at Level 1 — arguably stronger, because the estate demonstrates the discipline of stopping where the cost-benefit analysis says to stop. Does the doctoral claim survive? Yes, unchanged. The claim says nothing about isolation levels; it says the boundaries are formally modelled, and they are.

15.4 Choosing The guidance is short, because the analysis above has done the work: run the full twelve-month track unless a named constraint forces otherwise. A named constraint is a hard external deadline, a sustained loss of operator availability, or a Month 3 finding that the formal methods are intractable for this estate's complexity — not tiredness, not impatience, not the temptation to declare the engineering sufficient. The minimum-viable fixes are genuine achievements and the correct fallback when the full track is impossible; they are not a shortcut to the same thesis. If a cut becomes necessary, cut in the order the scenarios above imply: isolation beyond Level 1 first (the claim survives intact), the live router trials second (the claim survives weakened, with the replay evidence carrying the weight), and the collapse to the engineering fix last (the claim does not survive, and the doctorate is deferred rather than diluted). Every cut is a consequential decision, approved by the operator, recorded with its reasons — because a programme that cuts its own standards silently has already failed the standard the thesis will be judged by.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

16. Definition of Done and Appendices Part 1 — Definition of Done The roadmap states fourteen definition-of-done statements, to which this paper adds a fifteenth of its own. A statement is not a criterion until an independent reviewer can check it, so each statement below is operationalised as two to four concrete checks. The reviewer needs no access to the estate's internals beyond the specification repository, the test suites, and the evidence package: if a statement cannot be checked with those, it is not done. 1. The state machine has a formal specification. - Open spec/state-machine.md in the specification repository and confirm it defines all twelve states, the transition skeleton, and the guard table with one owner per concept. Confirm the formal model in proofs/ parses in the chosen language and covers every transition in the skeleton — no transition exists in prose that has no counterpart in the model. - Confirm the repository is versioned and that the specification the model was checked against is the version the estate currently implements (drift check). 2. Illegal transitions are shown to be unreachable under the defined failure model. - Run the model checker against the archived model and confirm it reports no reachable illegal state. - Inspect the failure model document and confirm each of the thirteen failure conditions is defined explicitly enough to be criticised. - Select at least two guards, weaken them, re-run the checker, and confirm a counterexample is produced — the proof must be capable of failing. 3. Core invariants are tested automatically. - Run the full invariant suite and confirm all 27 invariants (Appendix A) have at least one test in an applicable layer. - Confirm the suite runs without manual steps in the estate's continuous check, and inspect the traceability matrix for any invariant with a doctrine statement but no test. - Introduce a deliberate invariant violation in a test fixture and confirm the suite fails. 4. Mutated or expired approvals cannot execute. - Run the mutated-approval test suite: submit an approval whose intent digest no longer matches the intent, attempt execution in the harness, and confirm rejection is logged. - Attempt execution with an expired intent in the chaos harness and confirm the attempt is refused and the refusal is recorded as approval_expired, not silently dropped. - Confirm no test path exists in which a mutated or expired approval reaches execution_started. 5. Multi-device state conflicts resolve deterministically. - Run the multi-device conflict tests and confirm each row of the conflict-resolution table produces the specified resolution. - Replay the two-devices-one-approval case twice from the same initial conditions and confirm the same device's decision is recorded and the other is told it lost, identically both times. - Confirm no test leaves two inconsistent consequential outcomes standing. 6. Offline taps cannot become approvals. - Simulate a device offline at the moment of the approval tap, then reconnect, and confirm the tap produced a pending gesture only — no approval_granted event exists for it. - Confirm the server-side validation (intent exists, unexpired, digest matches, session authorised) ran after reconnect and independently of the client's cached card. - Confirm the client rendered the authoritative result after reconnect rather than its own cached assumption. 7. Duplicate taps cannot create duplicate consequences. - Execute the duplicate-tap test: two identical approval taps against the same intent, and confirm the second returns the existing decision and state with no second execution. - Confirm the idempotency key for the action is identical across both taps and that the execution record shows a single execution. - Confirm the attempt count incremented while the effect count did not. 8. Ambiguous execution becomes visible unknown or failed, not false completion. - Run the ambiguous-outcome test: a consequential execution whose result cannot be observed, and confirm the work item renders as execution_unknown or failed — never completed. - Confirm no automatic retry occurred for the consequential action (the safe-to-retry flag was not set). - Confirm the escalation record exists: the ambiguity was surfaced with the next safe option, not buried.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

9. Security boundaries and limitations are explicitly modeled. - Open the threat model and confirm all ten trust boundaries are drawn, the asset register is complete, and every consequential action has a named threat and control. Confirm the procedural-isolation limitation is stated in the model's own words, with compensating controls named — not implied, not footnoted. - Confirm the isolation upgrade roadmap (Levels 0–5) exists with evaluation criteria, and that the estate's current level is stated plainly. 10. Prompt injection, stale state, and approval fatigue are addressed. - Confirm each of the three appears in the threat catalogue with a named control and at least one test — a mention without a control does not count. - Run the prompt-injection egress test: consequential action attempted from untrusted external content, and confirm it is refused without explicit approval. - Run the stale-state test: a consequential decision attempted against expired source freshness, and confirm it renders unknown or re-reads rather than substituting. - Inspect the approval-rate metrics and confirm the fatigue controls (exact target/effect, risk classes, no bulk approval) are implemented, not merely specified. 11. The usage router is evaluated against baselines. - Confirm the router experiment report compares the governed policy against at least the local, cache-only, and unconstrained baselines on the stated objective function. - Confirm the decision-log schema carries outcome labels and that every evaluated decision links to a labelled outcome. - Confirm the router guardrails were enforced during evaluation: no evaluated run shows the router approving consequence or overriding an approval boundary. 12. The evaluation measures accepted work, repair, cost, latency, loops, and attention cost. - Confirm each of the six metrics is defined in the experiment protocol and present in the results — a missing metric fails the statement. - Confirm attention cost was measured with the characterised method from Month 9, with its noise and limitations stated alongside the numbers. - Confirm the results report uncertainty (credible intervals or equivalent), not point claims. 13. The limitations remain honest. - Read the limitations section and confirm it names, specifically: the failuremodel boundaries, the single-operator measurement thinness, the procedural isolation, and the bounded router evaluation. - Confirm no limitation is phrased as a vague gesture ("further work is needed") without the specific boundary it concedes. - Confirm the failure case studies exist and that at least one describes a gap that let a failure through, not only invariants that caught one. 14. The evidence supports the thesis. - For each clause of the doctoral claim, follow the traceability matrix to its supporting and refuting evidence and confirm both exist. - Confirm the adversarial integration review was conducted and its report records which claims were kept, cut, or downgraded, with reasons. - Confirm the operator (HgX) reviewed the package and approved the claim as stated — or that the claim was restated until he did. 15. The programme's artifacts are publishable by construction. - Open the specification repository, the formal model, the invariant suite, the consistency contract, the idempotency schema, the threat model, the router's labelled decision history, and the evidence package, and confirm each contains no private value — no family detail, credential, financial record, or live-source content. - Confirm the open-source staging tree (~/workspace/jarvis-estate/) builds from these artifacts without a separate sanitising effort — publishability was constructed in, not cleaned up after. - Confirm the trilogy's vision statement appears in the programme's own account of itself: the evidence package describes a system built to be open, with the publication boundary stated and tested.

Part 2 — Final Recommendation The roadmap's recommended sequence is restated here as a closing argument, because the sequence is the argument: Freeze the doctrine → Formalise the state machine → Turn doctrine into invariants → Define consistency and idempotency → Formalise security boundaries → Measure router performance → Integrate into doctoral evidence. The order is not administrative; it is logical. Nothing can be proven about a system whose contracts are not frozen, so the freeze comes first — one vocabulary, one state machine, one owner per concept, before any model is written. The state machine is formalised next because every later guarantee (approval binding, evidence discipline, consistency,

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

idempotency) is a property of transitions, and properties of transitions need a checked machine to hang on. Doctrine becomes invariants third because the invariants are the machine's properties made executable — the bridge between the proof and the running estate. Consistency and idempotency come fourth because they are the properties of the machine under the real world's faults: multiple devices, duplicate taps, lost networks, ambiguous outcomes. Security is formalised fifth because a threat model written before the system is specified describes a fiction; written after, it describes the thing the committee will actually attack. The router is measured sixth because measurement needs the governed system to exist first — you cannot evaluate a policy against a machine that is still changing shape. And evidence is integrated last because integration is arrangement, not discovery: the thesis is the argument the previous six phases earned. The fastest way to fix the gaps is not to add more features. That sentence, from the roadmap, deserves to be the programme's motto, because every pressure the twelve months will exert — to add a dashboard instead of characterising the noise, to add an isolation level instead of writing the cost-benefit analysis, to add formalism instead of running the trial — is a pressure to build rather than to prove. The programme resists it the same way the estate resists it: no proof, no done. A month is not complete when its artifacts exist but when its exit criteria are evidenced; a claim is not made when it is written but when its refuting evidence has been collected and came out in its favour. What this sequence produces, if it is followed honestly, is not just a thesis but a different estate: one whose doctrine is executable, whose approvals are binding objects rather than gestures, whose evidence is structural rather than decorative, and whose operator's attention is a budgeted quantity rather than an assumed infinite resource. The doctoral claim — a formally specified, empirically validated, human-governed agent control plane — is then not a description of what was built but a statement of what was shown. That is the correct sequence, and it is also the correct order of operations for everything the estate will build afterwards: freeze, formalise, verify, and only then claim.

Appendix B — Canonical Event Vocabulary The fifteen normative events. Each event is appended to the work item's history when its transition occurs; the vocabulary is frozen in Phase 0 and owned per the specification repository. Event

Definition

brief_received

The operator or an authorised routine submits a bounded unit of work.

brief_accepted

The orchestrator accepts the scope of the submitted work.

plan_created

A bounded plan for the work exists and is recorded.

artifact_produced

An inspectable output of the work exists.

evidence_packet_created

The structured proof bundle for the work exists.

approval_requested

The work has reached a consequence boundary; an approval intent is issued.

approval_granted

The exact bound intent was approved by the operator.

approval_denied

The bound intent was denied by the operator.

approval_expired

The bound intent passed its expiry without decision.

execution_started

The approved consequential action began executing.

execution_observed

The result of the execution was observed.

acceptance_passed

The bounded acceptance test passed against observed output.

failure_observed

A failure condition was observed during the work.

operator_paused

The operator paused the service or the work item.

observation_unavailable

Appendix D — References and Further Reading The estate's own sources first, then the general literature a committee would expect. General works are cited by name and idea only; no publication details are asserted beyond what is stated here. • Paper I — the J.A.R.V.I.S. operating white paper. The estate's operating doctrine: the Role → Live Source → Tool → Artifact → Evidence → Approval → Routine chain, the approval disciplines, and the silent-by-default routine posture. The philosophical source of the programme's claim. • Paper II — the J.A.R.V.I.S. backend technical white paper. The backend lifecycle (Brief → Route → Plan → Execute → Verify → Approve → Handoff), the runtime and skill architecture, and the scheduling and memory contracts. The engineering source of the state machine and invariant work. • Paper III — the J.A.R.V.I.S. front-end white paper. The operator loop (Brief → Work → Evidence → Approval → Decision) across the five client surfaces, the attention disciplines, and the rendering contracts. The source of the consistency model and the attention-cost invariant. • The Grok Bot field guide — The One-Person Company: how one operator turns one request into a small working team (independent 12-page field guide, 2026). Cited honestly as the doctrine's source text: the role, livesource, tool, artifact, evidence, approval, and routine pattern that Papers I–III formalise. The programme claims the formalisation, not the pattern. • The roadmap document — ~/workspace/jarvis-formal-spec/ROADMAP.md (2026-10-08). The normative source for this paper: phases, invariants, exit criteria, tables, and the fourteen definition-of-done statements. Where this paper and the roadmap disagree, the roadmap governs. • TLA+ — Leslie Lamport's temporal specification language and its model checker: the recommended primary tool for the Phase 1 state-machine proof, valued for its treatment of concurrency, failure, and liveness properties in systems work.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

• Alloy — the lightweight formal modelling language: the recommended alternative for Phase 1, valued for its lower learning curve and its analyser's ability to find counterexamples in bounded scopes. • Calm technology — Weiser & Brown, The Coming Age of Calm Technology: the intellectual ancestry of the attention-cost invariant — the proposition that technology should respect the periphery of human attention, here operationalised as a measurable, budgeted system property rather than a design preference. • STRIDE threat modelling — the spoofing, tampering, repudiation, information disclosure, denial of service, and elevation of privilege taxonomy: the structural basis of the Phase 5 threat catalogue, extended with the agentspecific threats (prompt injection, stale-state manipulation, approval fatigue, subagent deception). • Property-based testing — the discipline of asserting properties over randomly generated inputs and event sequences: the methodological basis of the Phase 2 Layer 2 suite, where the invariants are checked against generated approval mutations, expiry timings, duplicates, and network faults rather than hand-written cases. • Human-AI interaction governance — the literature on keeping a human meaningfully in command of autonomous systems: the field within which the approval-binding mechanism and the attention-cost measurement claim their contribution, and against which the thesis positions its "contracts around the model" proposition.

From Doctrine to Proof —Paper IV of the J.A.R.V.I.S. Series

The evidence ledger

Every claim the estate makes is graded by time. Proven — done, with the evidence attached. Verified — true now, timestamped at the last check. Tracked — not yet true, watched until it is proven. No claim ships without a grade.

AUDITS

Public audits, on the record

The estate audits itself in public, on a fixed cadence. Every audit runs the same template the same way, so the record stays comparable — week over week, quarter over quarter.

Weekly — Mondays, ~09:00 ET Monthly — the 1st of each month Quarterly — Jan · Apr · Jul · Oct, the 1st

The audit template

What every audit checks, in order.

  • The three surfaces — website, Dropbox archive, release repository: current, consistent with each other, and versioned.
  • Identity grep — the principal’s real name searched across every surface. The only acceptable count is zero.
  • Evidence cross-check — every Proven and Verified claim in the ledger re-tested against its evidence.
  • Tracked items — each open item re-stated with its current status. Nothing is silently dropped.
  • Score recompute — the stability score recalculated under rubric v1.0 and compared with the previous audit.

Weekly audit 2026-W43 — the baseline

Monday 19 October 2026 · the first audit on record; every later audit is measured against it.

  • Three surfaces checked — website built and verified; Dropbox archive complete (13 folders, 50 entries, byte-verified); repository committed locally.
  • Identity grep — 0 hits. @hgxengineering only, everywhere.
  • Evidence cross-check — all Proven and Verified claims tested; the ledger above is the result.
  • Tracked items — 2 open: public website URL, GitHub push. (The Dropbox folder link moved to Proven when the public link went live.)

SCORES

Stability scoring

One number, computed the same way every time, so the estate’s maturity is a curve anyone can read — not a mood. The rubric’s weights change only on the principal’s word.

85 / 100
BASELINE · WEEK 2026-W43 · 19 OCTOBER 2026

The first score on record. Every component is at full marks except publish health, which stands at zero until the site is public — the missing 15 points are exactly the unpublished website, stated in the open rather than rounded away.

score = 100 × ( 0.25·audit pass rate + 0.25·evidence completeness + 0.20·identity clean + 0.15·publish health + 0.15·correction stability ) — rubric v1.0

The concept registry

The estate’s load-bearing concepts — one name, one owner, one definition each.

  • Principal — HgX (@hgxengineering): the single human authority. Every consequential action waits on his word.
  • Publishing house — the release office: sole owner of the three public surfaces — website, Dropbox, repository.
  • Evidence — the ledger discipline: every claim is Proven, Verified, or Tracked. No claim without a grade.
  • Approval boundary — the last safe moment: preparation proceeds freely; external, binding, or destructive actions stop for explicit approval.
  • Identity — @hgxengineering only. The principal’s real name appears in no public material, ever.
  • Versioning law — versions only move forward, nothing is overwritten, and every change is committed with its reason.
  • Stability — the scored property above: measured by rubric v1.0 at every audit. The trend matters more than any single number.
FUTURE

Next — the road ahead

Paper IV sets the route: seven phases after the freeze, each producing artifacts that are publishable by construction. Formal validation is the precondition of publication — proof is what makes an open control plane safe to run.

PHASE 1 · PROVEN

Model-check the state machine

TLC model-checking of the TLA+ work-state machine under a defined failure model — illegal transitions shown unreachable.

PHASE 2

Verify the invariants

Doctrinal statements converted to executable invariants, verified through contract, property-based, and chaos testing.

PHASE 3

Define the consistency contract

The multi-device consistency model specified, so approval decisions carry a determinism guarantee on every surface.

PHASE 4

Specify idempotency

At-most-once semantics for consequential actions — duplicate taps and retries made formally safe.

PHASE 5

Formalise the security boundary

The threat model, capability matrix, and trust-boundary map — the boundary stated as proof, not procedure.

PHASE 6

Evaluate the router

The usage router measured against baselines on accepted work, repair cost, and attention cost.

PHASE 7

Doctoral evidence package

All of it integrated into the doctoral evidence — the end-state claim made defensible.

RELEASE

The open-source release

Doctrine, skills, contracts, and the reference implementation, published for anyone to inspect, test, and build on.

Open source is the main vision

J.A.R.V.I.S. is built in the open from the first day, because a system that demands to be trusted must be inspectable. The boundary is absolute: the system goes public — under MIT for code and CC-BY-4.0 for the papers — and the principal’s private life never does.
